Two properties of the formula deserve special emphasis. First, consistent with the binomial model analysis, the **option value depends only on the volatility <span>&#92;(&#92;\sigma&#92;)</span>, not on the average rate of return** <span>&#92;(&#92;\alpha&#92;)</span>. The drift of the stock under the real-world measure is entirely irrelevant to the option price. Second, unlike the European option, **there is no closed-form analytic formula for American option pricing** in the Black-Scholes framework. American options must be priced numerically — for instance using the binomial lattice backward induction algorithm with the early-exercise modification — or by solving a partial differential equation with a free boundary.

### Put-Call Parity and European Puts

The Black-Scholes formula for a **European put** can be derived either by direct computation or, more elegantly, from **put-call parity**. Put-call parity states that for European options on the same underlying with the same strike and expiry:

<span>&#92;[ C(S, t) - P(S, t) = S - K e^{-r(T-t)} &#92;]</span>

Rearranging and using the identity <span>&#92;(&#92;\mathcal{N}&#95;{cdf}(-x) = 1 - &#92;\mathcal{N}&#95;{cdf}(x)&#92;)</span> yields the European put price:

<span>&#92;[ P(S, t) = K e^{-r(T-t)} &#92;\mathcal{N}&#95;{cdf}(-d&#95;2) - S &#92;\mathcal{N}&#95;{cdf}(-d&#95;1) &#92;]</span>

The Black-Scholes formula represents the culmination of the binomial lattice approach: it is the analytic closed-form that the numerical lattice converges to as the number of time steps grows without bound, and it provides a benchmark against which computational methods can be validated.

# Lecture 7: Stochastic Calculus and the Black-Scholes Model

## Standard Brownian Motion

### From Discrete Random Walk to Continuous Process

The mathematical foundation of modern option pricing theory rests on the concept of **standard Brownian motion**, also called the **Wiener process**. To understand where this object comes from, it is helpful to begin with a discrete model and examine what happens as the time step shrinks to zero.

Consider a **special binomial lattice**, a discrete random walk starting at <span>&#92;( X&#95;0 = 0 &#92;)</span>. At each step, the increment <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta X&#95;n &#92;)</span> is a symmetric coin flip scaled by the square root of the time step:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Delta X&#95;n = &#92;begin{cases} &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &amp; &#92;text{with probability } p = 0.5 \\ -&#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &amp; &#92;text{with probability } 0.5 &#92;end{cases} &#92;]</span>

The choice to scale by <span>&#92;( &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;)</span> rather than <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> is deliberate and essential: it ensures that the variance of the cumulative sum grows linearly with time, which is the signature property of Brownian motion. Now we take the continuous-time limit. As <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>, the discrete time step becomes the differential <span>&#92;( dt &#92;)</span>, and the increment <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta X&#95;t &#92;)</span> becomes the infinitesimal <span>&#92;( dX&#95;t &#92;)</span>. The limit of this random walk is a **continuous process**, which is called **standard Brownian motion** and is conventionally denoted <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span>.

### Formal Properties

A standard Brownian motion <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> is defined by three fundamental properties.

First, **initialization**: <span>&#92;( Z(0) = 0 &#92;)</span>. The process starts at the origin.

Second, **normally distributed increments**: for all <span>&#92;( t &#92;geq 0 &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t > 0 &#92;)</span>, the increment <span>&#92;( Z(t + &#92;Delta t) - Z(t) &#92;)</span> is normally distributed with mean zero and variance <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ Z(t + &#92;Delta t) - Z(t) &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0, &#92;Delta t) &#92;]</span>

This says that over any time interval of length <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>, the change in the Brownian motion is a zero-mean Gaussian random variable whose variance equals the length of the interval.

Third, **independent increments**: for any <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq t_1 < t_2 &#92;leq t_3 < t_4 &#92;)</span>, the increments <span>&#92;( Z(t&#95;2) - Z(t&#95;1) &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( Z(t&#95;4) - Z(t&#95;3) &#92;)</span> are independent random variables. Non-overlapping increments carry no information about one another.

Two important consequences follow from these definitions. Taking <span>&#92;( t&#95;1 = 0 &#92;)</span> in the second property, one sees that <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0, t) &#92;)</span>: the process <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> at any time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span> has a normal distribution with mean zero and variance <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>. The independent-increments property is also known as the **Markovian property**: the future evolution of the process depends only on the current state, not on the full history. This is consistent with the **efficient market hypothesis**, which asserts that all past price information is already reflected in the current price.

### More Properties: The Infinitesimal Increment

It is instructive to examine the structure of an infinitesimal increment of Brownian motion more carefully. Since <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta Z&#95;t = Z(t + &#92;Delta t) - Z(t) &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0, &#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span>, we can write this increment as

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Delta Z&#95;t = &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;quad &#92;text{or equivalently} &#92;quad dZ&#95;t = &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sqrt{dt} &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span> is an independent standard normal random variable at each time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>. Equivalently, <span>&#92;( dZ&#95;t / dt = &#92;phi&#95;t / &#92;sqrt{dt} &#92;)</span>, which diverges as <span>&#92;( dt &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>. This captures a fundamental regularity result: the standard Brownian motion <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t = Z&#95;0 + &#92;int&#95;0^t dZ&#95;s &#92;)</span> is **continuous but not differentiable**. Its sample paths are nowhere smooth in the classical sense.

The moments of the standard normal <span>&#92;( &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;)</span> are:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t) = 0, &#92;quad &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t^2) = 1, &#92;quad &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t^3) = 0, &#92;quad &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t^4) = 3 &#92;]</span>

From these one computes the mean and variance of the squared increment <span>&#92;( (&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^2 &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{E}((&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^2) = &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;Delta t \cdot &#92;phi&#95;t^2) = &#92;Delta t \cdot &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t^2) = &#92;Delta t &#92;]</span>

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{var}((&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^2) = &#92;mathbf{E}((&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^4) - (&#92;mathbf{E}((&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^2))^2 = (&#92;Delta t)^2 &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi&#95;t^4) - (&#92;Delta t)^2 = O((&#92;Delta t)^2) &#92;]</span>

A crucial observation: the variance of <span>&#92;( (&#92;Delta Z&#95;t)^2 &#92;)</span> goes to zero *quadratically* in <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>. This means that as <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>, the squared increment <span>&#92;( (dZ&#95;t)^2 &#92;)</span> becomes deterministic, concentrating on its expected value. We can therefore write the **fundamental identity of stochastic calculus**:

<span>&#92;[ (dZ&#95;t)^2 = dt &#92;]</span>

This non-classical rule, where a stochastic differential squared equals a deterministic differential, is at the heart of *Itô's Lemma*.

### Summary of Important Properties of <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span>

To summarize the key operational facts: if <span>&#92;( &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span>, then

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Delta Z&#95;t = &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t}, &#92;quad dZ&#95;t = &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sqrt{dt}, &#92;quad (dZ&#95;t)^2 = dt &#92;]</span>

These identities will be used repeatedly in deriving *Itô's Lemma* and solving the Black-Scholes SDE.

---

## Itô's Process and the Stochastic Integral

### Itô's Process

A general **Itô's process** <span>&#92;( X&#95;t &#92;)</span> is a solution to the *stochastic differential equation* (SDE):

<span>&#92;[ dX&#95;t = a \cdot dt + b \cdot dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( a &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b &#92;)</span> can be constants or functions of <span>&#92;( (X&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span>. The term <span>&#92;( a \cdot dt &#92;)</span> is the **deterministic trend** component, governing the expected direction of movement, while <span>&#92;( b \cdot dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> is the **random fluctuation** component, encoding the uncertainty driven by the standard Brownian motion.

### The Stochastic (Itô) Integral

Before turning to *Itô's Lemma*, we need to define precisely what <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;0^T b(t) \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> means. In classical deterministic calculus, the integral <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;0^T f(t&#95;n) \, dt &#92;)</span> is defined as the limit of Riemann sums, and the choice of evaluation point within each sub-interval (left endpoint, right endpoint, midpoint) does not matter in the limit because the integrand is smooth. Formally:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;0^T f(t&#95;n) \, dt = &#92;lim&#95;{N &#92;to +&#92;infty} &#92;sum&#95;{n=0}^{N-1} f(t&#95;n) &#92;Delta t = &#92;lim&#95;{N &#92;to +&#92;infty} &#92;sum&#95;{n=0}^{N-1} f(t&#95;{n+1}) &#92;Delta t &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( t&#95;n = n &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t = T/N &#92;)</span>. For stochastic integrals, the situation is fundamentally different because the integrand <span>&#92;( b(t) &#92;)</span> and the integrator <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> are both random and correlated, so the choice of evaluation point matters crucially in the limit.

The **Itô integral** is defined by evaluating the integrand at the *left* endpoint of each sub-interval:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;0^T b(t) \, dZ&#95;t = &#92;lim&#95;{N &#92;to +&#92;infty} &#92;left( &#92;sum&#95;{n=0}^{N-1} b(t&#95;n)(Z(t&#95;{n+1}) - Z(t&#95;n)) &#92;right) &#92;]</span>

where the limit is taken in the **mean square sense**, meaning:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;lim&#95;{N &#92;to +&#92;infty} &#92;mathbf{E}&#92;left( &#92;left( &#92;sum&#95;{k=0}^{N-1} b(t&#95;{n+1})(Z(t&#95;{n+1}) - Z(t&#95;n)) - &#92;int&#95;0^T b(t) \, dZ&#95;t &#92;right)^2 &#92;right) = 0 &#92;]</span>

Two notes are important here. First, the partial sum <span>&#92;( &#92;sum&#95;{n=0}^{N-1} b(t&#95;n)(Z(t&#95;{n+1}) - Z(t&#95;n)) &#92;)</span> is a random variable, and so is the limiting Itô integral <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;0^T b(t) \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span>. Second, the left-endpoint convention is financially meaningful: it says that the trading position <span>&#92;( b(t&#95;n) &#92;)</span> is determined using only information available *up to* time <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span>, before seeing the Brownian increment <span>&#92;( Z(t&#95;{n+1}) - Z(t&#95;n) &#92;)</span>. Using the right-endpoint (which would yield a different answer) would correspond to using future information, which is not admissible in finance.

### Solution for Constant Coefficients

As an analogy, recall that the bond price satisfies the deterministic ODE <span>&#92;( d&#92;beta(&#92;tau) = r &#92;beta \, d&#92;tau &#92;)</span>, or equivalently <span>&#92;( d&#92;log(&#92;beta(&#92;tau)) = r \, d&#92;tau &#92;)</span>. Integrating gives <span>&#92;( &#92;beta(t) = &#92;beta(0) e^{rt} &#92;)</span>.

For the SDE <span>&#92;( dX&#95;t = a \, dt + b \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> with constants <span>&#92;( a, b &#92;)</span>, integrating directly gives:

<span>&#92;[ X(t) = X(0) + &#92;int&#95;0^t (a \, d&#92;tau + b \, dZ(&#92;tau)) = X(0) + a \cdot t + b \cdot Z&#95;t &#92;]</span>

The solution is simply the initial value plus a linear trend plus a Brownian motion term scaled by <span>&#92;( b &#92;)</span>. This is the simplest Itô process, sometimes called *arithmetic Brownian motion*.

---

## Geometric Brownian Motion and the Black-Scholes Model

### The GBM SDE

The **Black-Scholes model** for the stock price <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> is the *Geometric Brownian Motion* (GBM) model, a stochastic differential equation of the form:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = &#92;mu \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

or equivalently,

<span>&#92;[ dS&#95;t = &#92;mu S&#95;t \, dt + &#92;sigma S&#95;t \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

Here <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> is the **drift** (expected instantaneous rate of return) and <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is the **volatility** (standard deviation of returns per unit time), both treated as constants. This is an Itô process with <span>&#92;( a(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;mu S&#95;t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;sigma S&#95;t &#92;)</span>. The left-hand side <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t / S&#95;t &#92;)</span> is the instantaneous relative return, so the model says the return consists of a deterministic drift <span>&#92;( &#92;mu \, dt &#92;)</span> and a random fluctuation <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span>. The central question is: how do we solve this SDE for <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span>?

We cannot solve it the same way as the constant-coefficient case, because the coefficients depend on <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> itself. The correct tool is *Itô's Lemma*.

---

## Itô's Lemma

### Statement

**Itô's Lemma** is the stochastic analogue of the chain rule. It tells us how a smooth function of an Itô process evolves over time. Suppose that <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> satisfies the general SDE:

<span>&#92;[ dS&#95;t = a(S&#95;t, t) \, dt + b(S&#95;t, t) \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

Let <span>&#92;( G(S, t) : &#92;mathbb{R}^2 &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R} &#92;)</span> be a smooth (twice continuously differentiable) function. Then <span>&#92;( Y&#95;t = G(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span> is itself an Itô process, and it satisfies:

<span>&#92;[ dY&#95;t = &#92;left[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} + a(S&#95;t, t) &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} + &#92;frac{1}{2} b(S&#95;t, t)^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} &#92;right] dt + b(S&#95;t, t) &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

For the Black-Scholes model specifically, <span>&#92;( a(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;mu S&#95;t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;sigma S&#95;t &#92;)</span>, so the formula specializes to the well-known form involving the option Greeks: a theta term, a delta term, and a gamma term.

### Proof (Derivation)

The key step in understanding *Itô's Lemma* is to perform a Taylor expansion of <span>&#92;( G &#92;)</span> and keep track of which terms survive in the limit <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>, using the special stochastic rules.

For notational simplicity, write <span>&#92;( a &#92;equiv a(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b &#92;equiv b(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span>. By Taylor expansion:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Delta Y&#95;t = G(S&#95;t + &#92;Delta S&#95;t, t + &#92;Delta t) - G(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} &#92;Delta S&#95;t + &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} &#92;Delta t + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} (&#92;Delta S&#95;t)^2 + &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S &#92;partial t} (&#92;Delta S&#95;t)(&#92;Delta t) + O((&#92;Delta t)^2) &#92;]</span>

Now, since <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t = a \, dt + b \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( dZ&#95;t = O(&#92;sqrt{dt}) &#92;)</span>, the cross term satisfies <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta S&#95;t &#92;Delta t = O((&#92;Delta t)^{3/2}) &#92;)</span>, which is negligible. After discarding this cross term, we have:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Delta Y&#95;t &#92;approx &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} &#92;Delta S&#95;t + &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} &#92;Delta t + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} (&#92;Delta S&#95;t)^2 + O((&#92;Delta t)^{3/2}) &#92;]</span>

The crucial computation is <span>&#92;( (dS&#95;t)^2 &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ (dS&#95;t)^2 = (a \, dt + b \, dZ&#95;t)^2 = a^2 (dt)^2 + b^2 (dZ&#95;t)^2 + 2ab \, dt \, dZ&#95;t = b^2 \, dt &#92;]</span>

where we used the fundamental identity <span>&#92;( (dZ&#95;t)^2 = dt &#92;)</span>, together with <span>&#92;( (dt)^2 = 0 &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( dt \, dZ&#95;t = 0 &#92;)</span> (both vanish faster than <span>&#92;( dt &#92;)</span> as <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>). Taking the limit <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ dY&#95;t = &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} dt + &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} dS&#95;t + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} b^2 \, dt &#92;]</span>

Substituting <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t = a \, dt + b \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> and collecting terms:

<span>&#92;[ dY&#95;t = &#92;left[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} + a(S&#95;t, t) &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} + &#92;frac{1}{2} b(S&#95;t, t)^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} &#92;right] dt + b(S&#95;t, t) &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

This is *Itô's Lemma*. The extra term <span>&#92;( &#92;frac{1}{2} b^2 &#92;partial^2 G / &#92;partial S^2 &#92;)</span> — the **Itô correction term** — has no analogue in ordinary calculus. It arises precisely because Brownian motion has non-zero quadratic variation, captured by <span>&#92;( (dZ&#95;t)^2 = dt &#92;)</span>. In classical calculus, all second-order terms vanish; in stochastic calculus, the second-order Brownian term survives.

---

## Explicit Solution of the Black-Scholes SDE

### Applying Itô's Lemma to <span>&#92;( &#92;log S&#95;t &#92;)</span>

We now apply *Itô's Lemma* to solve the GBM SDE. Consider the function <span>&#92;( G(S, t) = &#92;log(S) &#92;)</span>. Its partial derivatives are:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial t} = 0, &#92;quad &#92;frac{&#92;partial G}{&#92;partial S} = &#92;frac{1}{S}, &#92;quad &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 G}{&#92;partial S^2} = -&#92;frac{1}{S^2} &#92;]</span>

For the Black-Scholes model, recall <span>&#92;( a(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;mu S&#95;t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b(S&#95;t, t) = &#92;sigma S&#95;t &#92;)</span>. Substituting into *Itô's Lemma*:

<span>&#92;[ d &#92;log(S&#95;t) = &#92;left[ 0 + &#92;mu S&#95;t \cdot &#92;frac{1}{S&#95;t} + &#92;frac{1}{2} (&#92;sigma S&#95;t)^2 \cdot &#92;left(-&#92;frac{1}{S&#95;t^2}&#92;right) &#92;right] dt + &#92;sigma S&#95;t \cdot &#92;frac{1}{S&#95;t} \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

<span>&#92;[ d &#92;log(S&#95;t) = &#92;left( &#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma^2 &#92;right) dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

This is a remarkable simplification: the SDE for <span>&#92;( &#92;log(S&#95;t) &#92;)</span> has *constant* coefficients, so we can integrate it directly. Integrating from <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;)</span> to <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;log(S&#95;t) - &#92;log(S&#95;0) = &#92;int&#95;0^t &#92;left(&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2&#92;right) ds + &#92;int&#95;0^t &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;s = &#92;left(&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2&#92;right) t + &#92;sigma(Z&#95;t - Z&#95;0) &#92;]</span>

Exponentiating both sides yields the **explicit solution of the Black-Scholes model**:

<span>&#92;[ S&#95;t = S&#95;0 \, e^{(&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)t + &#92;sigma Z&#95;t} &#92;]</span>

### Interpretation and the Lognormal Distribution

This explicit formula has several important consequences. First, assuming <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 > 0 &#92;)</span>, the stock price <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> is always strictly positive, since it is the exponential of a real number. This is one of the key advantages of GBM over arithmetic Brownian motion, which can take negative values and is therefore unsuitable as a model for stock prices.

Second, <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> has a **lognormal distribution**. Since <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0, t) &#92;)</span>, the exponent <span>&#92;( (&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)t + &#92;sigma Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> is normally distributed with mean <span>&#92;( (&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)t &#92;)</span> and variance <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma^2 t &#92;)</span>. Therefore <span>&#92;( &#92;log(S&#95;t/S&#95;0) &#92;)</span> is Gaussian, making <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> lognormally distributed.

Third, notice the **Itô correction**: the drift in the log-return is <span>&#92;( &#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, not <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span>. The term <span>&#92;( -&#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span> is purely a consequence of the nonlinearity of the exponential and the Itô correction from *Itô's Lemma*. It implies that the median of <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> grows at rate <span>&#92;( &#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, while the mean <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}[S&#95;t] = S&#95;0 e^{&#92;mu t} &#92;)</span> grows at rate <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span>. The difference between the mean growth rate and the median growth rate is exactly the volatility penalty <span>&#92;( &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, a reflection of Jensen's inequality applied to the convex exponential function.

---

# Lecture 8: Monte Carlo Methods for Option Pricing

## Risk-Neutral Valuation

### From the Binomial Lattice to the Continuous Model

**Monte Carlo (MC) simulation** is one of the most powerful and general methods for pricing financial derivatives. To understand why it works, we begin by recalling the risk-neutral pricing framework established in the binomial lattice setting, and then carry it over to the continuous Black-Scholes world.

In the binomial lattice, the no-arbitrage condition implies the existence of a **risk-neutral probability** <span>&#92;( q^&#42; &#92;)</span> such that:

<span>&#92;[ S(t&#95;n) = e^{-r &#92;Delta t} &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(S(t&#95;{n+1})) &#92;]</span>

<span>&#92;[ V(S(t&#95;n), t&#95;n) = e^{-r &#92;Delta t} &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(V(S(t&#95;{n+1}), t&#95;{n+1})) &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(\cdot) &#92;)</span> denotes the expectation using <span>&#92;( q^&#42; &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( V(S, t) &#92;)</span> is the European option value function. From equation (1), we can write:

<span>&#92;[ E^Q&#92;left(&#92;frac{S(t&#95;{n+1})}{S(t&#95;n)}&#92;right) = e^{r &#92;Delta t} = 1 + r &#92;Delta t + O((&#92;Delta t)^2) &#92;]</span>

Rearranging:

<span>&#92;[ E^Q&#92;left(&#92;frac{S(t&#95;{n+1}) - S(t&#95;n)}{S(t&#95;n)}&#92;right) = r &#92;Delta t + O((&#92;Delta t)^2) &#92;]</span>

Taking <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>, this implies that the expected rate of return under the risk-neutral measure is <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>, the risk-free rate. In fact, under the **risk-neutral probability measure** <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span>, the stock price dynamics change from the real-world drift <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> to the risk-free rate <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = r \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t^Q &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t^Q &#92;)</span> is a standard Brownian motion under <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span>. The actual (real-world) stock price dynamics are:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = &#92;mu \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

It is essential to note that the superscript <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span> emphasizes that paths simulated under the risk-neutral measure are not the same as paths of the actual underlying price process. The two measures use different drifts, but the same volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>.

### The Continuous Risk-Neutral Pricing Formula

Under the continuous model, the **risk-neutral pricing formula** for a European option with payoff at expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> is:

<span>&#92;[ V(S, t) = e^{-r(T-t)} &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(&#92;text{payoff}(S&#95;T)) &#92;]</span>

where the expectation <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(\cdot) &#92;)</span> assumes the underlying asset price follows the risk-neutral model <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t / S&#95;t = r \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t^Q &#92;)</span>. This formula is the cornerstone of modern derivatives pricing: the option value equals the expected discounted payoff under the risk-neutral measure. The real-world drift <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> does not appear in the pricing formula — only the risk-free rate <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span> and the volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> matter.

---

## Monte Carlo Pricing of European Options

### The Basic MC Algorithm

The risk-neutral pricing formula suggests a direct numerical method. Using risk-neutral pricing at time zero:

<span>&#92;[ V(S&#95;0, 0) = e^{-rT} &#92;mathbf{E}^Q&#92;left( &#92;text{payoff}(S&#95;T) &#92;right) &#92;]</span>

we can approximate this expectation by generating <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> independent samples <span>&#92;( (S&#95;T)^j, \, j = 1, &#92;ldots, M &#92;)</span> of <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> under the risk-neutral model, and averaging the discounted payoffs:

<span>&#92;[ V(S&#95;0, 0) &#92;approx &#92;frac{1}{M} &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^{M} &#92;left( e^{-rT} &#92;text{payoff}((S&#95;T)^j) &#92;right) &#92;]</span>

Under the Black-Scholes model, this is especially convenient because we have the explicit solution under the risk-neutral measure:

<span>&#92;[ S&#95;t = S&#95;0 e^{(r - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)t + &#92;sigma Z&#95;t} &#92;]</span>

To generate <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> independent samples of <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span>, we exploit the fact that <span>&#92;( Z&#95;T &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0, T) &#92;)</span>, so <span>&#92;( Z&#95;T &#92;)</span> can be written as <span>&#92;( &#92;sqrt{T} &#92;)</span> times a standard normal. In Matlab notation:

<span>&#92;[ S&#95;T = S&#95;0 e^{(r - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)T + &#92;sigma &#92;cdot &#92;sqrt{T} &#92;cdot &#92;text{randn}(M,1)} &#92;]</span>

Here <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> is an <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>-by-1 vector of independent terminal prices. The i.i.d. samples <span>&#92;( \{(S&#95;T)^j, \, j=1,&#92;ldots,M\} &#92;)</span> can be generated directly from standard normals using the explicit formula, with no need to time-step through the path. This is a major advantage of having a closed-form solution: there is **no time discretization error** — the only source of error is the sampling error from using a finite <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>.

---

## Monte Carlo Pricing of Path-Dependent Options

### What Are Path-Dependent Options?

A **path-dependent option** is one whose payoff depends not just on the terminal stock price <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span>, but on the entire price path <span>&#92;( S(t), \, 0 &#92;leq t &#92;leq T &#92;)</span>. Because the payoff depends on the path, we cannot simply generate <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> directly — we must simulate the entire path at discrete time points.

A **barrier option** is a canonical example. It comes into existence or ceases to exist when the stock price crosses a specified barrier level. For instance, an **up-and-out (Up-Out)** barrier option: if <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> ever crosses an upper barrier <span>&#92;( S&#95;u &#92;)</span>, the option pays nothing; otherwise it pays the standard vanilla payoff. The existence of the option therefore depends on whether the price path has crossed the barrier.

To price options whose payoffs depend on the path, we simulate price paths <span>&#92;( S(t&#95;n), \, n = 0, 1, &#92;ldots, N &#92;)</span> by stepping forward through time. Using the exact BS formula for each time step (since the log-return over <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> is exactly Gaussian under constant <span>&#92;( &#92;mu, &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>):

<span>&#92;[ S(t&#95;{n+1}) = S(t&#95;n) &#92;cdot e^{(r - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)&#92;Delta t + &#92;sigma &#92;cdot &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;cdot &#92;text{randn}(M,1)} &#92;]</span>

This step-by-step simulation produces <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> complete price paths simultaneously, one for each of the <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> simulated scenarios.

### Asian Options: A Worked Example

A classic example is the **European Asian option**, whose payoff is a function of the time-average of the stock price:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{1}{T} &#92;int&#95;0^T S&#95;t \, dt &#92;]</span>

For example, an **Asian call** has payoff:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;max&#92;left( &#92;frac{1}{T} &#92;int&#95;0^T S&#95;t \, dt - K, \, 0 &#92;right) &#92;]</span>

Asian options are popular because averaging dampens volatility and makes them harder to manipulate at expiry. For computational implementation, we discretize the integral. Let <span>&#92;( t&#95;n = t&#95;{n-1} + &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t = T/N &#92;)</span>. Define the running average:

<span>&#92;[ A&#95;n = &#92;frac{1}{n} &#92;sum&#95;{k=1}^{n} S(t&#95;k) &#92;approx &#92;frac{1}{t&#95;n} &#92;int&#95;0^{t&#95;n} S(&#92;tau) \, d&#92;tau &#92;]</span>

A useful recursion avoids storing the full sum at each step:

<span>&#92;[ A&#95;n = &#92;frac{1}{n}&#92;left((n-1) A&#95;{n-1} + S(t&#95;n)&#92;right) = &#92;frac{n-1}{n} A&#95;{n-1} + &#92;frac{1}{n} S(t&#95;n) &#92;]</span>

The full Monte Carlo algorithm in pseudocode is:

```
S_0 = S(0) * ones(M,1)
A_0 = 0
for n = 0, 1, ..., N-1
    A_{n+1} = n/(n+1) * A_n + 1/(n+1) * S_n
    S_{n+1} = S_n * exp((r - 0.5*sigma^2)*Delta_t + sigma*sqrt(Delta_t)*randn(M,1))
end
payoff = max(A_N - K, 0)
V_0 = exp(-rT) * mean(payoff)
```

Here all quantities are <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>-dimensional vectors, so each step simultaneously advances all <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> sample paths. The final option value is the discounted average of payoffs across all simulated paths.

---

## Sampling Error in Monte Carlo Pricing

### Why the Error Is <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span>

When we use the explicit Black-Scholes formula to generate samples of <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> (with no time discretization), the only error in the MC estimate comes from the use of a finite number of samples <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>. This is the **sampling error**. With <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> simulations, the sampling error is <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span>. Here is the precise argument.

Let <span>&#92;( Y^j = e^{-rT} &#92;text{payoff}((S&#95;T)^j) &#92;)</span> be the discounted payoff of the <span>&#92;( j &#92;)</span>-th simulation, where each <span>&#92;( Y^j &#92;)</span> is drawn independently from the same distribution as a random variable <span>&#92;( Y &#92;)</span>. The Monte Carlo estimator is:

<span>&#92;[ V^M = &#92;frac{Y^1 + &#92;cdots + Y^M}{M} &#92;]</span>

This is an **unbiased estimator** of <span>&#92;( V&#95;0 = &#92;mathbf{E}(Y) &#92;)</span>, since:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{E}(V^M) = &#92;frac{1}{M} &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^{M} &#92;mathbf{E}(Y^j) = &#92;mathbf{E}(Y) &#92;]</span>

The variance of the estimator is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{var}(V^M) = &#92;mathbf{E}&#92;left(&#92;left( &#92;frac{Y^1 + &#92;cdots + Y^M}{M} - &#92;mathbf{E}(V^M) &#92;right)^2 &#92;right) = &#92;frac{1}{M^2} &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^{M} &#92;mathbf{E}&#92;left((Y^j - &#92;mathbf{E}(Y))^2&#92;right) = &#92;frac{1}{M} &#92;mathbf{var}(Y) &#92;]</span>

using independence of the <span>&#92;( Y^j &#92;)</span> and the fact that they are identically distributed. Therefore, the **standard error** of the MC estimator is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{std}(V^M) = &#92;frac{1}{&#92;sqrt{M}} &#92;text{std}(Y) &#92;]</span>

By the **Central Limit Theorem**, as <span>&#92;( M &#92;to +&#92;infty &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ V^M - &#92;mathbf{E}(Y) &#92;Longrightarrow &#92;mathcal{N}&#92;left(0, &#92;frac{1}{&#92;sqrt{M}} &#92;sigma&#95;Y &#92;right) &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;Y = &#92;text{std}(Y) &#92;)</span>. Two important notes: the sampling error does *not* depend on the number of risky assets if the payoff function depends on multiple assets — this is why MC is particularly well-suited to **high-dimensional problems** (e.g., basket options on many underlyings). Additionally, <span>&#92;( V^M &#92;)</span> is itself a random quantity, since each <span>&#92;( Y^j &#92;)</span> is a random sample.

---

## Confidence Intervals for MC Estimates

### Constructing the Interval

In practice, the true standard deviation <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;Y &#92;)</span> is unknown. We estimate it from the sample using the **sample standard deviation**:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;hat{&#92;sigma} = &#92;left[ &#92;frac{&#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^{M} (Y^j - V^M)^2}{M-1} &#92;right]^{1/2} &#92;]</span>

Approximating <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;Y &#92;)</span> by <span>&#92;( &#92;hat{&#92;sigma} &#92;)</span>, the Central Limit Theorem gives:

<span>&#92;[ V^M - V&#95;0 &#92;approx &#92;mathcal{N}&#92;left(0, &#92;frac{&#92;hat{&#92;sigma}}{&#92;sqrt{M}}&#92;right) &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( V&#95;0 = &#92;mathbf{E}(Y) &#92;)</span> is the true option price.

### The Normal Quantiles

For the standard normal distribution, given any confidence level <span>&#92;( &#92;beta &#92;)</span>, the interval <span>&#92;( [-x^&#42;, x^&#42;] &#92;)</span> satisfying

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;{-x^&#42;}^{x^&#42;} e^{-x^2/2} dx = &#92;beta &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( p(x) = e^{-x^2/2} &#92;)</span> is the standard normal density, can be determined from standard tables. Key facts:

- If <span>&#92;( &#92;beta = 0.95 &#92;)</span>, then <span>&#92;( x^&#42; = 1.96 &#92;)</span>.
- If <span>&#92;( &#92;beta = 0.99 &#92;)</span>, then <span>&#92;( x^&#42; = 2.58 &#92;)</span>.

Therefore, with **99% confidence**, we can assert:

<span>&#92;[ V&#95;0 &#92;in &#92;left[ V^M - &#92;frac{2.58 &#92;hat{&#92;sigma}}{&#92;sqrt{M}}, \; V^M + &#92;frac{2.58 &#92;hat{&#92;sigma}}{&#92;sqrt{M}} &#92;right] &#92;]</span>

This confidence interval quantifies the uncertainty in the MC estimate. To halve the width of the interval (i.e., to halve the standard error), one must quadruple the number of simulations <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>, since the error scales as <span>&#92;( 1/&#92;sqrt{M} &#92;)</span>. This slow convergence rate is an intrinsic feature of Monte Carlo methods, but the method compensates by being dimension-independent and extremely flexible in handling complex payoffs.

---

# Lecture 9: Monte Carlo Simulation — Euler Method and Dynamic Trading

## Option Pricing Under the Generalized Black-Scholes Model

### Review: Constant-Parameter BS Model

We begin by recalling the setting from Lecture 8. Under the standard Black-Scholes model with constant <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>, the stock price follows:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = &#92;mu \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

and by *Itô's Lemma* we have the **explicit solution**:

<span>&#92;[ S&#95;t = S&#95;0 e^{(&#92;mu - &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2)t + &#92;sigma Z&#95;t} &#92;]</span>

Under no-arbitrage, the fair value of a European option satisfies:

<span>&#92;[ V(S, t) = e^{-r(T-t)} &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(&#92;text{payoff}(S&#95;T)) &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}^Q(\cdot) &#92;)</span> is under the risk-neutral dynamics <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t/S&#95;t = r \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span>. Since the explicit solution is available, sampling is easy: in Matlab, `randn` yields standard normal samples and thus <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> can be generated in one vectorized operation. A crucial benefit is that there is **no time discretization error** when the explicit formula is used — the only error is sampling error of order <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span>. We can also generate sample paths <span>&#92;( \{S(t&#95;n), \, n=0,1,&#92;ldots,N\} &#92;)</span> to price European path-dependent exotic options.

### The Generalized BS Model: Local Volatility

In practice, the assumption of a constant volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is often too restrictive. The **Local Volatility Function (LVF) model**, also called the **generalized Black-Scholes model**, replaces the constant <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> with a function <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span> that depends on both the current price and time:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = r \, dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t) \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

This is a much richer model — it can be calibrated to fit the observed market implied volatility surface, and it allows the volatility to be higher for lower stock prices (the so-called "volatility smile" or "skew" effect). However, the price we pay is that there is no explicit closed-form solution for <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span> in general. We must discretize and simulate numerically. We can still price any European option (plain vanilla or exotic) using MC under the generalized BS model, though there will now be an additional **time discretization error** when numerical stepping is used.

---

## The Euler Method for SDEs

### Derivation of the Euler Time Step

The standard numerical method for simulating an SDE is the **Euler method**, the stochastic analogue of Euler's method for ODEs. For the LVF model, divide the time interval <span>&#92;( [0, T] &#92;)</span> into <span>&#92;( N &#92;)</span> equal sub-intervals, with <span>&#92;( t&#95;n = n &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( n = 0, 1, &#92;ldots, N &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t = T/N &#92;)</span>.

Integrating the SDE over <span>&#92;( [t&#95;n, t&#95;{n+1}] &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} dS&#95;t = &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} r S&#95;t \, dt + &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t) S&#95;t \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

The Euler approximation freezes the integrands at the left endpoint <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span>:

- The deterministic integral: <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} r S&#95;t \, dt &#92;approx r S&#95;n &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>
- The stochastic integral: <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t) S&#95;t \, dZ&#95;t &#92;approx &#92;sigma(S&#95;n, t&#95;n) S&#95;n &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n) &#92;)</span>

Using <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n) = &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;phi&#95;n &#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;( &#92;phi&#95;n &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span> is independent at each step, we obtain the **Euler discretization**:

<span>&#92;[ S&#95;{n+1} = S&#95;n + S&#95;n &#92;left( r &#92;Delta t + &#92;sigma(S&#95;n, t&#95;n) &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;phi&#95;n &#92;right), &#92;quad &#92;phi&#95;n &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;]</span>

In the constant-<span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> case, this reduces to the familiar path-stepping formula used for Asian option pricing. In the LVF case, the local volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S&#95;n, t&#95;n) &#92;)</span> is evaluated at the current simulated price and time — this is what makes the Euler method straightforward to implement even for complex volatility surfaces.

---

## Error Analysis of the Euler Method

### Weak Convergence

The Euler method introduces a **time discretization error** in addition to the unavoidable sampling error. The accuracy of the Euler method is characterized in terms of **weak convergence**: for a time stepsize <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>, the error in expected values satisfies:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;left| &#92;mathbf{E}(S(T)) - &#92;mathbf{E}(S&#95;T^{&#92;Delta t}) &#92;right| &#92;leq C &#92;Delta t &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( S(T) &#92;)</span> is the exact solution to the SDE and <span>&#92;( S&#95;T^{&#92;Delta t} &#92;)</span> is the Euler approximation with step size <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( C &#92;)</span> is a constant that does not depend on <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>. Weak convergence is first-order in <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>: halving the time step halves the discretization error. Further details can be found in Section 4.8.2 of the course notes.

### Balancing Time Stepping Error and Sampling Error

When using the Euler method for option pricing, the total error has two components. The time discretization error is <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span>, and the sampling error (from using <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> simulations) is <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{total error} = O(&#92;Delta t) + O&#92;left(&#92;frac{1}{&#92;sqrt{M}}&#92;right) &#92;]</span>

If we want the total error to be <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span> — meaning the sampling error should not dominate — we need <span>&#92;( 1/&#92;sqrt{M} = O(&#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span>, which implies we should choose:

<span>&#92;[ M &#92;approx &#92;frac{&#92;text{constant}}{(&#92;Delta t)^2} &#92;]</span>

### Computational Complexity

With this choice of <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span>, the total computational complexity of the MC method is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{complexity} = O(&#92;text{&#x23;time steps} &#92;times &#92;text{&#x23;samples}) = O&#92;left(&#92;frac{T}{&#92;Delta t} &#92;cdot M&#92;right) = O&#92;left(&#92;frac{M}{&#92;Delta t}&#92;right) &#92;]</span>

Substituting <span>&#92;( M = O(1/(&#92;Delta t)^2) &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{complexity} = O&#92;left(&#92;frac{1}{(&#92;Delta t)^3}&#92;right) &#92;]</span>

Inverting, <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t = O(1 / &#92;text{complexity}^{1/3}) &#92;)</span>. Since the Euler error is <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span>, we conclude:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{error} = O(&#92;Delta t) = O&#92;left(&#92;frac{1}{&#92;text{complexity}^{1/3}}&#92;right) &#92;]</span>

This means that to reduce the error by a factor of 10, we must increase the computational effort by a factor of <span>&#92;( 10^3 = 1000 &#92;)</span>. The convergence rate of <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;text{complexity}^{1/3}) &#92;)</span> is relatively slow, which motivates research into variance reduction techniques and higher-order discretization methods such as the Milstein scheme.

---

## How Efficient Is MC Option Pricing?

To put the efficiency of the MC method in perspective, we should ask: how much computational work is needed to achieve a prescribed accuracy? We have seen that the computational complexity scales as <span>&#92;( O(M / &#92;Delta t) &#92;)</span>, and the error decays as <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;text{complexity}^{1/3}) &#92;)</span>. This is the fundamental efficiency tradeoff for the Euler-based MC method applied to the generalized BS model. In contrast, for the standard constant-parameter BS model, the explicit formula eliminates the time discretization error entirely, leaving only the sampling error at rate <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span> — a significantly better convergence rate in terms of the number of samples alone.

---

## Dynamic Trading Hedging Analysis via MC Simulation

### Motivation: Beyond Pricing

Monte Carlo simulation is not limited to computing option prices. It is equally powerful for **analyzing the performance of complex dynamic trading strategies**, which is crucial for risk management at financial institutions. In this section we consider the application to **dynamic hedging** analysis.

In continuous-time theory, a perfectly hedged portfolio replicates the option payoff exactly. In practice, however, hedging is a **dynamic trading process** and portfolio rebalancing can only be implemented at discrete times — not continuously. The question of how well a discrete-time hedging strategy approximates the ideal continuous hedge, and how much residual risk remains, is an important practical problem that MC simulation is ideally suited to address.

### Setup: Model-Based Hedging Analysis

We conduct a **model-based hedging analysis** based on the assumption that the stochastic model is correct. The scenario we consider is as follows: we are **selling an option** and simultaneously **dynamically trading the underlying asset**, with a cash account providing financing. This corresponds to a **self-financed dynamic trading strategy**:

<span>&#92;[ \{-V&#95;t, \, &#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, \, B&#95;t\} &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( -V&#95;t &#92;)</span> denotes the short position in the option (value received from selling), <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t &#92;)</span> is the value of the stock position (holding <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t &#92;)</span> shares), and <span>&#92;( B&#95;t &#92;)</span> is the bond account balance. The bond account ensures that the portfolio is always **self-financed** — cash flows from buying and selling shares are exactly offset by the bond account, so no external capital is ever injected or withdrawn.

### Goals of the Analysis

The central questions of hedging analysis are:

1. **How good is the hedging/trading strategy?** Does the portfolio track the option value closely?
2. **How risky is it?** How much risk (residual exposure) remains after hedging?
3. **How do we measure the risk?** What metrics best capture the distribution of hedging profit and loss (P&L)?

In addition to historical back-testing (which uses actual past price data), we can perform **hedging analysis based on a stochastic model** by generating many simulated price paths under the assumed model and running the hedging strategy on each path. By examining the distribution of the terminal hedging P&L across many simulated paths, we can characterize the risk and effectiveness of the strategy under the assumed model dynamics. This model-based approach is a fundamental component of risk management practice at banks and other financial institutions.

### Connecting to MC Simulation

The MC framework makes this analysis tractable. For each of the <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> simulated price paths, we:

1. Simulate the stock price path <span>&#92;( S(t&#95;n), \, n = 0, 1, &#92;ldots, N &#92;)</span> using the Euler method (or the exact formula under constant BS).
2. At each time step <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span>, compute the hedge ratio <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;{t&#95;n} &#92;)</span> (e.g., the Black-Scholes delta) and rebalance the portfolio.
3. Track the bond account <span>&#92;( B&#95;t &#92;)</span> to maintain self-financing.
4. At expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>, compute the terminal P&L = value of hedging portfolio minus option payoff.

Repeating across all <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> paths yields a sample distribution of the hedging P&L. Under perfect continuous hedging, the P&L would be zero for every path. The spread of the simulated P&L distribution across paths quantifies the **hedging error** arising from discrete rebalancing — this is the residual risk that the hedger must bear in practice. Tighter rebalancing schedules (smaller <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>) reduce this risk but increase transaction costs, creating a fundamental tradeoff that can also be studied via MC simulation.

# Lecture 10: Dynamic Hedging — Delta, Gamma, and Vega

## Dynamic Hedging (Trading) Analysis

The Black-Scholes framework assumes that the underlying asset price follows a geometric Brownian motion. Under this model the stock price dynamics are governed by the stochastic differential equation

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS}{S} = &#92;mu \, dt + &#92;sigma \, dZ&#95;t &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> is the drift, <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is the volatility, and <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> is a standard Brownian motion. The central question of dynamic hedging is: given that a financial institution has written an option and therefore holds a *short* option position <span>&#92;( -V(S,t) &#92;)</span>, how can it manage the resulting risk through continuous trading in the underlying asset?

The analysis begins by discretising time. We assume that trading is only permitted at times <span>&#92;( t&#95;n = n&#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>, where <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t = T/N &#92;)</span>, so that the trading dates form a uniform partition

<span>&#92;[ 0 = t&#95;0 &lt; t&#95;1 &lt; &#92;cdots &lt; t&#95;N = T. &#92;]</span>

Between consecutive trading dates <span>&#92;( (t&#95;n, t&#95;{n+1}] &#92;)</span> the hedger holds a fixed number <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;n &#92;)</span> of shares of the underlying asset. The hedging ratio <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;n &#92;)</span> is chosen at time <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span> based on information available at that moment, and is then kept constant until the next rebalancing date.

## Delta Hedging and the Binomial Lattice Approximation

When a binomial lattice is employed to price the option, the **delta** at node <span>&#92;( (n, j) &#92;)</span> — that is, at time <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span> and underlying price <span>&#92;( S^n&#95;j &#92;)</span> — is computed as the ratio of the change in option value to the change in stock price across the two branches emanating from that node:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;delta^n&#95;j = &#92;frac{V^{n+1}&#95;{j+1} - V^{n+1}&#95;j}{(u - d)S^n&#95;j} &#92;approx &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}(S^n&#95;j, t&#95;n). &#92;]</span>

Here <span>&#92;( u &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( d &#92;)</span> are the up- and down-factors of the binomial tree, and <span>&#92;( V^{n+1}&#95;{j+1} &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( V^{n+1}&#95;j &#92;)</span> are the option values at the two successor nodes. This finite-difference ratio is precisely the first-order approximation to the partial derivative <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V / &#92;partial S &#92;)</span>. More generally, regardless of the pricing method used, the number of units of the underlying held at time <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span> is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;delta&#95;n = &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}(S&#95;n, t&#95;n). &#92;]</span>

An important practical note concerns the simulation of underlying price paths for the purpose of evaluating hedging performance. When generating paths to assess the profit and loss (P&L) of the dynamic trading strategy, one uses the *real-world* drift <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span>, not the risk-neutral drift <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>. This is a critical distinction: the risk-neutral measure is used purely for pricing, whereas analysis of actual hedging performance must reflect the real probability distribution of stock price moves.

## Portfolio Construction and Rebalancing

The dynamic hedging portfolio is constructed to offset the short option position. **Initially**, at <span>&#92;( n = 0 &#92;)</span>, the institution is short the option (value <span>&#92;( -V&#95;0 = -V(S&#95;0, 0) &#92;)</span>), holds <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;0 &#92;)</span> units of the stock, and places the residual in a cash account so that the initial portfolio value is zero. The cash balance at inception is therefore

<span>&#92;[ B&#95;0 = V&#95;0 - &#92;delta&#95;0 S&#95;0. &#92;]</span>

The total portfolio value at any trading date <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span> is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Pi&#95;n = -V(S&#95;n, t&#95;n) + &#92;delta&#95;n S&#95;n + B&#95;n. &#92;]</span>

The portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi = \{-V,\, &#92;delta &#92;cdot S,\, B\} &#92;)</span> consists of the short option, the stock holding, and the cash account.

Over the interval <span>&#92;( [t&#95;n, t&#95;{n+1}) &#92;)</span> the cash account grows at the risk-free rate. At time <span>&#92;( t&#95;{n+1} &#92;)</span>, just before rebalancing, the portfolio value is

<span>&#92;[ -V(S&#95;{n+1}, t&#95;{n+1}) + &#92;delta&#95;n S&#95;{n+1} + B&#95;n e^{r&#92;Delta t}. &#92;]</span>

Rebalancing at <span>&#92;( t&#95;{n+1} &#92;)</span> means adjusting the stock position from <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;n &#92;)</span> to <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;{n+1} &#92;)</span> without changing the portfolio value (the rebalancing condition <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;{t^-&#95;{n+1}} = &#92;Pi&#95;{t^+&#95;{n+1}} &#92;)</span>). This forces the new cash balance to satisfy

<span>&#92;[ B&#95;{n+1} = B&#95;n e^{r&#92;Delta t} + (&#92;delta&#95;n - &#92;delta&#95;{n+1})S&#95;{n+1}. &#92;]</span>

The interpretation is immediate: if <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;{n+1} &gt; &#92;delta&#95;n &#92;)</span>, additional shares must be purchased and the cost is debited from the cash account; if <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;{n+1} &lt; &#92;delta&#95;n &#92;)</span>, shares are sold and the proceeds are credited to the cash account. At expiry <span>&#92;( t&#95;N = T &#92;)</span>, the portfolio formed at <span>&#92;( t&#95;{N-1} &#92;)</span> is liquidated, yielding the terminal portfolio value

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Pi&#95;N = -V(S&#95;N, t&#95;N) + &#92;delta&#95;{N-1} S&#95;N + B&#95;{N-1} e^{r&#92;Delta t}. &#92;]</span>

## Profit and Loss Analysis

The terminal value <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;N &#92;)</span> is a random variable because it depends on the particular path taken by the stock price. Three qualitative outcomes are possible: if <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;N &#92;equiv 0 &#92;)</span> along every path, the hedge is *perfect*; if <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;N &gt; 0 &#92;)</span>, the hedger realises a profit on that scenario; and if <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;N &lt; 0 &#92;)</span>, the hedger incurs a loss. In practice, a continuous-time delta hedge under the Black-Scholes model would be perfect, but because trading occurs only at discrete times, residual errors accumulate.

The quantity <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;N &#92;)</span> is therefore called the **hedging error**. To compare performance across options of different values, it is natural to normalise. The *discounted relative P&L* is defined as

<span>&#92;[ P&#38;L = &#92;frac{e^{-rT}&#92;Pi&#95;N}{V(S&#95;0, 0)}. &#92;]</span>

One examines the full distribution of this P&L across Monte Carlo scenarios: its mean, standard deviation, and tail quantiles. A fundamental question is whether the P&L is zero along every individual scenario path. In general it is not, even under the Black-Scholes model, because the discrete-time rebalancing introduces a discretisation error proportional to <span>&#92;( &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;)</span>.

## Computing Delta When Pricing by Monte Carlo

When option prices are computed by Monte Carlo rather than by an analytic formula or a lattice, obtaining the delta requires some care. The naive finite-difference approximation is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}(S&#95;0, 0) &#92;approx &#92;frac{V(S&#95;0 + &#92;Delta S, 0) - V(S&#95;0, 0)}{&#92;Delta S}. &#92;]</span>

Each option value is estimated by discounted expected payoff:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}(S&#95;0, 0) &#92;approx e^{-r&#92;Delta T} &#92;frac{&#92;text{mean}(&#92;text{payoff}(S(T)|&#95;{S&#95;0+&#92;Delta S})) - &#92;text{mean}(&#92;text{payoff}(S(T)|&#95;{S&#95;0}))}{&#92;Delta S}. &#92;]</span>

The statistical error in each Monte Carlo mean is of order <span>&#92;( O(1/&#92;sqrt{M}) &#92;)</span>, where <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> is the number of sample paths. The error in the finite-difference approximation to <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V/&#92;partial S &#92;)</span> is therefore of order <span>&#92;( O(1/(&#92;sqrt{M}&#92;,&#92;Delta S)) &#92;)</span>. This creates a fundamental tension: making <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta S &#92;)</span> small improves the finite-difference approximation but amplifies the statistical noise, requiring a very large number of paths. Indeed, for small <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta S &#92;)</span>, it can be difficult even to obtain the correct *sign* of the delta from naive independent simulations.

The standard remedy is to use the **same** set of random samples <span>&#92;( &#92;phi &#92;)</span> for both simulations, so that the sampling errors partially cancel in the difference. Under the Black-Scholes model the procedure is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;phi = &#92;text{randn}(M,1), &#92;quad S&#95;T = S&#95;0 e^{(r-0.5&#92;sigma^2)T + &#92;sigma&#92;sqrt{T}&#92;phi}, &#92;quad &#92;tilde{S}&#95;T = (S&#95;0+&#92;Delta S)e^{(r-0.5&#92;sigma^2)T+&#92;sigma&#92;sqrt{T}&#92;phi}. &#92;]</span>

The two option prices are then estimated as

<span>&#92;[ V&#95;0 = e^{-rT}&#92;text{mean}(&#92;text{payoff}(S&#95;T)), &#92;quad &#92;tilde{V}&#95;0 = e^{-rT}&#92;text{mean}(&#92;text{payoff}(&#92;tilde{S}&#95;T)), &#92;]</span>

and the delta is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;delta&#95;0 = &#92;frac{&#92;tilde{V}&#95;0 - V&#95;0}{&#92;Delta S}. &#92;]</span>

Because the same random draws <span>&#92;( &#92;phi &#92;)</span> drive both stock price processes, the two payoff averages are highly correlated, and their difference has a much smaller variance than the difference of two independent estimates. This *common random numbers* technique is essential for stable delta estimation via Monte Carlo.

## Delta-Neutral Hedging

**Delta-neutral hedging** is the strategy of choosing <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t = &#92;partial V/&#92;partial S(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span> so that the hedged portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi = \{-V,\, &#92;delta &#92;cdot S,\, B\} &#92;)</span> satisfies

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial &#92;Pi}{&#92;partial S} = -&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}(S,t) + &#92;delta &#92;equiv 0. &#92;]</span>

By matching the option's delta, the portfolio is rendered insensitive to *first-order* movements in the underlying. In other words, delta hedging eliminates the linear exposure of the option value to changes in <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>. However, because the option value is a nonlinear function of <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>, first-order cancellation is insufficient when stock price moves are large. The residual sensitivity to curvature is captured by the **gamma**, defined as the second partial derivative <span>&#92;( &#92;partial^2 V / &#92;partial S^2 &#92;)</span>.

## Delta and Gamma Neutral Hedging

To hedge both the first- and second-order sensitivities simultaneously, one requires the portfolio to satisfy

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial &#92;Pi}{&#92;partial S} = 0 &#92;quad &#92;text{and} &#92;quad &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 &#92;Pi}{&#92;partial S^2} = 0 &#92;]</span>

at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>. The underlying stock itself has zero gamma (it is linear in <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>), so adding more stock cannot neutralise gamma. One must introduce an additional instrument <span>&#92;( I &#92;)</span> — for example, a liquidly traded vanilla option on <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> — that has nonzero gamma <span>&#92;( &#92;partial^2 I/&#92;partial S^2 &#92;neq 0 &#92;)</span>. The extended portfolio is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;Pi = \{-V,\, &#92;delta&#95;S &#92;cdot S,\, &#92;delta&#95;I &#92;cdot I,\, B\}. &#92;]</span>

The delta-neutrality and gamma-neutrality conditions become the two-equation system

<span>&#92;[ -&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} + &#92;delta&#95;S + &#92;delta&#95;I &#92;frac{&#92;partial I}{&#92;partial S} = 0, &#92;]</span>

<span>&#92;[ -&#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + &#92;delta&#95;I &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 I}{&#92;partial S^2} = 0. &#92;]</span>

(Note that stock holdings <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;S &#92;)</span> do not appear in the second equation because the stock has zero gamma.) From the gamma equation one reads off the required holding in the additional instrument:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;delta&#95;I = &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V/&#92;partial S^2}{&#92;partial^2 I/&#92;partial S^2}. &#92;]</span>

With <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;I &#92;)</span> determined, one substitutes back into the delta equation to find <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;S &#92;)</span>. This two-instrument approach neutralises both the linear and quadratic responses of the portfolio to stock price movements, providing a much tighter hedge than delta alone, particularly when the stock makes large moves over a rebalancing interval.

## Vega Hedging

Under the Black-Scholes model, the only parameter that is not directly observable in the market is the volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>. All other inputs — the current stock price, the risk-free interest rate, the strike, and the time to maturity — are either directly quoted or can be read from standard financial data. The volatility must be estimated or implied from market prices. Because <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is uncertain, the option value is sensitive to errors in its specification, and this sensitivity is measured by the **vega**, defined as

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{vega} = &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial &#92;sigma}. &#92;]</span>

A hedger who wishes to insulate the portfolio against unexpected changes in volatility must construct a *vega-neutral* portfolio. Because the underlying stock has zero vega (its price does not depend on <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> directly), neutralising vega again requires a second option. The construction parallels the gamma-neutrality argument: one adds a position in a liquidly traded option <span>&#92;( I &#92;)</span> such that the portfolio's total vega is zero, then adjusts the stock holding to restore delta neutrality. In this way one can simultaneously achieve delta and vega neutrality, or even delta, gamma, and vega neutrality, by introducing two additional option instruments.

Under the Black-Scholes model it can be shown that for both European calls <span>&#92;( C &#92;)</span> and puts <span>&#92;( P &#92;)</span> the vega is the same and equals

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{vega} = &#92;frac{&#92;partial C}{&#92;partial &#92;sigma} = &#92;frac{&#92;partial P}{&#92;partial &#92;sigma} = &#92;frac{&#92;mathcal{N}'(d&#95;1)}{&#92;sigma S&#95;0 &#92;sqrt{T}}, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;mathcal{N}'(d) = &#92;frac{1}{&#92;sqrt{2&#92;pi}} e^{-&#92;frac{1}{2}d^2} &#92;)</span> is the standard normal density evaluated at <span>&#92;( d&#95;1 &#92;)</span>. Since <span>&#92;( &#92;mathcal{N}'(d&#95;1) &gt; 0 &#92;)</span> for all finite <span>&#92;( d&#95;1 &#92;)</span>, vega is strictly positive for both calls and puts: option values are always increasing in volatility. This strict monotonicity — the option price as a function of <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is strictly increasing on <span>&#92;( (0, +&#92;infty) &#92;)</span> — is a key property that underpins the uniqueness of implied volatility.

## Risk, P&L Distributions, and the Role of Volatility

In a Black-Scholes model, <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> represents the risk of the underlying, and the modelling assumption is that log-returns are approximately normally distributed. A natural follow-on question is: what does the distribution of the P&L look like under dynamic trading? Equivalently, what is the distribution of the call option terminal value <span>&#92;( V&#95;T &#92;)</span>?

When a portfolio involves derivative instruments, the distribution of the resulting P&L is typically *asymmetric* and far from normal. Options introduce convexity into payoffs, so the P&L distribution inherits a skewed or heavy-tailed character. This asymmetry is precisely what motivates the use of specialised risk measures rather than simply reporting mean and standard deviation. For a portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi &#92;)</span> of option positions, one wishes to compute the risk in <span>&#92;( P&#38;L = &#92;Pi&#95;t - &#92;Pi&#95;0 &#92;)</span> over a short horizon <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span> — for example, one trading day <span>&#92;( t = 1/250 &#92;)</span> of a year. The non-normality of this distribution motivates the risk measures examined in the next lecture.

---

# Lecture 11: Risk Measures, VaR, CVaR, and Implied Volatility

## Risk Measures: Motivation

Financial markets have historically experienced severe crashes roughly once per decade — the 1987 Black Monday crash and the 2008 global financial crisis are prominent examples. Financial institutions often hold complex instruments such as options whose P&L distributions are far from normal, and whose losses in extreme scenarios can vastly exceed what a naive standard-deviation-based risk estimate would suggest. The central practical question is: *what happens if the worst scenarios occur?*

Alan Greenspan, then Chairman of the Federal Reserve, articulated this concern at the Joint Central Bank Research Conference in 1995: *"Improving the characterization of the distribution of extreme values is of paramount importance."* This statement encapsulates the motivation for tail-risk measures that go beyond the mean and variance of the P&L distribution.

## Value-at-Risk

**Value-at-Risk (VaR)** was first introduced in the aftermath of the 1987 crash, originating from the Weatherstone (then JP Morgan CEO) "4:15 report" — a daily one-page summary of the firm's total risk. VaR is an extension of the notion of minimum profit (or equivalently maximum loss), and it is also frequently defined in terms of loss, where loss <span>&#92;( = -P&#38;L &#92;)</span>.

Precisely, VaR describes the *predicted minimum profit* (equivalently, the maximum loss) with a specified probability confidence level <span>&#92;( &#92;beta &#92;)</span> over a certain time horizon. VaR is a *percentile of the P&L distribution*. It allows a risk manager to state, for example: "With 95% confidence, the minimum profit (maximum loss) over the next week is <span>&#92;( &#92;cdots &#92;)</span>."

Formally, if <span>&#92;( p(x) &#92;)</span> is the probability density of P&L (whose explicit form is typically unknown for complex portfolios), then <span>&#92;( &#92;text{VaR}&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span> satisfies either of the equivalent conditions

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;{-&#92;infty}^{&#92;text{VaR}} p(x)\,dx = 1 - &#92;beta &#92;quad &#92;text{or} &#92;quad &#92;int&#95;{&#92;text{VaR}}^{&#92;infty} p(x)\,dx = &#92;beta. &#92;]</span>

The interpretation is that with probability <span>&#92;( &#92;beta&#92;% &#92;)</span>, the P&L is at least <span>&#92;( &#92;text{VaR} &#92;)</span>. When the P&L distribution is plotted as a loss distribution (i.e., <span>&#92;( -P&#38;L &#92;)</span>), the VaR and CVaR appear at the *right* tail; with respect to the P&L distribution itself, they appear at the *left* tail.

For a discrete set of scenarios, <span>&#92;( &#92;text{VaR}&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span> is the maximum P&L value <span>&#92;( x &#92;)</span> such that

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{Prob}(P&#38;L &#92;leq x) &#92;leq 1 - &#92;beta. &#92;]</span>

## Conditional Value-at-Risk

One serious deficiency of VaR is that it provides no information about the *magnitude* of losses beyond the quantile threshold. Knowing that losses exceed VaR with probability <span>&#92;( 1-&#92;beta &#92;)</span> says nothing about how bad those losses are — "What happens to the worst 5% of outcomes?" is a question that VaR cannot answer.

**Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR)**, also called Expected Shortfall, addresses this gap. Assuming that the P&L distribution is continuous, CVaR is defined as the *average P&L conditional on P&L being at most <span>&#92;( &#92;text{VaR}&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span>*:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;&#92;beta = &#92;mathbf{E}(P&#38;L \mid P&#38;L &#92;leq &#92;text{VaR}&#95;&#92;beta). &#92;]</span>

The interpretation is that CVaR is the *expected P&L given that we are experiencing the worst <span>&#92;( 1 - &#92;beta &#92;)</span> fraction of scenarios*. It captures not just the threshold but the average severity of tail losses. A technical revision to this definition is required when the P&L distribution is discontinuous (for example, when dealing with discrete scenario sets), but the intuition remains the same.

## VaR versus CVaR

A natural question is which risk measure — VaR or CVaR — is preferable. The answer from both theoretical and regulatory perspectives increasingly favours CVaR. CVaR is a *coherent* risk measure in the sense of Artzner et al. (1999), whereas VaR is not. The **main difference** concerns convexity: CVaR satisfies

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{CVaR}(&#92;alpha &#92;cdot &#92;Pi + (1-&#92;alpha) &#92;cdot &#92;hat{&#92;Pi}) &#92;leq &#92;alpha &#92;cdot &#92;text{CVaR}(&#92;Pi) + (1-&#92;alpha) &#92;cdot &#92;text{CVaR}(&#92;hat{&#92;Pi}) &#92;]</span>

for any portfolios <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi, &#92;hat{&#92;Pi} &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq &#92;alpha &#92;leq 1 &#92;)</span>. This convexity property means that diversification is always rewarded: the CVaR of a mixture of two portfolios is no larger than the weighted average of their individual CVaRs. VaR lacks convexity, which means that a diversified portfolio can in theory have a *higher* VaR than the sum of its components — a perverse property that undermines its use as a basis for risk limits. Regulation based on both VaR and CVaR has been imposed on financial institutions.

## Computing Scenario VaR and CVaR

In practice the P&L distribution is not known analytically, and one relies on Monte Carlo simulation. Given <span>&#92;( M &#92;)</span> independent P&L samples <span>&#92;( \{P&#38;L&#95;i,\, i = 1, &#92;ldots, M\} &#92;)</span>, the procedure is as follows.

First, sort the samples in ascending order so that <span>&#92;( P&#38;L&#95;1 &lt; P&#38;L&#95;2 &lt; &#92;cdots &lt; P&#38;L&#95;M &#92;)</span>. Next, identify the largest index <span>&#92;( i&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span> such that

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{i&#95;&#92;beta}{M} &#92;leq 1 - &#92;beta, &#92;quad &#92;text{equivalently,} &#92;quad i&#95;&#92;beta = &#92;lfloor (1-&#92;beta) &#92;cdot M &#92;rfloor. &#92;]</span>

The scenario VaR is then

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{VaR}&#95;&#92;beta = P&#38;L&#95;{i&#95;&#92;beta}, &#92;]</span>

that is, the <span>&#92;( i&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span>-th order statistic. The scenario CVaR is the average of the <span>&#92;( i&#95;&#92;beta &#92;)</span> worst outcomes:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;&#92;beta = &#92;frac{1}{i&#95;&#92;beta}(P&#38;L&#95;1 + &#92;cdots + P&#38;L&#95;{i&#95;&#92;beta}). &#92;]</span>

As a concrete example, consider <span>&#92;( M = 1000 &#92;)</span> paths and a confidence level of <span>&#92;( &#92;beta = 95&#92;% &#92;)</span>. Then <span>&#92;( i&#95;{5&#92;%} = &#92;lfloor 0.05 &#92;times 1000 &#92;rfloor = 50 &#92;)</span>, so

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{VaR}&#95;{95&#92;%} = P&#38;L&#95;{50}, &#92;quad &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;{95&#92;%} = &#92;frac{1}{50}(P&#38;L&#95;1 + &#92;cdots + P&#38;L&#95;{50}). &#92;]</span>

This scenario approach is widely used in industry, and regulatory capital requirements have been imposed based on both VaR and CVaR computed in precisely this manner.

## Model Uncertainty and Vega Risk

Even if all market inputs are observed without error, the Black-Scholes model depends on a volatility parameter <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> that must be estimated. The **option Greeks** measure the sensitivity of the option value to the underlying price and to model parameters. The three primary Greeks are:

- **Delta**: <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V / &#92;partial S &#92;)</span>, the sensitivity to the stock price.
- **Gamma**: <span>&#92;( &#92;partial^2 V / &#92;partial S^2 &#92;)</span>, the second-order sensitivity to the stock price.
- **Vega**: <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V / &#92;partial &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>, the sensitivity to the volatility.

Under the Black-Scholes model, the vega for both European calls and puts is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{vega} = &#92;frac{&#92;partial C}{&#92;partial &#92;sigma} = &#92;frac{&#92;partial P}{&#92;partial &#92;sigma} = &#92;frac{&#92;mathcal{N}'(d&#95;1)}{&#92;sigma S&#95;0 &#92;sqrt{T}}, &#92;quad &#92;text{where} &#92;quad &#92;mathcal{N}'(d) = &#92;frac{1}{&#92;sqrt{2&#92;pi}} e^{-&#92;frac{1}{2}d^2}. &#92;]</span>

Since <span>&#92;( &#92;mathcal{N}'(d&#95;1) > 0 &#92;)</span> always, vega is strictly positive for all options under the Black-Scholes model. Consequently, the option value as a function of volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is strictly monotonically increasing on <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;in (0, +&#92;infty) &#92;)</span>. This strict monotonicity is not just a curiosity — it implies that the mapping from volatility to option price is invertible, which is the foundation for **implied volatility**.

## Implied Volatility

Assume that today's market option price <span>&#92;( V&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}} &#92;)</span> is observed. The market price encodes information about the market's collective expectation of future volatility of the underlying. Since the Black-Scholes formula gives a one-to-one mapping between <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> and the option price (for fixed all other parameters), one can *invert* this relationship. The **implied volatility** <span>&#92;( &#92;check{&#92;sigma} &#92;)</span> is defined as the volatility that, when plugged into the Black-Scholes formula, reproduces the observed market price:

<span>&#92;[ V&#95;{\mathrm{BS}}(&#92;sigma) = V&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}}. &#92;]</span>

It can be shown that a unique positive implied volatility exists if and only if the market option price lies in the range of the Black-Scholes price as a function of <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>. For a European call, this means the market price must satisfy

<span>&#92;[ &#92;max(0,\, S&#95;0 - Ke^{-rT}) &#92;leq C&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}} &lt; S&#95;0. &#92;]</span>

Implied volatility is important for several reasons. It tells a trader the volatility that the option market is pricing in over the remaining life of the option — that is, the market's forward-looking estimate of risk. In practice, traders often quote and communicate option prices in terms of implied volatility rather than in dollar terms, because implied volatility is a more stable and intuitive quantity that strips out the mechanical dependence on moneyness and time to maturity. Knowing the volatility implied by market prices is also crucial for risk assessment and risk management, as it provides a direct measure of how much uncertainty the market perceives.

---

# Lecture 12: Volatility Smile, Implied Volatility, and SDE Approximation

## The Volatility Smile

The **implied volatility** <span>&#92;( &#92;check{&#92;sigma} &#92;)</span> for a given option is defined as the root of the nonlinear scalar equation

<span>&#92;[ f(&#92;sigma) = V&#95;{BS}(S&#95;0, 0;\, &#92;sigma) - V&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}} = 0, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( V&#95;{BS}(S&#95;0, 0;\, &#92;sigma) &#92;)</span> is the Black-Scholes option value function evaluated at the current stock price <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 &#92;)</span>, current time <span>&#92;( t = 0 &#92;)</span>, and volatility parameter <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>, for a specific option (a given strike <span>&#92;( K &#92;)</span> and maturity <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>).

If the Black-Scholes pricing model were literally correct — meaning that the underlying truly follows geometric Brownian motion with a single constant volatility — then all European options on the same underlying, regardless of their strike or maturity, would yield exactly the *same* implied volatility when inverted from market prices. The constant-<span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> Black-Scholes world predicts a flat implied volatility surface.

Empirical evidence tells a fundamentally different story. In practice, implied volatility varies systematically with both the strike price <span>&#92;( K &#92;)</span> and the time to expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>. This variation is called the **volatility smile** (or, depending on its shape, the *volatility skew* or *volatility sneer*). Real market data, such as that for European options on the QQQ fund tracking the NASDAQ 100 index (January 2004), shows that implied volatility typically decreases as the strike increases for equity options — a pattern known as the *volatility skew* or *sneer*. Intuitively, this reflects the market's greater concern about large downward moves (crashes) than the lognormal model assumes: out-of-the-money puts command higher implied volatilities than at-the-money or out-of-the-money calls.

The existence of the volatility smile constitutes evidence against the Black-Scholes model's constant-volatility assumption. The smile is a market phenomenon that must be taken into account when pricing and hedging exotic options, because using a single flat volatility will misprice options at non-at-the-money strikes.

## Computing Implied Volatility

The implied volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;check{&#92;sigma} &#92;)</span> is computed by solving the nonlinear equation <span>&#92;( f(&#92;sigma) = 0 &#92;)</span> numerically. The function <span>&#92;( f(&#92;sigma) = V&#95;{BS}(S&#95;0, 0;\, &#92;sigma) - V&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}} &#92;)</span> has a known shape: it can be shown that the second derivative satisfies <span>&#92;( f''(&#92;sigma) &gt; 0 &#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &lt; &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span> (so <span>&#92;( f &#92;)</span> is strictly *convex* below <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span>) and <span>&#92;( f''(&#92;sigma) &lt; 0 &#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &gt; &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span> (so <span>&#92;( f &#92;)</span> is strictly *concave* above <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span>), where the inflection point <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span> is given by

<span>&#92;[ &#92;sigma&#95;0^2 = &#92;left|&#92;ln&#92;!\left(&#92;frac{S&#95;0}{K}&#92;right) + rT&#92;right| &#92;cdot &#92;frac{2}{T}. &#92;]</span>

Manaster and Koehler (1982) established the following important result: if Newton's method is initialised at a starting value *above* <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span>, the Newton iterates converge to the implied volatility *monotonically* and *quadratically*. The Newton iteration for solving <span>&#92;( f(&#92;sigma) = 0 &#92;)</span> is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;sigma&#95;{k+1} = &#92;sigma&#95;k - &#92;frac{f(&#92;sigma&#95;k)}{f'(&#92;sigma&#95;k)} = &#92;sigma&#95;k - &#92;frac{V&#95;{BS}(&#92;sigma&#95;k) - V&#95;{\mathrm{mkt}}}{&#92;text{vega}(&#92;sigma&#95;k)}, &#92;]</span>

since <span>&#92;( f'(&#92;sigma) = &#92;partial V&#95;{BS}/&#92;partial &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is precisely the vega. Starting from above <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;0 &#92;)</span>, each iteration approximately doubles the number of correct significant digits in the estimate. Bisection is an alternative solver that is slower (linear convergence) but more robust, and is useful as a fallback when Newton fails to converge.

## Alternative Models: Local Volatility

The empirical existence of the volatility smile demonstrates that the constant-volatility Black-Scholes model is inadequate for capturing market dynamics. This has motivated a rich class of *alternative models*.

The simplest extension is the **local volatility function (LVF) model**, introduced by Dupire (1994). In this model the volatility is allowed to depend on both the current stock price and time:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = &#92;mu\, dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t)\, dZ&#95;t, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( Z&#95;t &#92;)</span> is a standard Brownian motion and <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S, t) &#92;)</span> is a *deterministic* function of the stock price and time. Under the risk-neutral probability measure, the drift <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> is replaced by the risk-free rate <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>:
