<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = r\, dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t)\, dZ&#95;t. &#92;]</span>

The LVF model retains the key property that option risk can be *eliminated* by dynamic hedging — the market remains complete — and so an option can be priced through *replication*. Dupire showed that there exists a unique local volatility surface <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S, t) &#92;)</span> that is consistent with any given set of observed European option prices (the *volatility surface*). The LVF model can thus reproduce the full observed volatility smile by construction.

## Stochastic Volatility: The Heston Model

A more ambitious generalisation allows the volatility itself to be a stochastic process. In **Heston's model** (under the risk-neutral measure), the stock price and its variance <span>&#92;( v &#92;)</span> satisfy the coupled system

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS}{S} = r\, dt + &#92;sqrt{v}\, dZ^{(1)}, &#92;]</span>
<span>&#92;[ dv = -&#92;lambda(v - &#92;bar{v})\, dt + &#92;eta&#92;sqrt{v}\, dZ^{(2)}, &#92;]</span>
<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{E}(dZ^{(1)} dZ^{(2)}) = &#92;rho\, dt, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;lambda &#92;)</span> is the speed of mean reversion of the variance <span>&#92;( v &#92;)</span> back to its long-run mean <span>&#92;( &#92;bar{v} &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( &#92;eta &#92;)</span> is the *volatility of volatility*, and <span>&#92;( &#92;rho &#92;)</span> is the correlation between the two Brownian motions. The variance process is a Cox-Ingersoll-Ross (CIR) process, which stays non-negative under appropriate parameter conditions (the Feller condition <span>&#92;( 2&#92;lambda&#92;bar{v} &#92;geq &#92;eta^2 &#92;)</span>).

The Heston model can address the volatility smile to some extent: the correlation <span>&#92;( &#92;rho &#92;)</span> between stock returns and volatility changes generates skew, while the volatility of volatility <span>&#92;( &#92;eta &#92;)</span> generates curvature. However, because volatility is now a second non-traded source of randomness, the market is *incomplete* — the risk associated with volatility fluctuations cannot be fully hedged by trading the stock alone.

## Jump Models: Merton's Jump-Diffusion

Another class of alternative models introduces **jumps** in the stock price path. Merton's jump-diffusion model takes the form

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = (r - &#92;kappa&#92;lambda)\, dt + &#92;sigma\, dZ&#95;t + (J-1)\, dq&#95;t, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( q&#95;t &#92;)</span> is a Poisson counting process with

<span>&#92;[ dq&#95;t = &#92;begin{cases} 1 &#92;&amp; &#92;text{with probability } &#92;lambda\, dt \\ 0 &#92;&amp; &#92;text{with probability } 1 - &#92;lambda\, dt, &#92;end{cases} &#92;]</span>

and <span>&#92;( &#92;kappa &#92;equiv &#92;mathbf{E}(J-1) &#92;)</span>. Here <span>&#92;( &#92;lambda &gt; 0 &#92;)</span> is the **jump intensity** — the average number of jumps per year, so that for example <span>&#92;( &#92;lambda = 0.1 &#92;)</span> means one jump every ten years on average. The term <span>&#92;( -&#92;kappa&#92;lambda\, dt &#92;)</span> in the drift is a compensator ensuring that the expected return under the risk-neutral measure remains <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>.

When a jump occurs (i.e., <span>&#92;( dq&#95;t = 1 &#92;)</span>), the stock price jumps instantaneously from its pre-jump value <span>&#92;( S(t^-) &#92;)</span> to <span>&#92;( S(t) = S(t^-)J &#92;)</span>. The jump amplitude <span>&#92;( J &#92;)</span> is a positive random variable; in Merton's original specification, <span>&#92;( &#92;log J &#92;)</span> is normally distributed with constant mean <span>&#92;( &#92;mu&#95;J &#92;)</span> and constant variance <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma&#95;J^2 &#92;)</span>.

For Merton's jump model, analytic pricing formulas exist for European calls and puts (see Merton 1976, Lewis 2002). Nevertheless, the market is *incomplete* because jump risk cannot be eliminated by a finite number of traded instruments. As a practical matter, hedging using standard options with shorter maturity can be effective in managing the residual jump risk. More complex hybrid models — combining jumps with stochastic volatility (jump + SV), or jumps with a local volatility function (jump + LVF) — provide even richer descriptions of market dynamics. Simulation of jump models can be implemented by using the uniform distribution to determine whether a jump occurs in each time step, as detailed in Section 9.5 of the course notes.

## Numerical Approximation to SDEs

For general stochastic processes where analytic solutions do not exist, one must rely on numerical time-stepping schemes. Consider an Ito process of the general form

<span>&#92;[ dX&#95;t = a(X&#95;t, t)\, dt + b(X&#95;t, t)\, dZ&#95;t. &#92;]</span>

The rigorous meaning of this SDE is given by the integral equation

<span>&#92;[ X(t + &#92;Delta t) = X(t) + &#92;int&#95;t^{t+&#92;Delta t} a(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau)\, d&#92;tau + &#92;int&#95;t^{t+&#92;Delta t} b(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau)\, dZ(&#92;tau). &#92;]</span>

Over a small interval <span>&#92;( [t&#95;n,\, t&#95;n + &#92;Delta t] &#92;)</span>, the integrands can be approximated by their values at the left endpoint <span>&#92;( t&#95;n &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;n + &#92;Delta t} a(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau)\, d&#92;tau &#92;approx a(X(t&#95;n), t&#95;n)&#92;Delta t, &#92;quad &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;n+&#92;Delta t} b(X(&#92;tau),&#92;tau)\, dZ(&#92;tau) &#92;approx b(X(t&#95;n), t&#95;n) &#92;cdot &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n), &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n) = Z(t&#95;n + &#92;Delta t) - Z(t&#95;n) &#92;)</span> is the Brownian increment over the step.

## The Euler-Maruyama Method

The **Euler-Maruyama method** is the simplest time-stepping scheme for SDEs, obtained by substituting the above approximations. Let <span>&#92;( &#92;tilde{X}&#95;t &#92;)</span> denote the numerical approximation to <span>&#92;( X&#95;t &#92;)</span>. The Euler-Maruyama update is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;{n+1}) - &#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n) = a(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n)&#92;Delta t + b(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n) &#92;cdot &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;cdot &#92;phi&#95;t, &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span>, so that <span>&#92;( &#92;sqrt{&#92;Delta t} &#92;cdot &#92;phi&#95;t &#92;)</span> has the same distribution as the Brownian increment <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n) &#92;)</span>.

## Weak and Strong Convergence

There are two distinct notions of accuracy for SDE numerical schemes. **Weak convergence** concerns the accuracy of expected values of functions of the solution. A numerical scheme is said to converge with *weak order* <span>&#92;( &#92;beta &#92;)</span> if, for sufficiently small <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &gt; 0 &#92;)</span> and for each polynomial <span>&#92;( g &#92;)</span>,

<span>&#92;[ |&#92;mathbf{E}(g(X&#95;T)) - &#92;mathbf{E}(g(&#92;tilde{X}&#95;T))| &#92;leq &#92;text{constant} &#92;cdot (&#92;Delta t)^&#92;beta. &#92;]</span>

Weak convergence measures accuracy in an *expected value* sense — it is the relevant notion when computing option prices, which are discounted expected payoffs. The Euler-Maruyama method has **weak order <span>&#92;( &#92;beta = 1 &#92;)</span>**.

**Strong convergence** measures path-wise accuracy. A scheme converges with *strong order* <span>&#92;( &#92;gamma &#92;)</span> if, for sufficiently small <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &gt; 0 &#92;)</span>,

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathbf{E}(|X&#95;T - &#92;tilde{X}&#95;T|) &#92;leq &#92;text{constant} &#92;cdot (&#92;Delta t)^&#92;gamma. &#92;]</span>

Strong convergence is the relevant notion when path-by-path accuracy is required — for instance, when simulating the variance process in a stochastic volatility model where the variance must remain non-negative along each simulated path. The Euler-Maruyama method has **strong order <span>&#92;( &#92;gamma = 1/2 &#92;)</span>**, which is lower than its weak order and can be a significant limitation for applications requiring path accuracy.

## The Milstein Method: A Higher-Order Scheme

The **Milstein method** improves upon Euler-Maruyama by incorporating an additional correction term derived from Ito's lemma applied to the diffusion coefficient <span>&#92;( b(X&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span>. The Milstein update rule is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;{n+1}) = &#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n) + a&#92;!\left(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n&#92;right)&#92;Delta t + b&#92;!\left(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n&#92;right) &#92;cdot &#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n) + &#92;frac{1}{2} b&#92;!\left(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n&#92;right) &#92;frac{&#92;partial b}{&#92;partial X}&#92;!\left(&#92;tilde{X}(t&#95;n), t&#95;n&#92;right) &#92;left[(&#92;Delta Z(t&#95;n))^2 - &#92;Delta t&#92;right]. &#92;]</span>

The extra term proportional to <span>&#92;( (&#92;Delta Z)^2 - &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> is the Milstein correction. The Milstein method achieves **strong and weak convergence order <span>&#92;( &#92;gamma = &#92;beta = 1 &#92;)</span>**, which is a significant improvement over the Euler-Maruyama method's strong order of <span>&#92;( 1/2 &#92;)</span>.

The derivation of the Milstein method proceeds by applying Ito's lemma to the coefficient functions <span>&#92;( a(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau) &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( b(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau) &#92;)</span> over the interval <span>&#92;( [t&#95;n, &#92;tau] &#92;)</span>. By Ito's lemma,

<span>&#92;[ a(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau) = a(X(t&#95;n), t&#95;n) + &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} &#92;left[&#92;frac{&#92;partial a}{&#92;partial &#92;tau} + a&#92;frac{&#92;partial a}{&#92;partial X} + &#92;frac{1}{2}b^2&#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 a}{&#92;partial X^2}&#92;right] du + &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} b &#92;frac{&#92;partial a}{&#92;partial X} dZ&#95;u, &#92;]</span>

and similarly for <span>&#92;( b(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau) &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ b(X(&#92;tau), &#92;tau) = b(X(t&#95;n), t&#95;n) + &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} &#92;left[&#92;frac{&#92;partial b}{&#92;partial &#92;tau} + a&#92;frac{&#92;partial b}{&#92;partial X} + &#92;frac{1}{2}b^2&#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 b}{&#92;partial X^2}&#92;right] du + &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} b &#92;frac{&#92;partial b}{&#92;partial X} dZ&#95;u. &#92;]</span>

The double integrals of the form <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} du\, d&#92;tau &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} du\, dZ&#95;&#92;tau &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} dZ&#95;u\, d&#92;tau &#92;)</span> are all of order higher than <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> and are therefore dropped. The key term that survives and produces the Milstein correction is the stochastic double integral

<span>&#92;[ &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{t&#95;{n+1}} &#92;left( &#92;int&#95;{t&#95;n}^{&#92;tau} b(X(u), u) &#92;frac{&#92;partial b}{&#92;partial X}\, dZ&#95;u &#92;right) dZ&#95;&#92;tau, &#92;]</span>

which, to leading order, evaluates to <span>&#92;( b&#92;,(&#92;partial b/&#92;partial X)&#92;cdot &#92;frac{1}{2}[(&#92;Delta Z)^2 - &#92;Delta t] &#92;)</span>. The factor <span>&#92;( (&#92;Delta Z)^2 - &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span> has mean zero (since <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}[(&#92;Delta Z)^2] = &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>), so the Milstein correction is a mean-zero term that nonetheless reduces the path-wise error from order <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t^{1/2} &#92;)</span> to order <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta t &#92;)</span>. For further details on the derivation, see Seydel, *Tools for Computational Finance*, 2006.

The practical upshot is that when accuracy along individual paths is required — for instance, in stochastic volatility models where the simulated variance must remain positive — the Milstein method is the more appropriate choice. The Euler-Maruyama scheme is simpler to implement and adequate for computing option prices via expected payoffs (weak convergence), but its poor strong convergence can lead to numerical artefacts such as negative variance along some paths.

# Lecture 13: The Black-Scholes PDE and American Options

## Models for Correlated Processes

When modeling a financial system with multiple underlying assets, we often need to capture the statistical dependence between their returns. Correlated rates of return, with **correlation** <span>&#92;( &#92;rho &#92;)</span>, can be modeled by a system of stochastic differential equations. For two assets <span>&#92;( S^{(1)} &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( S^{(2)} &#92;)</span>, the **Local Volatility Function (LVF)** model for correlated processes is written as the system

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{dS^{(1)}}{S^{(1)}} = &#92;mu^{(1)} dt + &#92;sigma^{(1)} dZ^{(1)}, &#92;quad
&#92;frac{dS^{(2)}}{S^{(2)}} = &#92;mu^{(2)} dt + &#92;sigma^{(2)} dZ^{(2)}, &#92;quad
&#92;mathbf{E}&#92;!\left(dZ^{(1)} dZ^{(2)}&#92;right) = &#92;rho &#92; dt,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( Z^{(i)} &#92;)</span> are standard Brownian motions. Substituting the discrete-time representation <span>&#92;( dZ^{(i)} = &#92;phi^{(i)} &#92;sqrt{dt} &#92;)</span>, where <span>&#92;( &#92;phi^{(i)} &#92;)</span> are standard normal random variables, the correlation condition becomes

<span>&#92;[
&#92;mathbf{E}&#92;!\left(&#92;phi^{(1)} &#92;phi^{(2)}&#92;right) = &#92;rho.
&#92;]</span>

Therefore, to simulate two correlated asset paths we need to generate standard normals <span>&#92;( &#92;phi^{(1)}, &#92;phi^{(2)} &#92;)</span> with prescribed pairwise correlation <span>&#92;( &#92;rho &#92;)</span>.

## Generating Correlated Standard Normals

In general, suppose we wish to simulate a random vector <span>&#92;( &#92;phi = (&#92;phi^{(1)}, &#92;phi^{(2)}, &#92;ldots, &#92;phi^{(n)}) &#92;)</span> of standard normal components that have a specified **correlation matrix** <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span>. We can always generate independent standard normals <span>&#92;( &#92;epsilon&#95;1, &#92;ldots, &#92;epsilon&#95;n &#92;)</span> satisfying <span>&#92;( &#92;epsilon&#95;i &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span>, but the challenge is to introduce the desired cross-correlations.

### Positive Semi-Definiteness of the Correlation Matrix

Let <span>&#92;( &#92;phi = &#92;{&#92;phi^{(1)}, &#92;ldots, &#92;phi^{(n)}&#92;} &#92;)</span> be standard normals with covariance correlation matrix <span>&#92;( Q = (&#92;rho&#95;{ij}) &#92;)</span>, so that <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi^{(i)}, &#92;phi^{(j)}) = &#92;rho&#95;{ij} &#92;)</span>. For any vector <span>&#92;( x &#92;)</span>, consider the random variable <span>&#92;( &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n &#92;phi^{(i)} x&#95;i &#92;)</span>. Since each <span>&#92;( &#92;phi^{(i)} &#92;)</span> has zero mean, this sum has mean zero:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;mathbf{E}&#92;!\left(&#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n &#92;phi^{(i)} x&#95;i&#92;right) = &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n x&#95;i &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi^{(i)}) = 0.
&#92;]</span>

Computing its variance reveals

<span>&#92;[
&#92;mathbf{var}&#92;!\left(&#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n x&#95;i &#92;phi^{(i)}&#92;right)
= &#92;mathbf{E}&#92;!\left(&#92;left(&#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n x&#95;i &#92;phi^{(i)}&#92;right)^2&#92;right)
= &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^n &#92;mathbf{E}(x&#95;i x&#95;j &#92;phi^{(i)} &#92;phi^{(j)})
= &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^n x&#95;i x&#95;j &#92;rho&#95;{ij}
= x^T Q x.
&#92;]</span>

Since variance is always non-negative, we have <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{var}(&#92;sum x&#95;i &#92;phi^{(i)}) &#92;geq 0 &#92;)</span>, which immediately implies <span>&#92;( x^T Q x &#92;geq 0 &#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;( x &#92;)</span>. Therefore any valid correlation matrix <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span> must be **positive semi-definite**.

### Cholesky Factorization

For any symmetric positive semi-definite matrix <span>&#92;( Q &#92;)</span>, there exists an upper triangular matrix <span>&#92;( G &#92;)</span> such that

<span>&#92;[
Q = G^T G,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( G &#92;)</span> is upper triangular. In MATLAB this factorization is computed as <span>&#92;( G = &#92;mathtt{chol}(Q) &#92;)</span>. Now let <span>&#92;( &#92;epsilon&#95;1, &#92;ldots, &#92;epsilon&#95;n &#92;)</span> be independent standard normals satisfying <span>&#92;( &#92;epsilon&#95;i &#92;sim &#92;mathcal{N}(0,1) &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;epsilon&#95;i, &#92;epsilon&#95;j) = 0 &#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;( i &#92;neq j &#92;)</span>. Define the vector <span>&#92;( &#92;phi = G^T &#92;epsilon &#92;)</span>, that is

<span>&#92;[
&#92;phi = G^T &#92;epsilon.
&#92;]</span>

Then <span>&#92;( &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi) = G^T &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;epsilon) = 0 &#92;)</span> and the covariance matrix of <span>&#92;( &#92;phi &#92;)</span> is

<span>&#92;[
&#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;phi &#92;phi^T)
= &#92;mathbf{E}(G^T &#92;epsilon &#92;epsilon^T G)
= G^T &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;epsilon &#92;epsilon^T) G
= G^T I G
= G^T G
= Q.
&#92;]</span>

So <span>&#92;( &#92;phi &#92;)</span> has exactly the desired correlation structure. The practical recipe in MATLAB is therefore: compute the Cholesky factor <span>&#92;( G = &#92;mathtt{chol}(Q) &#92;)</span>, draw <span>&#92;( &#92;epsilon = &#92;mathtt{randn}(n,1) &#92;)</span>, and set <span>&#92;( &#92;phi = G^T &#92;epsilon &#92;)</span>.

## The Black-Scholes PDE

### Derivation via the Hedge Portfolio

We now turn to the central analytical result of the module: the **Black-Scholes PDE**. Assume a more general **Local Volatility Function (LVF)** model for the underlying asset price <span>&#92;( S&#95;t &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{dS&#95;t}{S&#95;t} = &#92;mu(S&#95;t, t) &#92; dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t) &#92; dZ&#95;t, &#92;quad Z&#95;t &#92;sim &#92;text{standard Brownian motion},
&#92;]</span>

which reduces to the classical Black-Scholes model when <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> are constants. We wish to find the value <span>&#92;( V(S, t) &#92;)</span> of a European option with expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> and payoff function <span>&#92;( &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;T) &#92;)</span>. We assume no-arbitrage, continuous trading, and that any amount (infinitely divisible) of the asset can be traded.

Applying **Ito's Lemma** to <span>&#92;( V(S&#95;t, t) &#92;)</span>, the option value satisfies the SDE

<span>&#92;[
dV = &#92;left[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;mu(S&#95;t,t) S&#95;t &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma(S&#95;t,t)^2 S&#95;t^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} &#92;right] dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t,t) S&#95;t &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} dZ&#95;t.
&#92;]</span>

At time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, form the **delta-hedged portfolio** <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;t = &#92;{V&#95;t, -&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> consisting of a long position in the option and a short position of <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t &#92;)</span> units of the underlying. The instantaneous change of this portfolio is

<span>&#92;[
d&#92;Pi&#95;t = dV&#95;t - &#92;delta&#95;t &#92; dS&#95;t.
&#92;]</span>

Expanding using the SDE for <span>&#92;( V &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>, and choosing <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t = &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} &#92;)</span> so that the stochastic <span>&#92;( dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span> terms cancel, the portfolio becomes **riskless** and we obtain

<span>&#92;[
d&#92;Pi&#95;t = &#92;left( &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma(S&#95;t, t)^2 S&#95;t^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} &#92;right) dt.
&#92;]</span>

Notice that the drift term <span>&#92;( &#92;mu(S&#95;t,t) &#92;)</span> has cancelled: the hedge eliminates both the stochastic exposure and the drift. By the **no-arbitrage principle**, a riskless portfolio must earn the risk-free rate <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>. If <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t > r&#92;Pi&#95;t &#92; dt &#92;)</span>, the portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;{V&#95;t, -&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, -&#92;Pi&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> (borrowing <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;t &#92;)</span> in cash) is an arbitrage; if <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi_t < r&#92;Pi_t &#92; dt &#92;)</span>, the reverse portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;{-V&#95;t, +&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, &#92;Pi&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> is an arbitrage. Hence we must have <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t = r&#92;Pi&#95;t &#92; dt &#92;)</span>, which gives

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma(S&#95;t,t)^2 S&#95;t^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} = r&#92;left(V&#95;t - S&#95;t &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S}&#92;right).
&#92;]</span>

Rearranging, the **Black-Scholes PDE** for any European option is the boundary value problem

<span>&#92;[
&#92;begin{cases}
&#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;dfrac{1}{2} &#92;sigma(S,t)^2 S^2 &#92;dfrac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + rS &#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} - rV = 0, &#92;quad 0 &#92;leq S < +&#92;infty, &#92;quad 0 &#92;leq t < T, &#92;&#92;[6pt]
V(S, T) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S).
&#92;end{cases}
&#92;]</span>

### Remarks on the BS PDE

Several important observations follow from this derivation. First, any European option value function satisfies the BS PDE throughout the region <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq S < +&#92;infty &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq t < T &#92;)</span>. Second, the BS PDE holds even when volatility is a function <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S,t) &#92;)</span> of both price and time, and it can be generalized to multi-asset settings. Third — and most striking — the expected return <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span> of the underlying does **not** appear in the BS PDE. Consequently, the no-arbitrage value of the option does not depend on <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span>. The BS PDE is therefore purely an expression of no-arbitrage and the existence of a hedged portfolio, not of any belief about market direction. It is a natural question whether the *expected return* of the option depends on <span>&#92;( &#92;mu &#92;)</span>; the answer is no for the no-arbitrage price, precisely because hedging removes the drift exposure.

As a simple verification, note that <span>&#92;( V(S,t) = S &#92;)</span> satisfies the PDE. Since <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V / &#92;partial t = 0 &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( &#92;partial V / &#92;partial S = 1 &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( &#92;partial^2 V / &#92;partial S^2 = 0 &#92;)</span>, substituting into the left-hand side gives <span>&#92;( 0 + 0 + rS &#92;cdot 1 - rS = 0 &#92;)</span>, which is satisfied identically. This is financially reasonable: holding one unit of the underlying with payoff <span>&#92;( S&#95;T &#92;)</span> at maturity is itself a financial contract whose value today is simply <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>, consistent with the boundary condition <span>&#92;( V(S&#95;T, T) = S&#95;T &#92;)</span>.

Computational methods for option pricing include binomial and lattice models, PDE-based methods (typically used when the number of risk factors is no more than 3), and Monte Carlo methods (typically used for higher-dimensional problems).

---

# Lecture 14: The American Option and the Complementarity Formulation

## Black-Scholes PDE for European Options — Recap

The lecture opens by recalling the general LVF setup. Under the asset dynamics <span>&#92;( dS&#95;t/S&#95;t = &#92;mu(S&#95;t,t)dt + &#92;sigma(S&#95;t,t)dZ&#95;t &#92;)</span>, the delta-hedged portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;t = &#92;{V&#95;t, -&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> with <span>&#92;( &#92;delta&#95;t = &#92;partial V/&#92;partial S &#92;)</span> satisfies <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t = (&#92;partial V/&#92;partial t + &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;partial^2 V/&#92;partial S^2) dt &#92;)</span>. For a European option the no-arbitrage condition <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t = r&#92;Pi&#95;t &#92; dt &#92;)</span> forces the BS PDE to hold as an equality. More explicitly, if <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t > r&#92;Pi&#95;t dt &#92;)</span> then the portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;{V&#95;t, -&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, -&#92;Pi&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> is an arbitrage, while if <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi_t < r&#92;Pi_t dt &#92;)</span> then <span>&#92;( &#92;{-V&#95;t, +&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, &#92;Pi&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> is an arbitrage; hence equality must hold and the BS PDE is a strict equality for European options.

## American Options

A **European option** can be exercised only at the expiry date <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>. An **American option**, by contrast, can be exercised at any time <span>&#92;( t &#92;leq T &#92;)</span> at the discretion of the holder. The fair option value is the no-arbitrage value computed under the assumption that the holder exercises optimally. The optimal exercise strategy for a European option is simple — exercise at <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> only when the option expires in the money, i.e., when <span>&#92;( &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;T) > 0 &#92;)</span>. For a European put, the holder exercises at <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> when <span>&#92;( S_T < K &#92;)</span>.

For American options the situation is much richer. Mathematically, pricing an American option in continuous time is a **stochastic optimal control** (or *optimal stopping*) problem. The American option is also **weakly path dependent**: at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, whether the option still exists depends on whether it has already been exercised along the price path.

## Properties of the American Option Value Function

Let <span>&#92;( V^A(S, t) &#92;)</span> denote the value of the American option at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, assuming the option has not yet been exercised.

**Property 1.** The American option value is always at least as large as the immediate exercise value:

<span>&#92;[
V^A(S, t) &#92;geq &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S).
&#92;]</span>

If this were violated at some point, an arbitrage would exist by immediately exercising the option.

**Property 2.** An American option is worth at least as much as the corresponding European option:

<span>&#92;[
C&#95;t^A &#92;geq C&#95;t^E, &#92;quad P&#95;t^A &#92;geq P&#95;t^E,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( C&#95;t^A, P&#95;t^A &#92;)</span> denote the American call and put prices, and <span>&#92;( C&#95;t^E, P&#95;t^E &#92;)</span> denote their European counterparts. This holds because the American option grants strictly more rights.

**Property 3.** When the underlying pays **no dividend**, it is never optimal to exercise an American call early, but it may be optimal to exercise an American put early. To see why for calls, observe that under no-arbitrage with <span>&#92;( r > 0 &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq t < T &#92;)</span>, the European call value satisfies

<span>&#92;[
C^E(S, t) &#92;geq &#92;max(S - Ke^{-r(T-t)}, 0) > &#92;max(S - K, 0).
&#92;]</span>

This inequality is established by contradiction: if <span>&#92;( C^E(S,t) < &#92;max(S - Ke^{-r(T-t)}, 0) &#92;)</span>, then at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span> the portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;{C^E, -S, Ke^{-r(T-t)}&#92;} &#92;)</span> has negative value <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi_t < 0 &#92;)</span>, yet at <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> the value <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;T = 0 &#92;)</span>, which is an arbitrage. The graph of the European call value therefore always lies strictly above the immediate exercise payoff <span>&#92;( (S-K)^+ &#92;)</span>. This means that a holder who wishes to monetize the option is always better off **selling** the call in the market rather than exercising it, because the market price exceeds the exercise payoff. Consequently <span>&#92;( C^A(S,t) = C^E(S,t) > &#92;max(S-K, 0) &#92;)</span> and early exercise of an American call is suboptimal when there are no dividends.

## Optimal Exercising and the Early Exercise Boundary

For an American put, early exercise can be optimal when the stock price is sufficiently low. At expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>, it is optimal to exercise a put if <span>&#92;( S_T < S_T^* = K &#92;)</span>. At any earlier time <span>&#92;( 0 &#92;leq t < T &#92;)</span>, there exists a **critical price** <span>&#92;( S&#95;t^&#42; &#92;)</span> that defines the optimal exercise strategy: for a put, the holder should exercise at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span> if and only if <span>&#92;( S_t < S_t^* &#92;)</span>.

The curve <span>&#92;( &#92;{S&#95;t^&#42;&#92;}&#95;{t=0}^T &#92;)</span> is called the **early exercise boundary**. Determining the optimal exercise strategy for an American option holder is equivalent to determining this boundary. From the early exercise curve for an American put (with typical parameters <span>&#92;( K = 100 &#92;)</span>), the boundary starts well below the strike at <span>&#92;( t = 0 &#92;)</span> and rises monotonically to converge to <span>&#92;( K &#92;)</span> at expiry <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span>. Only when the stock price falls below this curve does immediate exercise dominate.

## American Options with Dividend: the Call Case

With a continuous **dividend yield** <span>&#92;( q > 0 &#92;)</span>, the BS PDE for a European option is modified to

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + (r - q) S &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} - rV = 0.
&#92;]</span>

Consider an American call when the underlying pays continuous yield <span>&#92;( q &#92;)</span>. If <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> is sufficiently large at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, the call will be exercised at expiry with high probability, and the call is essentially the same as <span>&#92;( S - K &#92;)</span> at expiry, which equals <span>&#92;( Se^{-q(T-t)} - Ke^{-r(T-t)} &#92;)</span> at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>. Thus the European call value has the asymptotic behavior

<span>&#92;[
C(S, t) &#92;approx S e^{-q(T-t)} - K e^{-r(T-t)}, &#92;quad S &#92;to +&#92;infty.
&#92;]</span>

When <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> is large enough it is possible that

<span>&#92;[
0 < S e^{-q(T-t)} - K e^{-r(T-t)} < S - K,
&#92;]</span>

meaning the European call price can fall below the immediate exercise payoff <span>&#92;( &#92;max(S-K,0) = S - K &#92;)</span>. Therefore, when the underlying pays a continuous dividend yield <span>&#92;( q &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> is sufficiently large, it can be **optimal for the American call holder to exercise early**. The dividend creates an incentive to exercise: by holding the call the investor forgoes the dividend stream, and for deep in-the-money calls this foregone income can outweigh the time value benefit of waiting.

## PDE Formulation for American Option Pricing

To price an American option numerically, we need a PDE formulation that **implicitly** determines the optimal exercise decision. For simplicity assume no dividends (the case with dividends is analogous). At time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, form the same hedge portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;t = &#92;{V&#95;t, -(&#92;partial V/&#92;partial S) S&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span>. Ito's Lemma gives the same expression <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t = (&#92;partial V/&#92;partial t + &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;partial^2 V/&#92;partial S^2) dt &#92;)</span>.

For an American option, arbitrage strategy (1) — which requires only a **long** position in the option — remains valid. If <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t > r&#92;Pi&#95;t dt &#92;)</span>, then the portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;{V&#95;t, -&#92;delta&#95;t S&#95;t, -&#92;Pi&#95;t&#92;} &#92;)</span> is an arbitrage, so we must have <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi&#95;t &#92;leq r&#92;Pi&#95;t dt &#92;)</span>. Equivalently, in terms of the option value:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + r &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} S - rV &#92;leq 0.
&#92;]</span>

Financially, this means that the riskless hedge portfolio <span>&#92;( &#92;Pi&#95;t &#92;)</span> cannot grow at a rate *higher* than the risk-free rate.

The reverse arbitrage strategy (2) — which requires a **short** position in the option — may not be available if the holder decides to exercise the option. Hence we cannot argue that <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi_t < r&#92;Pi_t dt &#92;)</span> always leads to arbitrage. However, consider the **continuation region** where <span>&#92;( V&#95;t > &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;t) &#92;)</span>: in this case exercise is not optimal. If <span>&#92;( d&#92;Pi_t < r&#92;Pi_t dt &#92;)</span>, consider the zero-cost strategy

<span>&#92;[
&#92;bar{&#92;Pi}&#95;t = &#92;left&#92;{ -V&#95;t, &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} S&#95;t, &#92;left&#92;{V&#95;t - &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} S&#95;t&#92;right&#92;} &#92;right&#92;},
&#92;]</span>

which has value <span>&#92;( &#92;bar{&#92;Pi}&#95;t = 0 &#92;)</span>. If the option is exercised at <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span>, we immediately unwind positions and receive <span>&#92;( V&#95;t - &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;t) > 0 &#92;)</span>. If the option is not exercised, <span>&#92;( d&#92;bar{&#92;Pi}&#95;t = -d&#92;Pi&#95;t + r&#92;Pi&#95;t dt > 0 &#92;)</span>. Either way we profit with zero initial investment, which is an arbitrage. Therefore when <span>&#92;( V&#95;t > &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;t) &#92;)</span>, it must be that <span>&#92;( d&#92;bar{&#92;Pi}&#95;t = 0 &#92;)</span>, i.e., the BS PDE holds as a strict equality.

## The Complementarity Formulation

Combining the two constraints, no-arbitrage for American options implies <span>&#92;( V(S,t) &#92;geq &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;)</span> and

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + (r-q) S &#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} - rV &#92;leq 0.
&#92;]</span>

In addition, either <span>&#92;( (S,t) &#92;)</span> is in the continuation region <span>&#92;( &#92;mathcal{C} = &#92;{(S,t): V(S,t) > &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S)&#92;} &#92;)</span> (in which case it is not optimal to exercise and the BS PDE is an equality) or <span>&#92;( V(S,t) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;)</span> (the exercise region). These two conditions are combined into the **complementarity condition**

<span>&#92;[
&#92;left(V(S,t) - &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S)&#92;right) &#92;left(&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;frac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + r&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} S - rV &#92;right) = 0.
&#92;]</span>

The complete **partial differential complementarity problem** for American option pricing is therefore:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;begin{cases}
&#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;dfrac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;dfrac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + (r-q) S &#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} - rV &#92;leq 0, &#92;&#92;[6pt]
V(S,t) - &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;geq 0, &#92;&#92;[6pt]
&#92;left(V(S,t) - &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S)&#92;right)&#92;!\left(&#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial t} + &#92;dfrac{1}{2}&#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;dfrac{&#92;partial^2 V}{&#92;partial S^2} + (r-q)S&#92;dfrac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial S} - rV&#92;right) = 0, &#92;&#92;[6pt]
0 &#92;leq S < +&#92;infty, &#92;quad 0 < t < T, &#92;&#92;[6pt]
V(S, T) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S).
&#92;end{cases}
&#92;]</span>

This formulation elegantly encodes both the PDE in the continuation region and the early exercise constraint simultaneously. Importantly, the **early exercise boundary** <span>&#92;( &#92;{S&#95;t^&#42;&#92;} &#92;)</span> is determined **implicitly** by the solution <span>&#92;( V(S,t) &#92;)</span>. For a put, the critical price at time <span>&#92;( t &#92;)</span> is <span>&#92;( S&#95;t^&#42; = &#92;max&#92;{S : V(S,t) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S)&#92;} &#92;)</span>. For an American call with dividend yield, it is <span>&#92;( S&#95;t^&#42; = &#92;min&#92;{S : V(S,t) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S)&#92;} &#92;)</span>.

## Introduction to the PDE Approach for Pricing European Options

When <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( q &#92;)</span> are constants, a series of variable substitutions reduces the BS PDE to the **heat equation**. Introduce <span>&#92;( S = Ke^x &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( t = T - 2&#92;tau/&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( &#92;hat&#92;delta = 2r/&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( &#92;delta = 2(r-q)/&#92;sigma^2 &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( V(S,t) &#92;equiv v(x,&#92;tau) &#92;)</span>, and further <span>&#92;( v(x,&#92;tau) = Ke^{-&#92;frac{1}{2}(&#92;delta-1)x - (&#92;frac{1}{4}(&#92;delta-1)^2 + &#92;hat&#92;delta)&#92;tau} u(x,&#92;tau) &#92;)</span>. Under these transformations the BS PDE is equivalent to

<span>&#92;[
u&#95;&#92;tau = u&#95;{xx},
&#92;]</span>

which is the classical *heat equation*: it models the diffusion of heat along an infinite bar from an initial distribution. An important property is that the solution to a heat equation is **infinitely smooth** for all <span>&#92;( &#92;tau > 0 &#92;)</span>, even if the initial distribution (corresponding to the option payoff) is discontinuous. This smoothing underlies the analytical Black-Scholes formula for constant-parameter European options.

### Types of PDE

A general PDE with two independent variables can be written as <span>&#92;( Au&#95;{xx} + Bu&#95;{xy} + Cu&#95;{yy} + F(u&#95;x, u&#95;y, u, x, y) = 0 &#92;)</span>. The PDE is classified by the sign of the discriminant <span>&#92;( B^2 - 4AC &#92;)</span>: it is **parabolic** when <span>&#92;( B^2 - 4AC = 0 &#92;)</span> (which is the case for the BS equations), **hyperbolic** when <span>&#92;( B^2 - 4AC > 0 &#92;)</span>, and **elliptic** when <span>&#92;( B^2 - 4AC < 0 &#92;)</span>. Different types of PDE have fundamentally different qualitative properties and require different numerical treatment.

The heat equation (and thus the BS equation with constant <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span>) has an explicit closed-form solution — namely, the Black-Scholes formula. However, for LVF models with <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma(S,t) &#92;)</span> varying in space and time, and for American options, numerical methods based on discretizations are required to compute solutions.

---

# Lecture 15: Finite Difference Discretization of the BS PDE

## Numerical Methods for PDE: Overview

The preceding lectures established the BS PDE and its complementarity extension for American options. We now turn to **numerical methods** for solving these equations. It is important first to understand which methods are appropriate in different settings.

### Comparison of Numerical Methods

**Lattice methods** (binomial trees and their generalizations) are actually equivalent to simple *explicit finite difference* methods applied to the BS PDE. While they are intuitive, they are not very efficient and become very complex for certain path-dependent features.

**Monte Carlo methods** simulate paths of the underlying asset and estimate option prices as discounted expectations. Monte Carlo is the **method of choice when there are more than 3 underlying stochastic factors**, because PDE methods suffer from the curse of dimensionality in high dimensions. However, Monte Carlo is generally difficult to apply to American options with early exercise features. The *Longstaff-Schwartz* least-squares Monte Carlo method addresses this, but it remains significantly more complex than PDE approaches. For problems with 3 or fewer stochastic factors, Monte Carlo is not very efficient compared to PDE methods.

**Numerical PDE methods** are the **method of choice for fewer than 3 stochastic factors**. They offer several advantages: complex features such as complex American-type contracts are easy to handle within the PDE complementarity framework; they compute option values and hedging parameters (Greeks) for the **whole range of asset prices** simultaneously, not just a single point, which is useful for simulating hedging strategies; they can handle complex nonlinear PDE problems arising in finance; and one can build a general library so that many different contracts can be quickly priced.

## The BS PDE in Time-to-Expiry Form

For the following discretization discussion, we work with the transformation <span>&#92;( &#92;tau = T - t &#92;)</span> (time to expiry). In this convention <span>&#92;( &#92;tau = 0 &#92;)</span> corresponds to the expiry date and <span>&#92;( &#92;tau = T &#92;)</span> corresponds to the present. The BS PDE (without dividends) takes the forward-in-time form

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;&#92;tau = &#92;frac{&#92;sigma^2}{2} S^2 V&#95;{SS} + rS V&#95;S - rV,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( V &#92;)</span> is the option value, <span>&#92;( T &#92;)</span> is the expiry time, <span>&#92;( &#92;tau = T - t &#92;)</span> is time to expiry, <span>&#92;( &#92;sigma &#92;)</span> is volatility, <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> is the asset price, and <span>&#92;( r &#92;)</span> is the risk-free interest rate. The function <span>&#92;( V(S, &#92;tau) &#92;)</span> now represents the option value when the time to expiry is <span>&#92;( &#92;tau &#92;)</span> and the underlying price is <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span>. The initial condition (at <span>&#92;( &#92;tau = 0 &#92;)</span>) is <span>&#92;( V(S, 0) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;)</span>, and we march forward in <span>&#92;( &#92;tau &#92;)</span>.

The BS PDE is a *drift-diffusion equation with a decay term*. The three constituent terms have distinct characters. The **diffusion term** <span>&#92;( &#92;frac{&#92;sigma^2}{2} S^2 V&#95;{SS} &#92;)</span> tends to smooth out discontinuities; when this term dominates, the PDE is *parabolic*, and the overall behavior is smooth and well-posed. The **drift term** <span>&#92;( rS V&#95;S &#92;)</span> is a convection term that preserves and transports discontinuities in the solution. The **decay/discounting term** <span>&#92;( -rV &#92;)</span> represents discounting at the risk-free rate.

## Overview of the Numerical PDE Approach

To numerically solve the BS PDE, one follows a systematic procedure. The initial condition is <span>&#92;( V(S, 0) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;)</span>. Because the theoretical domain for <span>&#92;( S &#92;)</span> is <span>&#92;( [0, +&#92;infty) &#92;)</span>, we must use a finite **computational domain** <span>&#92;( [0, S&#95;{\max}] &#92;times [0, T] &#92;)</span> to approximate the infinite domain. Boundary conditions must be imposed at <span>&#92;( S = 0 &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( S = S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span>. The effect of the boundary condition at <span>&#92;( S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span> is small if the initial spot price <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 &#92;)</span> is sufficiently far from the boundary.

The continuous domain is **discretized** by a finite set of grid points <span>&#92;( &#92;{(S&#95;i, &#92;tau^n) : n = 0, &#92;ldots, N; &#92; i = 0, &#92;ldots, M&#92;} &#92;)</span>. The PDE is then discretized at each interior grid point to obtain an algebraic equation that relates values at time level <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^n &#92;)</span> with values at <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^{n+1} &#92;)</span>. As <span>&#92;( N, M &#92;to +&#92;infty &#92;)</span> (the grid becomes finer in both dimensions), the computed values converge to the true PDE solution.

## Discretization Methods

The two main families of **discretization PDE methods** are:

The **finite difference (FD) method** replaces derivatives by finite differences, with error analysis carried out via Taylor series. Standard FD analysis technically requires smooth payoff functions. For the same discrete equations, an alternative **finite element (FE)** analysis allows discontinuous payoffs. In practice, FD and FE methods applied to the BS PDE yield the same discrete equations.

## Finite Difference Approximations and Truncation Error

Assume the function <span>&#92;( f(x) &#92;)</span> is smooth, and let <span>&#92;( f&#95;i &#92;equiv f(x&#95;i) &#92;)</span> with grid spacings <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;equiv x&#95;i - x&#95;{i-1} &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;equiv x&#95;{i+1} - x&#95;i &#92;)</span>. Assume the values <span>&#92;( f&#95;{i-1} &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( f&#95;i &#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;( f&#95;{i+1} &#92;)</span> are known.

### First Derivative Approximations

By Taylor expansion about <span>&#92;( x&#95;i &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
f&#95;{i+1} = f&#95;i + (f&#95;x)&#95;i &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + (f&#95;{xx})&#95;i &#92;frac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}^2}{2} + &#92;cdots
&#92;]</span>

Solving for <span>&#92;( (f&#95;x)&#95;i &#92;)</span> gives the **forward difference** approximation:

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;x)&#95;i = &#92;frac{f&#95;{i+1} - f&#95;i}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}} + O(&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}),
&#92;]</span>

which has **truncation error** <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}) &#92;)</span>, i.e., first order accuracy.

Similarly, from the backward Taylor expansion

<span>&#92;[
f&#95;{i-1} = f&#95;i - (f&#95;x)&#95;i &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} + (f&#95;{xx})&#95;i &#92;frac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}^2}{2} + &#92;cdots,
&#92;]</span>

the **backward difference** approximation is

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;x)&#95;i = &#92;frac{f&#95;i - f&#95;{i-1}}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}} + O(&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}),
&#92;]</span>

again first order. When the grid is uniform with <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} = &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} = &#92;Delta x &#92;)</span>, subtracting the two Taylor expansions and solving yields the **central difference** (second-order) approximation

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;x)&#95;i = &#92;frac{f&#95;{i+1} - f&#95;{i-1}}{2&#92;Delta x} + O(&#92;Delta x^2),
&#92;]</span>

which achieves **second-order accuracy**, a significant improvement. On a non-uniform grid where <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;neq &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;)</span>, the general central difference formula is

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;x)&#95;i = &#92;frac{f&#95;{i+1} - f&#95;{i-1}}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}} - &#92;frac{f&#95;{xx}}{2}(&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} - &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}) + O&#92;!\left(&#92;frac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}^3 + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}^3}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}}&#92;right),
&#92;]</span>

which reduces to the second-order formula on uniform grids.

### Second Derivative Approximations

For the second derivative, the general formula on a non-uniform grid is obtained by combining the forward and backward difference quotients:

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;{xx})&#95;i = &#92;frac{&#92;dfrac{f&#95;{i+1} - f&#95;i}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}} + &#92;dfrac{f&#95;{i-1} - f&#95;i}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}}{&#92;dfrac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}{2}} + O(&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} - &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}) + O&#92;!\left(&#92;frac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}^3}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}&#92;right) + O&#92;!\left(&#92;frac{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}^3}{&#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}&#92;right).
&#92;]</span>

On a uniform grid with <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta x&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} = &#92;Delta x&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} = &#92;Delta x &#92;)</span>, this simplifies to the classical **second-order central** formula

<span>&#92;[
(f&#95;{xx})&#95;i = &#92;frac{f&#95;{i+1} - 2f&#95;i + f&#95;{i-1}}{&#92;Delta x^2} + O(&#92;Delta x^2).
&#92;]</span>

---

# Lecture 16: Implicit Methods, Crank-Nicolson, and Stability

## The BS PDE: Structure of Terms

Working in time-to-expiry form, the BS PDE is

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;&#92;tau = &#92;frac{&#92;sigma^2}{2} S^2 V&#95;{SS} + rS V&#95;S - rV,
&#92;]</span>

which is a *drift-diffusion equation with a decay term*. Recalling the structure of each term: the **diffusion term** <span>&#92;( &#92;frac{&#92;sigma^2}{2} S^2 V&#95;{SS} &#92;)</span> tends to smooth out discontinuities — when diffusion dominates, the PDE is *parabolic* and solutions are smooth. The **drift term** <span>&#92;( rSV&#95;S &#92;)</span> is a convection term that preserves and transports discontinuities in the solution without smoothing them. The **decay/discounting term** <span>&#92;( -rV &#92;)</span> introduces exponential discounting. These three terms together define the character of the numerical approximation problem.

## Overview of PDE Numerical Methods (Recap and Comparison)

Lattice methods are actually simple explicit finite difference methods applied to the BS PDE; they are not very efficient and become very complex for certain non-standard features. Monte Carlo methods are the method of choice when there are more than 3 stochastic factors, but they are generally difficult to handle for American early exercise (the *Longstaff-Schwartz* method is the principal approach for this case), and for 3 or fewer stochastic factors they are not very efficient. Numerical PDE methods are the method of choice for fewer than 3 stochastic factors; complex features including complex American-type contracts are easy to handle; they provide option values and hedging parameters for the whole range of asset prices simultaneously (useful for simulating hedging strategies); they can handle nonlinear PDE problems; and they can be built into general libraries for pricing many different contracts quickly.

The general PDE framework (European options without dividends, in <span>&#92;( &#92;tau &#92;)</span>-form) requires the initial condition <span>&#92;( V(S, 0) = &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S) &#92;)</span>, a finite computational domain <span>&#92;( [0, S&#95;{\max}] &#92;times [0, T] &#92;)</span> as an approximation to <span>&#92;( [0,+&#92;infty) &#92;times [0, T] &#92;)</span>, boundary conditions at <span>&#92;( S = 0 &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( S = S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span>, and a grid <span>&#92;( &#92;{(S&#95;i, &#92;tau^n)&#92;} &#92;)</span> with <span>&#92;( n = 0, &#92;ldots, N &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( i = 0, &#92;ldots, M &#92;)</span>. The boundary effect at <span>&#92;( S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span> is small if <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 &#92;)</span> is sufficiently far from the boundary.

## Finite Difference Approximations for the BS PDE

The finite difference approximations derived in Lecture 15 apply directly to the spatial derivatives in the BS PDE. At grid point <span>&#92;( (S&#95;i, &#92;tau^n) &#92;)</span>, using the central difference for the first spatial derivative and the second-order formula for the second derivative, we write

<span>&#92;[
(V&#95;S)&#95;i^n &#92;approx &#92;frac{V&#95;{i+1}^n - V&#95;{i-1}^n}{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}, &#92;qquad
(V&#95;{SS})&#95;i^n &#92;approx &#92;frac{&#92;dfrac{V&#95;{i+1}^n - V&#95;i^n}{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}}} + &#92;dfrac{V&#95;{i-1}^n - V&#95;i^n}{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}}{&#92;dfrac{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}}}{2}}.
&#92;]</span>

These can be rewritten in the standard **tridiagonal form** by introducing coefficients. Define

<span>&#92;[
&#92;alpha&#95;i = &#92;frac{&#92;sigma&#95;i^2 S&#95;i^2}{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} (&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}})}, &#92;qquad
&#92;beta&#95;i = &#92;frac{&#92;sigma&#95;i^2 S&#95;i^2}{&#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} (&#92;Delta S&#95;{i+&#92;frac{1}{2}} + &#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}})},
&#92;]</span>

so that the discretization of the right-hand side of the BS PDE at grid point <span>&#92;( i &#92;)</span> involves <span>&#92;( V&#95;{i-1} &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( V&#95;i &#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;( V&#95;{i+1} &#92;)</span> with respective coefficients that depend on the choice of approximation for <span>&#92;( V&#95;S &#92;)</span> (central, forward, or backward difference).

### Positive Coefficients Condition and Upwinding

For a finite difference scheme to be **monotone** (and hence non-oscillatory and stable in a strong sense), all off-diagonal coefficients in the tridiagonal system must be non-negative — the so-called **positive coefficients condition**. With a central difference approximation for the drift term <span>&#92;( rSV&#95;S &#92;)</span>, this condition may be violated when the drift dominates the diffusion. The ratio of drift to diffusion is characterized by the **local Peclet number** <span>&#92;( P&#95;i = rS&#95;i &#92;Delta S / (&#92;sigma^2 S&#95;i^2) &#92;)</span>; when <span>&#92;( |P&#95;i| > 1 &#92;)</span> the positive coefficients condition fails and spurious oscillations can appear.

**Upstream weighting** (or *upwinding*) resolves this by choosing the first-order one-sided difference for <span>&#92;( V&#95;S &#92;)</span> in the direction **opposite** to the flow. Since the drift coefficient <span>&#92;( rS &#92;)</span> is positive (for <span>&#92;( r > 0 &#92;)</span>), the solution "flows" to the right in asset-price space, and a **backward (upstream) difference** <span>&#92;( (V&#95;S)&#95;i &#92;approx (V&#95;i - V&#95;{i-1}) / &#92;Delta S&#95;{i-&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;)</span> should be used for the drift term. This choice guarantees positive coefficients at the cost of reducing the spatial accuracy of the drift term to first order; the diffusion term retains second-order accuracy.

## Time Discretization: The Explicit, Implicit, and Crank-Nicolson Methods

### The Explicit Method

The simplest time discretization is the **explicit** (or *forward Euler*) method, which evaluates the right-hand side of the BS PDE at the current known time level <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^n &#92;)</span>. The time derivative is approximated as

<span>&#92;[
&#92;left(&#92;frac{&#92;partial V}{&#92;partial &#92;tau}&#92;right)&#95;i^n &#92;approx &#92;frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{&#92;Delta &#92;tau},
&#92;]</span>

and the spatial terms are evaluated at <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^n &#92;)</span>. This gives an explicit update formula for <span>&#92;( V&#95;i^{n+1} &#92;)</span> directly in terms of already-known values at <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^n &#92;)</span>. The *explicit method* corresponds precisely to the binomial/lattice method in its simplest form.

The explicit method imposes a **stability restriction** on the time step. The stability condition requires that the time step <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;)</span> satisfy

<span>&#92;[
&#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;leq &#92;frac{1}{&#92;alpha&#95;i + &#92;beta&#95;i + r} &#92;quad &#92;text{for all } i,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;( &#92;alpha&#95;i &#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;( &#92;beta&#95;i &#92;)</span> are the diffusion-related coefficients at grid node <span>&#92;( i &#92;)</span>. On a uniform grid in log-price coordinates this becomes the familiar condition <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;leq (&#92;Delta S)^2 / &#92;sigma^2 S^2 &#92;)</span>, which can be extremely restrictive: if the spatial grid is refined by a factor of 2, the time step must be reduced by a factor of 4. This makes the explicit method **conditionally stable** and, for fine grids, computationally expensive because the total number of time steps grows as the square of the number of spatial nodes.

### The Fully Implicit Method

The **fully implicit** method evaluates the right-hand side of the BS PDE at the new (unknown) time level <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^{n+1} &#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{&#92;Delta &#92;tau} = &#92;alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^{n+1} - (&#92;alpha&#95;i + &#92;beta&#95;i + r) V&#95;i^{n+1} + &#92;beta&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^{n+1}.
&#92;]</span>

At each time step, this couples all the unknown values <span>&#92;( V&#95;i^{n+1} &#92;)</span> simultaneously in a **tridiagonal linear system**, which is solved by *LU factorization* (Thomas algorithm) in <span>&#92;( O(M) &#92;)</span> operations per time step. The key advantage is **unconditional stability**: the implicit method is stable for any choice of <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;)</span>, without any restriction relating <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;)</span> to <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta S &#92;)</span>. The time accuracy is first order: truncation error <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta &#92;tau) &#92;)</span> in time.

### The Crank-Nicolson Method

The **Crank-Nicolson** method is a weighted average of the explicit and implicit methods, evaluating the spatial terms at the mid-point in time:

<span>&#92;[
&#92;frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{&#92;Delta &#92;tau} = &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;left[&#92;alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^n - (&#92;alpha&#95;i + &#92;beta&#95;i + r) V&#95;i^n + &#92;beta&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^n&#92;right] + &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;left[&#92;alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^{n+1} - (&#92;alpha&#95;i + &#92;beta&#95;i + r) V&#95;i^{n+1} + &#92;beta&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^{n+1}&#92;right].
&#92;]</span>

This is equivalent to evaluating the right-hand side at the half-time level <span>&#92;( &#92;tau^{n+&#92;frac{1}{2}} &#92;)</span> via the trapezoidal rule. Crank-Nicolson is **unconditionally stable** (like the fully implicit method) and achieves **second-order accuracy in time** <span>&#92;( O(&#92;Delta &#92;tau^2) &#92;)</span>. It also requires solving a tridiagonal system at each time step, with the same <span>&#92;( O(M) &#92;)</span> computational cost. For smooth problems, Crank-Nicolson is generally the preferred method because it combines stability with higher-order accuracy.

## Convergence: The Lax Equivalence Theorem

The fundamental result linking the components of numerical analysis for PDEs is the **Lax equivalence theorem**: for a consistent finite difference scheme applied to a well-posed linear PDE, **stability** is the necessary and sufficient condition for **convergence**. That is,

<span>&#92;[
&#92;text{Convergence} &#92;iff &#92;text{Consistency} + &#92;text{Stability}.
&#92;]</span>

**Consistency** means that the truncation error (the error introduced by replacing continuous derivatives with finite differences at a single grid point) goes to zero as <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau, &#92;Delta S &#92;to 0 &#92;)</span>. All three methods above — explicit, fully implicit, and Crank-Nicolson — are consistent. **Stability** means that errors do not grow without bound as the computation proceeds through many time steps. The explicit method is only **conditionally stable** (requiring the time-step restriction above), while both the fully implicit and Crank-Nicolson methods are **unconditionally stable**.

## Computational Domain and Boundary Conditions

The computational domain is <span>&#92;( [0, S&#95;{\max}] &#92;)</span>. The upper boundary <span>&#92;( S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span> is chosen large enough that boundary errors are negligible, typically

<span>&#92;[
S&#95;{\max} &#92;approx S&#95;0 &#92;exp&#92;!\left(rT + 3&#92;sigma&#92;sqrt{T}&#92;right),
&#92;]</span>

i.e., approximately 3 standard deviations above the forward price — the so-called **3&#92;(\sigma&#92;) truncation**. At <span>&#92;( S = 0 &#92;)</span>, the asset price can never become positive again (it is an absorbing boundary in the GBM model), so for a put the boundary condition is <span>&#92;( V(0, &#92;tau) = Ke^{-r&#92;tau} &#92;)</span> (the present value of receiving the strike) and for a call it is <span>&#92;( V(0, &#92;tau) = 0 &#92;)</span>. At <span>&#92;( S = S&#95;{\max} &#92;)</span>, for a call one typically uses <span>&#92;( V(S&#95;{\max}, &#92;tau) = S&#95;{\max} - Ke^{-r&#92;tau} &#92;)</span> (linear payoff) and for a put <span>&#92;( V(S&#95;{\max}, &#92;tau) = 0 &#92;)</span>. The effect of these boundary conditions on the interior solution at the initial spot price <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 &#92;)</span> is small if <span>&#92;( S&#95;0 &#92;)</span> is sufficiently far from the boundaries.

## Summary: Practical Implementation

Each time step of an implicit or Crank-Nicolson scheme reduces to solving a **tridiagonal linear system** of dimension <span>&#92;( M-1 &#92;)</span> (the number of interior grid points). This system is solved by **LU factorization** (the Thomas algorithm), which requires <span>&#92;( O(M) &#92;)</span> operations. Marching through <span>&#92;( N &#92;)</span> time steps gives a total cost of <span>&#92;( O(MN) &#92;)</span> operations. For American options, at each time step after solving the linear system one also enforces the early exercise constraint by taking <span>&#92;( V&#95;i^{n+1} &#92;leftarrow &#92;max(V&#95;i^{n+1}, &#92;mathrm{payoff}(S&#95;i)) &#92;)</span>, which adds only <span>&#92;( O(M) &#92;)</span> work per time step.

The three methods are summarized as follows. The *explicit method* requires no linear solve but is conditionally stable with restriction <span>&#92;( &#92;Delta &#92;tau &#92;leq 1/(&#92;alpha&#95;i + &#92;beta&#95;i + r) &#92;)</span> and is first order in time. The *fully implicit method* is unconditionally stable and first order in time, requiring one tridiagonal solve per step. The *Crank-Nicolson method* is unconditionally stable and **second order in both time and space**, and is generally the recommended method for option pricing PDE problems.

# Lectures 17–20: PDE Methods for Option Pricing

## Lecture 17: Black-Scholes PDE Discretization

### Overview of the PDE Approach

The Black-Scholes partial differential equation provides a deterministic alternative to Monte Carlo simulation for pricing options. Rather than simulating thousands of stochastic asset paths, the PDE approach discretizes the pricing equation on a fixed spatial-temporal grid and marches backward in time from the known terminal payoff. This lecture develops the finite difference machinery needed to translate the continuous Black-Scholes operator into a computable algebraic scheme.

The **Black-Scholes PDE** written in forward time <span>&#92;(\tau = T - t&#92;)</span>, so that <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span> corresponds to the option expiry, takes the form

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;\tau = \frac{1}{2}\sigma^2 S^2 V&#95;{SS} + rS V&#95;S - rV \equiv \mathcal{L}V.
&#92;]</span>

Denoting the differential operator on the right-hand side as <span>&#92;(\mathcal{L}&#92;)</span>, we seek a numerical scheme that approximates <span>&#92;(V&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}V = 0&#92;)</span> at each interior grid node.

### Discretization Outline

The computational domain is the rectangle <span>&#92;(S \in [0, S&#95;{\max}]&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(\tau \in [0, T]&#92;)</span>. We place <span>&#92;(m+1&#92;)</span> nodes in the asset-price direction at positions <span>&#92;(S_0 < S_1 < \cdots < S_m&#92;)</span> (not necessarily uniform) and advance through <span>&#92;(N&#92;)</span> timesteps <span>&#92;(\tau^0 < \tau^1 < \cdots < \tau^N&#92;)</span>. The discrete option value at node <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span> and time level <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> is written <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^n \approx V(S&#95;i, \tau^n)&#92;)</span>.

Approximating the second-order spatial derivative by the standard three-point central difference and the first-order spatial derivative by one of several choices (central, forward, or backward), the Black-Scholes operator at node <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span> is expressed in terms of coefficients <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i&#92;)</span>, defined such that

<span>&#92;[
(\mathcal{L}V)&#95;i \approx \alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i-1} - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i)V&#95;i + \beta&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}.
&#92;]</span>

The different approximations for the drift term <span>&#92;(rS V&#95;S&#92;)</span> yield different expressions for these coefficients.

### The Three Differencing Choices for the Drift Term

**Central differencing** uses the symmetric approximation

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;S \approx \frac{V&#95;{i+1} - V&#95;{i-1}}{S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1}},
&#92;]</span>

which produces second-order accuracy in <span>&#92;(\Delta S&#92;)</span> but can generate negative coefficients under certain conditions.

The central-difference coefficients are

<span>&#92;[
\alpha&#95;i^{central} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;i - S&#95;{i-1})(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})} - \frac{rS&#95;i}{S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1}},
\quad
\beta&#95;i^{central} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;i)(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})} + \frac{rS&#95;i}{S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1}}.
&#92;]</span>

**Forward differencing** replaces <span>&#92;(V&#95;S&#92;)</span> with

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;S \approx \frac{V&#95;{i+1} - V&#95;i}{S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;i},
&#92;]</span>

yielding the coefficient pair

<span>&#92;[
\alpha&#95;i^{forward} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;i - S&#95;{i-1})(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})},
\quad
\beta&#95;i^{forward} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;i)(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})} + \frac{rS&#95;i}{S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;i}.
&#92;]</span>

Because all terms are non-negative (for <span>&#92;(r > 0&#92;)</span>), forward differencing always satisfies <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0&#92;)</span>.

**Backward differencing** approximates

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;S \approx \frac{V&#95;i - V&#95;{i-1}}{S&#95;i - S&#95;{i-1}},
&#92;]</span>

giving

<span>&#92;[
\alpha&#95;i^{backward} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;i - S&#95;{i-1})(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})} - \frac{rS&#95;i}{S&#95;i - S&#95;{i-1}},
\quad
\beta&#95;i^{backward} = \frac{\sigma^2 S&#95;i^2}{(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;i)(S&#95;{i+1} - S&#95;{i-1})}.
&#92;]</span>

### Boundary Conditions

At <span>&#92;(S = 0&#92;)</span> the Black-Scholes equation degenerates, and the boundary value is set analytically: for a put, <span>&#92;(V(0, \tau) = K e^{-r\tau}&#92;)</span>; for a call, <span>&#92;(V(0, \tau) = 0&#92;)</span>. At the upper boundary <span>&#92;(S = S&#95;{\max}&#92;)</span>, a linear far-field condition is imposed, freezing the second derivative to zero so that <span>&#92;(\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial S^2} = 0&#92;)</span> at <span>&#92;(S&#95;{\max}&#92;)</span>. For a call this gives <span>&#92;(V(S&#95;{\max}, \tau) = S&#95;{\max} - Ke^{-r\tau}&#92;)</span>, and for a put <span>&#92;(V(S&#95;{\max}, \tau) = 0&#92;)</span>. At the initial time <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span> the terminal payoff sets the initial condition: <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^0 = \max(S&#95;i - K, 0)&#92;)</span> for a call, <span>&#92;(\max(K - S&#95;i, 0)&#92;)</span> for a put.

### The Explicit Method

The simplest time-stepping scheme evaluates all spatial derivatives at the known time level <span>&#92;(\tau^n&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
\frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{\Delta\tau} = \alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^n - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i) V&#95;i^n + \beta&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^n.
&#92;]</span>

Rearranging, the update formula is

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;i^{n+1} = V&#95;i^n\bigl(1 - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)\Delta\tau\bigr) + V&#95;{i-1}^n \,\alpha&#95;i \Delta\tau + V&#95;{i+1}^n \,\beta&#95;i \Delta\tau. \tag{19}
&#92;]</span>

This is an **explicit** or *forward Euler* scheme: each updated value <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^{n+1}&#92;)</span> is a linear combination of three values at the previous time level, requiring no matrix solve. The computational cost per step is <span>&#92;(O(m)&#92;)</span>.

### A Pathological Example: The Supershare Option

To illustrate why the choice of differencing matters deeply, consider the **supershare option**, whose payoff is

<span>&#92;[
V(S, 0) = \frac{1}{K&#95;d}\mathbf{1}&#95;{K&#95;u \leq S \leq K&#95;d},
&#92;]</span>

a binary function that is zero everywhere except on the interval <span>&#92;([K&#95;u, K&#95;d]&#92;)</span>. When the central difference scheme is applied to such a payoff on a coarse grid, the computed option values at nearby nodes become negative — physically nonsensical, since an option cannot have negative value. This spurious oscillation arises because the central-difference <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{central}&#92;)</span> can be negative, allowing sign oscillations to propagate. The forward-difference scheme eliminates these oscillations because both <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{forward}&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i^{forward}&#92;)</span> are non-negative, so the explicit update formula (19) computes <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^{n+1}&#92;)</span> as a positive combination of non-negative values, guaranteeing non-negative option prices throughout the computation.

### Positive Coefficient Discretization

Examining equation (19) carefully, we see that the coefficients multiplying <span>&#92;(V&#95;{i-1}^n&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^n&#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;(V&#95;{i+1}^n&#92;)</span> on the right-hand side must all be non-negative for the scheme to be a **positive coefficient discretization**. The coefficient of <span>&#92;(V&#95;i^n&#92;)</span> is <span>&#92;(1 - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)\Delta\tau&#92;)</span>, which requires

<span>&#92;[
\Delta\tau \leq \frac{1}{\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r}, \quad \forall\, i.
&#92;]</span>

The coefficients of <span>&#92;(V&#95;{i-1}^n&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(V&#95;{i+1}^n&#92;)</span> are <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i \Delta\tau&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i \Delta\tau&#92;)</span> respectively, which are non-negative if and only if <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span>. This mathematical requirement proves essential: it is both a necessary condition for stability and a guarantee that the discrete solution remains non-oscillatory.

### Upstream Weighting

For the standard Black-Scholes equation with <span>&#92;(r > 0&#92;)</span>, forward differencing always guarantees <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0&#92;)</span>, so forward differencing alone suffices. However, in more general pricing problems — for instance, interest-rate derivative models where the drift coefficient may change sign — neither central nor forward differencing alone guarantees non-negative coefficients at every node. The systematic solution is **upstream weighting**.

The *upstream weighting* scheme first computes <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{ups}&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i^{ups}&#92;)</span> by testing whether the forward-difference coefficients are already non-negative:

<span>&#92;[
\text{If } \bigl(\alpha&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0 \text{ and } \beta&#95;i^{forward} \geq 0\bigr) \text{ then }
\alpha&#95;i^{ups} = \alpha&#95;i^{forward},\; \beta&#95;i^{ups} = \beta&#95;i^{forward};
\text{ else }
\alpha&#95;i^{ups} = \alpha&#95;i^{backward},\; \beta&#95;i^{ups} = \beta&#95;i^{backward}. \tag{22}
&#92;]</span>

It can be verified that this selection always yields <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i^{ups} \geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i^{ups} \geq 0&#92;)</span>. Since central weighting is second-order accurate while upstream weighting is only first-order, in practice a **preprocessing step** selects central differencing whenever it already satisfies the non-negativity requirement, and falls back to upstream weighting only when necessary:

<span>&#92;[
\text{For each } i: \quad
\text{If } \bigl(\alpha&#95;i^{central} \geq 0 \text{ and } \beta&#95;i^{central} \geq 0\bigr) \text{ then use central; else use upstream.} \tag{23}
&#92;]</span>

For the simple Black-Scholes equation (with <span>&#92;(r > 0&#92;)</span> and a non-negative asset price), the preprocessing step reduces to always using forward differencing everywhere, since forward differencing satisfies the non-negativity requirement at every node. In all subsequent analysis we assume this preprocessing step has been applied, so that <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span> everywhere.

---

## Lecture 18: Implicit Methods and the Matrix Formulation

### Computational Domain and Choice of S-max

Before assembling any scheme, one must specify the size of the computational domain. The choice of <span>&#92;(S&#95;{\max}&#92;)</span> is a practical compromise: it must be large enough that the boundary condition imposed there is accurate, yet not so large that the grid resolution near the strike price is wasted. A common heuristic is <span>&#92;(S&#95;{\max} = 4K&#92;)</span> to <span>&#92;(10K&#92;)</span>, with the upper bound chosen so that the far-field boundary condition error is negligible relative to the desired solution accuracy.

### Limitations of the Explicit Method

Although the explicit scheme is easy to implement, it imposes a severe **stability restriction** on the allowed timestep. For the Black-Scholes equation discretized on a grid with small spacing near the strike, the stability condition <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau \leq 1/(\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)&#92;)</span> can force extremely small timesteps. On a uniform grid with spacing <span>&#92;(\Delta S&#92;)</span>, the dominant diffusion coefficient scales as <span>&#92;(\sigma^2 S^2 / (\Delta S)^2&#92;)</span>, and so the stability condition becomes approximately <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau \lesssim (\Delta S)^2 / (\sigma^2 S^2)&#92;)</span>. Halving the spatial grid requires quadrupling the number of timesteps, making the explicit method computationally expensive for high-accuracy work.

### The Fully Implicit Method

The **fully implicit** (or *backward Euler*) scheme evaluates all spatial derivatives at the unknown time level <span>&#92;(\tau^{n+1}&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
\frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{\Delta\tau} = \alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^{n+1} - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i) V&#95;i^{n+1} + \beta&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^{n+1} - r V&#95;i^{n+1}.
&#92;]</span>

Rearranging, we obtain a tridiagonal linear system for the unknowns <span>&#92;(V^{n+1}&#92;)</span> at each timestep. In matrix notation with <span>&#92;(M^n&#92;)</span> denoting the discretization matrix of the operator <span>&#92;(\mathcal{L}&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[
(I + M^n) V^{n+1} = V^n.
&#92;]</span>

This system has a unique solution for any <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau > 0&#92;)</span> because <span>I + M^n&#92;)</span> is strictly diagonally dominant whenever <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i, \beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span>. The fully implicit method is **unconditionally stable** — no restriction on <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau&#92;)</span> is needed. The penalty is that it is only first-order accurate in time, <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau)&#92;)</span>, while remaining second-order in space, <span>&#92;(O((\Delta S)^2)&#92;)</span>.

### Crank-Nicolson

The **Crank-Nicolson** method averages the explicit and implicit evaluations at half the weight each:

<span>&#92;[
\frac{V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n}{\Delta\tau} = \frac{1}{2}\Bigl[\bigl(\alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^n - (\alpha&#95;i+\beta&#95;i+r)V&#95;i^n + \beta&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^n\bigr) + \bigl(\alpha&#95;i V&#95;{i-1}^{n+1} - (\alpha&#95;i+\beta&#95;i+r)V&#95;i^{n+1} + \beta&#95;i V&#95;{i+1}^{n+1}\bigr)\Bigr].
&#92;]</span>

Defining the half-step matrix <span>&#92;(\hat{M}^n = M^n / 2&#92;)</span>, the matrix form of Crank-Nicolson is

<span>&#92;[
(I + \hat{M}^n) V^{n+1} = (I - \hat{M}^n) V^n.
&#92;]</span>

Crank-Nicolson is **second-order accurate** in both time and space, <span>&#92;(O((\Delta\tau)^2, (\Delta S)^2)&#92;)</span>, and is unconditionally stable. However, as we shall see in the stability analysis, it does not always produce positive coefficients unless the timestep is sufficiently small.

### Local Volatility Function Models

The framework extends directly to **local volatility function (LVF)** models, where the volatility depends on both the asset price and time: <span>&#92;(\sigma = \sigma(S, t)&#92;)</span>. In this case the coefficients <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i&#92;)</span> vary with the time level <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span>, so the matrix <span>&#92;(M&#92;)</span> must be recomputed at each step. The implicit system at each timestep is still tridiagonal and the computational structure is unchanged; only the coefficients differ from step to step. This makes the LVF model only modestly more expensive than the constant-volatility Black-Scholes model.

### LU Decomposition for Tridiagonal Systems

Both the implicit and Crank-Nicolson methods require solving a tridiagonal linear system at every timestep. The standard algorithm is **LU decomposition** specialized to tridiagonal form. For a system of size <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span>, the decomposition requires <span>&#92;(O(m)&#92;)</span> operations and <span>&#92;(O(m)&#92;)</span> storage. The tridiagonal LU factorization is stable whenever the matrix is diagonally dominant, which is guaranteed by the positive-coefficient requirement. Because the tridiagonal structure is fixed across timesteps (for constant-volatility models), the sparsity pattern of the factorization need only be determined once, and back-substitution at each step costs <span>&#92;(O(m)&#92;)</span>.

### Convergence: Consistency and Stability

A finite difference method is **convergent** if

<span>&#92;[
\lim&#95;{\Delta S, \Delta\tau \to 0} (V&#95;i^n - V(S&#95;i, \tau^n)) = 0 \quad \text{for all } 0 \leq i \leq N,\; 0 \leq j \leq m.
&#92;]</span>

Convergence depends on two independent properties: **local truncation error** (consistency) and **stability**.

The **truncation error** of a discretization of <span>&#92;(V&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}V = 0&#92;)</span> is defined by substituting a smooth test function <span>&#92;(\psi(S, \tau)&#92;)</span> (with bounded derivatives) into the discrete scheme in place of the grid values. The T.E. measures how much the scheme fails to satisfy the PDE exactly:

<span>&#92;[
T.E. = \bigl[\{(\psi&#95;\tau)&#95;i^n - (\mathcal{L}\psi)&#95;i^n\}&#95;{discrete}\bigr] - \bigl[\{\psi&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}\psi\}&#95;i^n\bigr].
&#92;]</span>

A scheme is **consistent** if the truncation error tends to zero as <span>&#92;(\Delta S, \Delta\tau \to 0&#92;)</span>. For the explicit and implicit methods the truncation error is <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau, (\Delta S)^2)&#92;)</span>; for Crank-Nicolson it is <span>&#92;(O((\Delta\tau)^2, (\Delta S)^2)&#92;)</span>.

A method is **stable** if the **amplification** factor in error propagation is no larger than one — equivalently, if the discrete solution remains bounded as the number of timesteps grows. Stability, combined with consistency, is both necessary and sufficient for convergence (this is the Lax equivalence theorem).

---

## Lecture 19: Stability Analysis, Upstream Weighting, and Rannacher Timestepping

### Stability of the Explicit Finite Difference Method

To analyze stability of the explicit scheme for the Black-Scholes equation, note that the boundary at <span>&#92;(S&#95;{\max}&#92;)</span> satisfies <span>&#92;(V&#95;m^{n+1} = V&#95;m^n&#92;)</span> (Dirichlet condition), and we assume the preprocessing step ensures <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(\beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span> for all interior nodes. From the update equation, taking absolute values and exploiting non-negativity of the coefficients:

<span>&#92;[
