|V&#95;i^{n+1}| \leq |V&#95;i^n| \,\bigl(1 - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)\Delta\tau\bigr) + |V&#95;{i-1}^n| \,\alpha&#95;i \Delta\tau + |V&#95;{i+1}^n| \,\beta&#95;i \Delta\tau.
&#92;]</span>

This is valid only when <span>&#92;(1 - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)\Delta\tau \geq 0&#92;)</span> for every <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span>. Defining the infinity norm <span>&#92;(\|V^n\|&#95;\infty = \max&#95;i |V&#95;i^n|&#92;)</span>, we can bound:

<span>&#92;[
|V&#95;i^{n+1}| \leq \|V^n\|&#95;\infty \bigl[(1 - (\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)\Delta\tau) + \alpha&#95;i \Delta\tau + \beta&#95;i \Delta\tau\bigr] = \|V^n\|&#95;\infty (1 - r\Delta\tau) \leq \|V^n\|&#95;\infty.
&#92;]</span>

Since this holds for all interior nodes <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span>, and trivially at the boundaries, we conclude

<span>&#92;[
\|V^{n+1}\|&#95;\infty \leq \|V^n\|&#95;\infty. \tag{14}
&#92;]</span>

As long as <span>&#92;(\|V^0\|&#95;\infty&#92;)</span> is bounded (the initial payoff is bounded), the discrete solution remains bounded as <span>&#92;(n \to \infty&#92;)</span>. In other words, the discretization is *stable*. The key conclusion is:

**The explicit method is stable if and only if**

<span>&#92;[
\Delta\tau \leq \frac{1}{\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r}, \quad \forall\, i.
&#92;]</span>

This condition is both **necessary and sufficient** for stability (given positive coefficients). The necessity is illustrated dramatically by a numerical example for a heat equation: with <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau / (\Delta x)^2 = 0.5&#92;)</span> the solution is accurate (values 0.4419 and 0.1607 matching the exact 0.4420 and 0.1606), but with <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau / (\Delta x)^2 = 0.52&#92;)</span> the solution explodes catastrophically (values 625.0347 and -457.3122).

### Stability of the Implicit Methods

The fully implicit method is **unconditionally stable**. To see this, note that the implicit system at each step is <span>&#92;((I + M^n)V^{n+1} = V^n&#92;)</span>. When <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i, \beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span>, the matrix <span>&#92;(I + M^n&#92;)</span> has the M-matrix property: all diagonal entries are positive, all off-diagonal entries are non-positive, and the matrix is strictly diagonally dominant. An infinity-norm argument analogous to the explicit case shows that

<span>&#92;[
\|V^{n+1}\|&#95;\infty \leq \|V^n\|&#95;\infty
&#92;]</span>

for any <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau > 0&#92;)</span>. The implicit method always has positive coefficients (for any <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau&#92;)</span>) whenever <span>&#92;(\alpha&#95;i, \beta&#95;i \geq 0&#92;)</span>.

**Crank-Nicolson** is also unconditionally stable in the sense that the amplification factor does not exceed one for any <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau&#92;)</span>. However, Crank-Nicolson has positive coefficients in the updated solution only if the timestep satisfies

<span>&#92;[
\Delta\tau \leq \frac{2}{\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r},
&#92;]</span>

which is twice as large as the explicit stability constraint. When this condition is violated, Crank-Nicolson may produce mild oscillations in the numerical solution, particularly near discontinuities in the payoff or its derivatives.

### Convergence Rates

The convergence rate of a consistent, stable scheme is determined by its local truncation error. For the explicit and fully implicit methods:

<span>&#92;[
\text{Error} = O(\Delta\tau) + O((\Delta S)^2).
&#92;]</span>

For Crank-Nicolson:

<span>&#92;[
\text{Error} = O((\Delta\tau)^2) + O((\Delta S)^2).
&#92;]</span>

Crank-Nicolson is therefore potentially much more accurate per unit of computational work, since halving <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau&#92;)</span> reduces the time error by a factor of four rather than two.

### The Problem with Non-Smooth Payoffs: Why Second-Order Convergence Fails

Standard put and call payoffs have a kink in the first derivative at the strike price <span>&#92;(S = K&#92;)</span>. Digital options have a jump discontinuity in the payoff itself. These nonsmooth initial conditions produce rapidly varying solutions near <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span>, and the high-frequency components excited by the discontinuity interact with Crank-Nicolson's time-averaging in a way that introduces oscillations and degrades convergence.

Numerically, standard Crank-Nicolson applied to a European put with <span>&#92;(T = 0.25&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(K = 100&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(r = 0.10&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(S = 100&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(\sigma = 0.8&#92;)</span> (exact value 14.45191) shows erratic convergence ratios (4.3, 2.2, 2.1) rather than the expected ratio of 4.0. Plots of the computed gamma (second derivative of option price with respect to <span>&#92;(S&#92;)</span>) under standard Crank-Nicolson show spurious oscillations localized near <span>&#92;(S = K&#92;)</span>; the delta plot shows a visible kink. These artifacts arise because the nonsmooth payoff excites oscillatory Fourier modes that standard C-N does not adequately damp.

### Rannacher Timestepping

The cure, proposed by *Rannacher (1984)*, is elegant: replace the first two timesteps (those immediately adjacent to the initial rough payoff) with **fully implicit** steps, and then switch to Crank-Nicolson for all subsequent steps. This procedure damps the high-frequency oscillatory modes excited by the nonsmooth initial data without sacrificing the overall second-order convergence rate. Formally:

<span>&#92;[
\text{Steps 1 and 2: } (I + M)V^{n+1} = V^n \quad (\text{fully implicit}).
&#92;]</span>
<span>&#92;[
\text{Steps 3, 4, \ldots, N: } (I + \hat{M})V^{n+1} = (I - \hat{M})V^n \quad (\text{Crank-Nicolson}).
&#92;]</span>

Numerical experiments confirm that *Rannacher smoothing* restores second-order convergence. With the same European put test case, the Rannacher-smoothed C-N produces convergence ratios of precisely 4.0 at each refinement level (errors 0.02485, 0.00625, 0.00156, 0.00039 — each approximately one-quarter of the previous). The gamma plot becomes smooth and free of oscillations.

For **discontinuous payoffs** (such as digitals), explicit payoff smoothing of the initial condition may also be needed in addition to Rannacher timestepping. For continuous but non-smooth payoffs (ordinary puts and calls), Rannacher timestepping alone suffices, provided there is a grid node placed exactly at <span>&#92;(S = K&#92;)</span>.

### Timestep Selection

Constant timesteps are simple but suboptimal. Near <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span> the solution changes rapidly (due to the rough payoff), requiring small steps for accuracy; at later times the solution is smooth and large steps suffice. A **variable timestep selector** exploits this by choosing the next timestep <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau^{n+2}&#92;)</span> based on the observed change over the current step:

<span>&#92;[
\Delta\tau^{n+2} = \left[\frac{\texttt{dnorm}}{\texttt{MaxRelChange}}\right] \Delta\tau^{n+1}, \tag{3}
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(\texttt{dnorm}&#92;)</span> is a user-specified target relative change and

<span>&#92;[
\texttt{MaxRelChange} = \max&#95;i \left[ \frac{|V(S&#95;i, \tau^n + \Delta\tau^{n+1}) - V(S&#95;i, \tau^n)|}{\max(D, |V(S&#95;i, \tau^n + \Delta\tau^{n+1})|, |V(S&#95;i, \tau^n)|)} \right].
&#92;]</span>

The scale factor <span>&#92;(D&#92;)</span> (set to 1 for dollar-denominated options) prevents artificially small timesteps when the option value is near zero. The ratio <span>&#92;(\texttt{MaxRelChange} / \Delta\tau^{n+1}&#92;)</span> approximates the relative time derivative of <span>&#92;(V&#92;)</span>, and the selector chooses <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau^{n+2}&#92;)</span> so that this approximate derivative times the new timestep equals the target constant. A typical value <span>&#92;(\texttt{dnorm} = 0.05&#92;)</span> ensures the maximum relative change per step is about 5%. It can be shown that as <span>&#92;(\texttt{dnorm} \to 0&#92;)</span> the truncation error tends to zero. For convergence-rate testing, <span>&#92;(\texttt{dnorm}&#92;)</span> must be halved whenever <span>&#92;(\Delta S&#92;)</span> is halved.

### Summary for Lecture 19

The key takeaways from stability and convergence analysis are as follows. For the standard Black-Scholes equation, the fully implicit method always possesses positive coefficients, ensuring stability for any timestep; it is first-order in time. The Crank-Nicolson method achieves second-order convergence but only guarantees positive coefficients when <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau \leq 2/(\alpha&#95;i + \beta&#95;i + r)&#92;)</span>. Non-smooth payoffs cause oscillations and slow convergence in C-N; the cure is two fully implicit steps (Rannacher timestepping) followed by C-N, which restores second-order convergence. The variable timestep selector, which takes small steps when the solution changes rapidly and large steps when it changes slowly, is the recommended approach for production computations.

---

## Lecture 20: American Options and the Linear Complementarity Problem

### The Early Exercise Problem

A **American option** grants its holder the right to exercise at any time before expiry. This feature introduces a free boundary: there exists a critical asset price <span>&#92;(S^&#42;(\tau)&#92;)</span> (the early exercise boundary) below which it is optimal to exercise a put immediately, receiving the intrinsic value <span>&#92;(K - S&#92;)</span> rather than holding the option. The PDE framework must be modified to incorporate this constraint.

The correct mathematical formulation replaces the Black-Scholes PDE with a **partial differential complementarity problem**. At every point <span>&#92;((S, \tau)&#92;)</span> in the domain, the option value <span>&#92;(V(S, \tau)&#92;)</span> must simultaneously satisfy three conditions:

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}V \geq 0, \quad V \geq V^&#42; \equiv \max(K - S, 0), \quad (V&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}V)(V - V^&#42;) = 0.
&#92;]</span>

The first condition says the PDE residual is non-negative (the option is never worth holding if it would violate the PDE). The second is the early exercise constraint: the option value always exceeds the intrinsic value. The third (complementarity) condition enforces that at each point exactly one of the other two holds with equality: either the PDE is satisfied exactly (and <span>&#92;(V > V^&#42;&#92;)</span>), or the option is exercised (and <span>&#92;(V = V^&#42;&#92;)</span>).

### Matrix Form and the Discrete LCP

After discretizing in space with the positive-coefficient preprocessing step applied, the system at each timestep can be written in matrix form. For the explicit scheme:

<span>&#92;[
\hat{V} = (I - M^n \Delta\tau) V^n,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(\hat{V}&#92;)</span> is the tentative value before the constraint is applied. For the implicit and Crank-Nicolson schemes, the matrix equation (before applying the constraint) is

<span>&#92;[
A V^{n+1} = B V^n,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(A = I + M^n&#92;)</span> for implicit and <span>&#92;(A = I + \hat{M}^n&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(B = I - \hat{M}^n&#92;)</span> for C-N. Incorporating the discrete early exercise constraint produces the **Linear Complementarity Problem (LCP)**:

<span>&#92;[
A V^{n+1} \geq B V^n, \quad V^{n+1} \geq V^&#42;, \quad (A V^{n+1} - B V^n)^T (V^{n+1} - V^&#42;) = 0.
&#92;]</span>

### Explicit Evaluation of the American Constraint

The simplest approach to enforcing the early exercise constraint is the **explicit** (or *projected*) method: first solve the unconstrained system for a trial value <span>&#92;(\hat{V}^{n+1}&#92;)</span>, then apply the constraint node by node:

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;i^{n+1} = \max\bigl(\hat{V}&#95;i^{n+1},\; V&#95;i^&#42;\bigr).
&#92;]</span>

For the fully explicit scheme this requires no additional linear-algebra work — the trial value is already available as a vector of node updates. For implicit or C-N, one solves the unconstrained tridiagonal system and then applies the projection.

The drawback is accuracy. Applying the constraint explicitly (after the solve) is only **first-order accurate in time** for American options, even when Crank-Nicolson is used for the European part of the computation. The early exercise boundary contributes a source of first-order error that prevents the overall scheme from achieving its nominal second-order rate. Convergence tables for an American put (<span>&#92;(T = 0.25&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(r = 0.10&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(K = 100&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(S = 100&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(\sigma = 0.2&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(\sigma = 0.8&#92;)</span>) with constant timesteps demonstrate convergence ratios of approximately 2.5, confirming the <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau)&#92;)</span> rate regardless of whether C-N or implicit is used.

### Implicit LCP Methods

To recover second-order convergence for American options, one must treat the constraint **implicitly** — that is, enforce it simultaneously with the time step, so that the free boundary position at time <span>&#92;(\tau^{n+1}&#92;)</span> is determined as part of the solution at step <span>&#92;(n+1&#92;)</span>. Several methods exist:

**Projected Successive Over-Relaxation (PSOR)** solves the LCP iteratively by applying SOR updates node by node while enforcing the constraint at each node after each update. It converges but can be slow and requires careful tuning of the relaxation parameter.

The **UL method** (Brennan-Schwartz algorithm) exploits the tridiagonal structure of the LCP. It performs a forward sweep (upper-lower decomposition) and applies the constraint during back-substitution. While elegant, it requires careful ordering and may not extend cleanly to multi-factor problems.

**Newton-type** methods linearize the nonlinear LCP at each iteration using the current iterate to identify the active set (nodes where the constraint is binding).

### The Penalty Method

The most practical implicit method for general use is the **penalty method** of Forsyth and Vetzal (2002). The idea is to replace the LCP with a nonlinear PDE by adding a penalty term that becomes large whenever the option value drops below the early exercise value:

<span>&#92;[
V&#95;\tau - \mathcal{L}V + \rho \max(V^&#42; - V, 0) = 0,
&#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(\rho&#92;)</span> is a large positive **penalty parameter**. When <span>&#92;(V > V^&#42;&#92;)</span>, the penalty term is zero and the equation reduces to the Black-Scholes PDE. When <span>&#92;(V < V^*&#92;)</span>, the large penalty term forces <span>&#92;(V&#92;)</span> back toward <span>&#92;(V^&#42;&#92;)</span>. As <span>&#92;(\rho \to \infty&#92;)</span>, the solution of the penalty equation converges to the true American option value. In practice, <span>&#92;(\rho \approx 1/\text{tol}&#92;)</span>, where tol is the desired solution tolerance.

After discretization in time (using implicit or C-N), the penalty equation becomes a **nonlinear tridiagonal system** at each step. This is solved iteratively by **Newton's method** or a fixed-point iteration. The algorithm at each timestep is:

1. Initialize <span>&#92;(V^{n+1, (0)} = V^n&#92;)</span>.
2. For <span>&#92;(k = 0, 1, 2, \ldots&#92;)</span>: compute the penalty term at the current iterate, assemble the modified tridiagonal system, and solve for <span>&#92;(V^{n+1, (k+1)}&#92;)</span>.
3. Stop when <span>&#92;(\|V^{n+1, (k+1)} - V^{n+1, (k)}\|_\infty < \text{tol}&#92;)</span>.

The extraordinary property of the penalty method is that it converges in a **constant number of nonlinear iterations per timestep**, independent of grid refinement (<span>&#92;(\Delta S \to 0&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(\Delta\tau \to 0&#92;)</span>). Experiments consistently show approximately 2.2 to 2.3 iterations per timestep, confirming **quadratic (Newton-like) convergence** of the nonlinear iteration. This constant iteration count means the total computational work scales as <span>&#92;(O(m \cdot N)&#92;)</span> — the same asymptotic complexity as a European option solve. The penalty method is also straightforward to generalize to multi-factor problems where the tridiagonal structure is replaced by sparse matrices solvable with standard software.

### Convergence of American Option Schemes

With **constant timesteps** and the implicit constraint:

- The explicit-constraint method converges at rate <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau)&#92;)</span> (linear convergence). Convergence ratios of approximately 2.5 are observed in practice.
- The implicit-constraint penalty method also does not achieve quadratic convergence with constant timesteps, because the early exercise boundary near <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span> causes the solution to change very rapidly.

Near the initial time <span>&#92;(\tau = T - t = 0&#92;)</span>, theoretical analysis shows that the early exercise boundary moves very rapidly, causing large changes in the solution on small timescales. If instead the timestep is chosen so that

<span>&#92;[
\max&#95;i |V&#95;i^{n+1} - V&#95;i^n| \simeq d
&#92;]</span>

for some fixed constant <span>&#92;(d&#92;)</span>, then quadratic convergence (error <span>&#92;(O(d^2)&#92;)</span>) can be achieved. The variable timestep selector described in Lecture 19 achieves precisely this: it takes small steps near <span>&#92;(\tau = 0&#92;)</span> (where the solution changes rapidly) and large steps later (where the solution is smooth).

Numerical experiments with the variable timestep selector (halving dnorm at each grid refinement) confirm:

- **Explicit constraint + variable timestep**: linear convergence ratios near 2.0–2.1 (still <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau)&#92;)</span>).
- **Implicit constraint (penalty) + variable timestep**: quadratic convergence ratios near 4.0–4.5, confirming <span>&#92;(O((\Delta\tau)^2)&#92;)</span>.

For <span>&#92;(\sigma = 0.2&#92;)</span> with implicit constraint and variable timestep, the convergence table shows errors of 0.00464, 0.00108, 0.00027, 0.00006 — ratios of approximately 4.3, 4.0, 4.5 — compelling evidence for quadratic convergence. For <span>&#92;(\sigma = 0.8&#92;)</span> the same pattern holds.

Plots of the **delta** (first derivative of value with respect to <span>&#92;(S&#92;)</span>) for an American put illustrate an important feature: with Crank-Nicolson (implicit constraint), the delta is smooth everywhere except at the early exercise boundary where the put transitions from zero gamma to positive gamma; with explicit constraint, the delta shows a small discontinuity at the exercise boundary because the constraint is not enforced smoothly. The fully implicit scheme produces a smooth delta profile.

### Summary: American Options

The final summary of the American option analysis is as follows. Applying the early exercise constraint **explicitly** (projecting after each linear solve) is simple but limits convergence to <span>&#92;(O(\Delta\tau)&#92;)</span), making Crank-Nicolson's second-order temporal accuracy useless. Applying the constraint **implicitly** (via the penalty method or other implicit LCP solvers) requires solving a nonlinear system at each step, but the penalty method converges in a constant number of iterations. To achieve quadratic convergence with the implicit constraint, the **variable timestep selector** is essential — constant timesteps fail to capture the rapidly moving free boundary near expiry. The combination of implicit constraint enforcement (penalty method), Rannacher smoothing, and the variable timestep selector constitutes a complete, production-quality finite difference solver for American options.

The penalty method has several further advantages. Convergence is rigorously established. The number of nonlinear iterations per timestep is bounded independently of grid refinement. It generalizes straightforwardly to multi-factor problems using standard sparse matrix software. These properties make it the recommended approach for American option pricing in practice.

# Lecture 21a: Convex Optimization

## Introduction and Motivation

This lecture provides a self-contained introduction to convex optimization, drawing on the standard reference Boyd and Vandenberghe, *Convex Optimization* (available at https://stanford.edu/~boyd/cvxbook). The topics covered are convex optimization problems, convex sets, convex functions, methods for establishing convexity, operations that preserve convexity, and the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) optimality conditions. These ideas underpin the efficient solution of portfolio optimization problems such as minimizing CVaR, which will be treated in Lecture 21b.

## Convex Optimization Problems

**Convex optimization** is the task of minimizing a convex function, or equivalently maximizing a concave function, over a convex feasible set. The general nonlinearly constrained minimization problem — the **primal problem** — has the form

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{x} \; f(x) \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad g&#95;i(x) &#92;leq 0,\; i=1,&#92;ldots,m, \quad q&#95;j(x) = 0,\; j=1,&#92;ldots,l \tag{1} &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x)&#92;)</span> are assumed to be continuously differentiable. A point satisfying all constraints is called **feasible**. The problem (1) is a **convex optimization** problem precisely when <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span> and each inequality constraint function <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> are convex and each equality constraint function <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x)&#92;)</span> is linear. Under these conditions, the feasible set of (1) is itself convex. Maximizing a concave function over a convex set is an equivalent way to formulate convex optimization. A key and practically important fact is that for a convex optimization problem, it is possible to compute a **global minimizer** using efficient algorithms. By contrast, for a nonconvex problem one can generally expect to compute only a local minimizer at best.

The distinction between local and global minimizers matters greatly in practice. A **local minimizer** <span>&#92;(x&#95;&#42;&#92;)</span> satisfies <span>&#92;(f(x&#95;&#42;) &#92;leq f(x)&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> with <span>&#92;(&#92;|x - x&#95;&#42;&#92;| &#92;leq &#92;delta&#92;)</span> for some <span>&#92;(&#92;delta > 0&#92;)</span>. A **global minimizer** satisfies <span>&#92;(f(x&#95;&#42;) &#92;leq f(x)&#92;)</span> for all feasible <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>. For convex problems local minimizers are global minimizers, which is the central computational advantage of convexity.

## Convex Sets

A set <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{D}&#92;)</span> is **convex** if the line segment connecting any two points in <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{D}&#92;)</span> lies entirely within <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{D}&#92;)</span>. Formally, for any <span>&#92;(x&#95;1, x&#95;2 &#92;in &#92;mathcal{D}&#92;)</span> and any <span>&#92;(0 &#92;leq &#92;theta &#92;leq 1&#92;)</span>,

<span>&#92;[ &#92;theta x&#95;1 + (1-&#92;theta)x&#95;2 &#92;in &#92;mathcal{D}. &#92;]</span>

Standard examples of convex sets include <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span> itself, the nonnegative orthant <span>&#92;(\{x : x > 0\}&#92;)</span>, and the Euclidean ball <span>&#92;(\{x : &#92;|x&#92;|&#95;2 &#92;leq 1\}&#92;)</span>. The set <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{D} = [1,2] &#92;cup [4,5]&#92;)</span>, on the other hand, is not convex because a point between the two intervals — say, <span>&#92;(3&#92;)</span> — lies outside the set even though it lies on a segment connecting a point in <span>&#92;([1,2]&#92;)</span> to a point in <span>&#92;([4,5]&#92;)</span>.

## Convex Functions

Assume that a function <span>&#92;(f(&#92;cdot)&#92;)</span> is defined on <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span>, so <span>&#92;(f : &#92;mathbb{R}^n &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span>. The function <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span> is **convex** if for all <span>&#92;(x, y&#92;)</span> and all <span>&#92;(0 &#92;leq &#92;theta &#92;leq 1&#92;)</span>,

<span>&#92;[ f(&#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y) &#92;leq &#92;theta f(x) + (1-&#92;theta)f(y). &#92;]</span>

Geometrically this says the function lies below every chord: the value of <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span> at any convex combination of two points does not exceed the corresponding convex combination of the function values. A function is **concave** if <span>&#92;(-f&#92;)</span> is convex. A function is **strictly convex** if the inequality above holds strictly for all <span>&#92;(x &#92;neq y&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(0 < &#92;theta < 1&#92;)</span>. A function <span>&#92;(f : &#92;mathbb{R}^n &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span> is said to be **log-concave** (respectively **log-convex**) if <span>&#92;(f > 0&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(&#92;log f&#92;)</span> is concave (respectively convex).

## Establishing Convexity of a Function

There are several approaches for proving that a function is convex.

**Proof by definition.** One can directly verify the defining inequality <span>&#92;(f(&#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y) &#92;leq &#92;theta f(x) + (1-&#92;theta)f(y)&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(x, y&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(&#92;theta &#92;in [0,1]&#92;)</span>.

**Restriction to a line.** A function <span>&#92;(f(&#92;cdot)&#92;)</span> is convex if and only if for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> and all directions <span>&#92;(v&#92;)</span>, the univariate function <span>&#92;(g(t) = f(x + tv)&#92;)</span> is convex (as a function of <span>&#92;(t&#92;)</span>). This often reduces a multivariate convexity proof to a one-dimensional problem.

**First-order condition.** A differentiable function with a convex domain is convex if and only if

<span>&#92;[ f(y) &#92;geq f(x) + &#92;nabla f(x)^T (y - x) \quad &#92;text{for all } x, y &#92;text{ in the domain.} &#92;]</span>

This says that the tangent hyperplane (the first-order approximation) is a global underestimator of <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span>. The proof proceeds by assuming <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span> is convex, letting <span>&#92;(t &#92;in [0,1]&#92;)</span>, and using the convexity inequality <span>&#92;(f(x + t(y-x)) &#92;leq (1-t)f(x) + tf(y)&#92;)</span>; dividing by <span>&#92;(t&#92;)</span> and taking the limit <span>&#92;(t &#92;to 0&#92;)</span> yields the stated inequality. The converse direction follows by applying the inequality twice (at <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> and at <span>&#92;(y&#92;)</span> evaluated from a point <span>&#92;(z = &#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y&#92;)</span>), multiplying by <span>&#92;(&#92;theta&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(1-&#92;theta&#92;)</span> respectively, and adding to recover the convexity inequality <span>&#92;(&#92;theta f(x) + (1-&#92;theta)f(y) &#92;geq f(z)&#92;)</span>.

**Second-order condition.** A function <span>&#92;(f(&#92;cdot)&#92;)</span> is twice differentiable if the <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span>-by-<span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> symmetric **Hessian** matrix <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x)&#92;)</span>, whose <span>&#92;((i,j)&#92;)</span> entry is <span>&#92;(&#92;partial^2 f / &#92;partial x&#95;i &#92;partial x&#95;j&#92;)</span>, exists at every <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>. For a twice differentiable function, <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span> is convex if and only if

<span>&#92;[ &#92;nabla^2 f(x) &#92;succeq 0 \quad &#92;text{(positive semidefinite) for all } x. &#92;]</span>

If <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x) &#92;succ 0&#92;)</span> (positive definite) for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>, then <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span> is strictly convex. This Hessian criterion is the most computationally convenient test when derivatives are available.

## Examples of Convex and Concave Functions

In one dimension, the following functions are convex: the affine function <span>&#92;(ax + b&#92;)</span> for any <span>&#92;(a, b &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span>; the exponential <span>&#92;(e^{ax}&#92;)</span> for any <span>&#92;(a&#92;)</span>; the power <span>&#92;(x^&#92;alpha&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(x > 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(&#92;alpha &#92;geq 1&#92;)</span>; and the absolute power <span>&#92;(|x|^p&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(p &#92;geq 1&#92;)</span>. Concave functions in one dimension include the affine <span>&#92;(ax + b&#92;)</span> (which is simultaneously convex and concave), the power <span>&#92;(x^&#92;alpha&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(x > 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(0 &#92;leq &#92;alpha &#92;leq 1&#92;)</span>, and the logarithm <span>&#92;(&#92;log(x)&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(x > 0&#92;)</span>.

In <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span>, the affine function <span>&#92;(f(x) = a^T x + b&#92;)</span> is both convex and concave. All **norms** are convex: the <span>&#92;(p&#92;)</span>-norm <span>&#92;(&#92;|x&#92;|&#95;p = (&#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^n |x&#95;i|^p)^{1/p}&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(p &#92;geq 1&#92;)</span> and the <span>&#92;(&#92;infty&#92;)</span>-norm <span>&#92;(&#92;|x&#92;|&#95;&#92;infty = &#92;max&#95;k |x&#95;k|&#92;)</span> are all convex.

## Operations That Preserve Convexity

Several operations on convex functions produce new convex functions, which allows one to recognize convexity of complicated expressions by decomposition.

**Nonnegative weighted sum.** If each <span>&#92;(f&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> is convex and <span>&#92;(&#92;alpha&#95;i &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span>, then <span>&#92;(&#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;alpha&#95;i f&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> is convex. This result extends to infinite sums and integrals.

**Composition with an affine mapping.** If <span>&#92;(f(&#92;cdot)&#92;)</span> is convex, then <span>&#92;(f(Ax + b)&#92;)</span> is convex. A useful special case is the norm of an affine function: <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;|Ax + b&#92;|&#92;)</span> is convex.

**Pointwise maximum.** If <span>&#92;(f&#95;1(x), &#92;ldots, f&#95;m(x)&#92;)</span> are convex, then their pointwise maximum <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;max\{f&#95;1(x), &#92;ldots, f&#95;m(x)\}&#92;)</span> is convex. This is proved by noting that for convex <span>&#92;(f&#95;1&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(f&#95;2&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(0 &#92;leq &#92;theta &#92;leq 1&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ f(&#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y) = &#92;max(f&#95;1(&#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y), f&#95;2(&#92;theta x + (1-&#92;theta)y)) &#92;]</span>
<span>&#92;[ &#92;leq &#92;max(&#92;theta f&#95;1(x) + (1-&#92;theta)f&#95;1(y),\; &#92;theta f&#95;2(x)+(1-&#92;theta)f&#95;2(y)) &#92;]</span>
<span>&#92;[ &#92;leq &#92;max(&#92;theta f&#95;1(x), &#92;theta f&#95;2(x)) + &#92;max((1-&#92;theta)f&#95;1(y),(1-&#92;theta)f&#95;2(y)) = &#92;theta f(x) + (1-&#92;theta)f(y). &#92;]</span>

An example is <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;max&#95;i (a&#95;i^T x - b&#95;i)&#92;)</span>. More generally, the supremum <span>&#92;(g(x) = &#92;sup&#95;{y &#92;in &#92;mathcal{D}&#95;y} f(x,y)&#92;)</span> is convex in <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> whenever <span>&#92;(f(x,y)&#92;)</span> is convex in <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> for each fixed <span>&#92;(y&#92;)</span>. An example is <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;max&#95;{y &#92;in &#92;mathcal{D}&#95;y} &#92;|x - y&#92;|&#92;)</span>.

## Nonlinear Programming and the Lagrangian

To discuss optimality conditions for the general problem (1), we introduce the **Lagrangian** function <span>&#92;(L : &#92;mathbb{R}^n &#92;times &#92;mathbb{R}^m &#92;times &#92;mathbb{R}^l &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span> defined by

<span>&#92;[ L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} f(x) + &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;lambda&#95;i g&#95;i(x) + &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^l &#92;nu&#95;j q&#95;j(x). &#92;]</span>

Here <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda&#95;i&#92;)</span> is the **Lagrange multiplier** associated with the inequality constraint <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x) &#92;leq 0&#92;)</span>, and <span>&#92;(&#92;nu&#95;j&#92;)</span> is the Lagrange multiplier associated with the equality constraint <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x) = 0&#92;)</span>. The Lagrangian is central to optimization theory and algorithms.

## The Lagrange Dual Function and Duality

The **Lagrange dual function** <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G} : &#92;mathbb{R}^m &#92;times &#92;mathbb{R}^l &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span> is defined as

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} &#92;inf&#95;x L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu). &#92;]</span>

To understand its convexity, note that <span>&#92;(-&#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = &#92;sup&#95;x \{-L(x,&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)\}&#92;)</span>. The function <span>&#92;(-L(x,&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span> is linear in <span>&#92;((&#92;lambda, &#92;nu)&#92;)</span> for fixed <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>, and a pointwise supremum of linear (hence convex) functions is convex. Therefore <span>&#92;(-&#92;mathcal{G}&#92;)</span> is convex, meaning <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}&#92;)</span> is **concave**.

The dual function provides a **lower bound** on the primal optimal value <span>&#92;(f^&#42;&#92;)</span>. More precisely, if <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> is primal feasible (i.e., <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x) &#92;leq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x) = 0&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(i, j&#92;)</span>) and <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span> (dual feasibility), then

<span>&#92;[ f(x) &#92;geq L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) &#92;geq &#92;inf&#95;x L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu). &#92;]</span>

Minimizing over <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> gives <span>&#92;(f^&#42; &#92;geq &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu)&#92;)</span> for all dual feasible <span>&#92;((&#92;lambda, &#92;nu)&#92;)</span>. The **dual problem** seeks the best (tightest) lower bound:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;max&#95;{&#92;lambda, &#92;nu} &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu) \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad &#92;lambda &#92;geq 0. &#92;]</span>

The maximum dual value <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42;&#92;)</span> yields the **best lower bound** for the primal optimal <span>&#92;(f^&#42;&#92;)</span>. The dual problem is always convex regardless of whether the primal is convex.

**Weak duality** states that <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42; &#92;leq f^&#42;&#92;)</span> always holds. **Strong duality**, <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42; = f^&#42;&#92;)</span>, holds under appropriate conditions: for convex problems it usually holds (Slater's condition is a standard sufficient condition); for nonconvex problems strong duality may fail. If the duality gap <span>&#92;(f^&#42; - &#92;mathcal{G}^&#42;&#92;)</span> is zero at a primal-dual feasible point <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>, then primal and dual optimality are simultaneously attained.

As an illustration, consider the standard linear program (LP):

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;x \; c^T x \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad Ax = b,\; x &#92;geq 0. \tag{LP} &#92;]</span>

The Lagrangian is <span>&#92;(L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = c^T x - &#92;lambda^T x + &#92;nu^T(Ax - b)&#92;)</span>. The dual function equals

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = &#92;inf&#95;x \{(c - &#92;lambda + A^T &#92;nu)^T x - b^T &#92;nu\} = &#92;begin{cases} -b^T &#92;nu & &#92;text{if } A^T &#92;nu - &#92;lambda + c = 0, \\\\ -&#92;infty & &#92;text{otherwise.} &#92;end{cases} &#92;]</span>

The dual LP is then: <span>&#92;(&#92;max&#95;&#92;nu -b^T &#92;nu&#92;)</span> subject to <span>&#92;(A^T &#92;nu - &#92;lambda + c = 0&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span>.

## KKT Optimality Conditions

The **Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions** are the first-order necessary conditions for optimality of the general constrained problem. Let <span>&#92;(f&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(g&#95;i&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(q&#95;j&#92;)</span> be continuously differentiable. At a point <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>, the KKT conditions are:

**Primal feasibility:**
<span>&#92;[ q&#95;j(x^&#42;) = 0 \quad j=1,&#92;ldots,l; \qquad g&#95;i(x^&#42;) &#92;leq 0 \quad i=1,&#92;ldots,m. &#92;]</span>

**Dual feasibility:**
<span>&#92;[ &#92;lambda^&#42; &#92;geq 0. &#92;]</span>

**Stationarity** (gradient of Lagrangian with respect to <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> equals zero):
<span>&#92;[ &#92;nabla&#95;x L(x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;) = &#92;nabla f(x^&#42;) + &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;lambda&#95;i^&#42; &#92;nabla g&#95;i(x^&#42;) + &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^l &#92;nu&#95;j^&#42; &#92;nabla q&#95;j(x^&#42;) = 0. \tag{4} &#92;]</span>

**Complementary slackness:**
<span>&#92;[ &#92;lambda&#95;i^&#42; g&#95;i(x^&#42;) = 0 \quad i=1,&#92;ldots,m. &#92;]</span>

The role of complementary slackness is to encode the fact that a Lagrange multiplier for an inequality constraint is nonzero only when that constraint is active (binding) at the solution.

For a **convex problem** with differentiable objective and constraints satisfying a constraint qualification (e.g., Slater's condition), <span>&#92;(x^&#42;&#92;)</span> is a minimizer if and only if there exist multipliers <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;&#92;)</span> such that KKT is satisfied at <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>. KKT is therefore both necessary and sufficient for convex problems. For a **nonconvex problem**, KKT is only a necessary condition at a local minimizer satisfying a constraint qualification; it is not sufficient.

---

# Lecture 21b: CVaR Portfolio Optimization

## Motivation: Computing CVaR Optimal Portfolios

This lecture addresses the practically important problem of portfolio optimization using **Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR)** as the risk measure. The setting is a hedge fund with <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> financial assets — including options — whose random loss vector over a time horizon <span>&#92;([0,t]&#92;)</span> (e.g., one month) is <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbf{L} &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span>. The loss distribution is assumed to be specified. A concrete example is a portfolio of options on two underlying stocks <span>&#92;(S^A&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(S^B&#92;)</span>; the instrument loss is <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbf{L} = V&#95;0 - V&#95;t&#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;(V&#95;0&#92;)</span> is today's portfolio value and <span>&#92;(V&#95;t&#92;)</span> is the portfolio value at the end of the horizon. Note the sign convention throughout: VaR and CVaR are defined with respect to loss, which is the negative of profit and loss (P&L).

Denote by <span>&#92;(x &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span> the vector of percentage holdings in the <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> instruments. The **portfolio loss** is then <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbf{L}^T x&#92;)</span>. The portfolio optimization objectives are to choose <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> so as to minimize some measure of risk while simultaneously maximizing expected profit.

## Comparison: Minimizing VaR versus CVaR

A key motivation for using CVaR rather than VaR as the risk measure is illustrated by the behavior of these two measures over a two-asset portfolio. Plotting <span>&#92;(&#92;text{VaR}&#95;{0.95}&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(&#92;text{CVaR}&#95;{0.95}&#92;)</span> as functions of the position in Asset 1 (with Asset 2 taking the complementary position) reveals that VaR, shown as the solid curve, is a nonsmooth function of the portfolio allocation with multiple local minimizers. CVaR, shown as the dashed curve, is a smooth convex function that lies above VaR (as expected, since CVaR is the mean of losses beyond VaR). The fundamental consequence is that **minimizing VaR is a nonconvex problem**: it can have many local minimizers, and computing a global minimizer is very difficult. By contrast, **minimizing CVaR is a convex problem** and a minimizer can be computed efficiently. This is the central practical and theoretical advantage of CVaR-based portfolio optimization.

## CVaR Portfolio Optimization: Mathematical Formulation

Assume that the asset loss has a continuous distribution, so the portfolio loss <span>&#92;(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L}) = x^T &#92;mathbf{L}&#92;)</span> has a continuous CDF. Recall from earlier lectures that CVaR at level <span>&#92;(&#92;beta&#92;)</span> is the expected loss conditional on the loss exceeding the <span>&#92;(&#92;beta&#92;)</span>-quantile (VaR), corresponding to the shaded right tail of the loss distribution.

Denote <span>&#92;(z^+ = &#92;max(z, 0)&#92;)</span>. The key insight due to **Rockafellar and Uryasev (2002)** is that for portfolio loss <span>&#92;(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L}) = x^T &#92;mathbf{L}&#92;)</span>, the auxiliary function

<span>&#92;[ F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha) = &#92;alpha + (1-&#92;beta)^{-1} &#92;mathbf{E}&#92;left[(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L}) - &#92;alpha)^+&#92;right] &#92;]</span>

satisfies

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;&#92;beta(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L})) = &#92;min&#95;{&#92;alpha &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}} F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha). &#92;]</span>

This means the CVaR of the portfolio loss equals the minimum over <span>&#92;(&#92;alpha&#92;)</span> of <span>&#92;(F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha)&#92;)</span>. As a direct consequence, minimizing CVaR over the portfolio allocation <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> is equivalent to jointly minimizing <span>&#92;(F&#95;&#92;beta&#92;)</span> over both <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(&#92;alpha&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{x &#92;in &#92;mathcal{X}} &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;&#92;beta(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L})) &#92;\iff &#92;min&#95;{x &#92;in &#92;mathcal{X},\, &#92;alpha &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}} F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha). &#92;]</span>

This reformulation is enormously powerful because <span>&#92;(F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha)&#92;)</span> is jointly convex in <span>&#92;((x, &#92;alpha)&#92;)</span> when <span>&#92;(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L})&#92;)</span> is linear in <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>, which converts the CVaR minimization into a tractable convex program.

## Computing a Minimum CVaR Portfolio

The **minimum CVaR risk portfolio** solves

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{(x, &#92;alpha) &#92;in &#92;mathcal{X} &#92;times &#92;mathbb{R}} F&#95;&#92;beta(x, &#92;alpha). \tag{1} &#92;]</span>

In practice, the loss distribution is not known analytically — there is generally no explicit formula for the probability density of the portfolio loss <span>&#92;(f(x, &#92;mathbf{L})&#92;)</span>. Instead, one works with a set of <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span> equally probable i.i.d. loss scenarios <span>&#92;(\{(&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i\}&#95;{i=1}^m&#92;)</span>, where <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span> is typically large (e.g., <span>&#92;(m = 10^5&#92;)</span>), generated by Monte Carlo simulation. The expectation is approximated by the sample mean <span>&#92;(&#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;mathbf{L}) &#92;approx &#92;bar{L} = &#92;frac{1}{m} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m (&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i&#92;)</span>.

For any given portfolio <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>, the CVaR is approximated by the sample CVaR:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;text{CVaR}^S&#95;&#92;beta(&#92;mathbf{L}^T x) &#92;approx &#92;min&#95;&#92;alpha \left\{ &#92;alpha + &#92;frac{1}{(1-&#92;beta)m} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;left((&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x - &#92;alpha&#92;right)^+ &#92;right\}. &#92;]</span>

The feasible set <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{X}&#92;)</span> imposes realistic portfolio constraints: the average loss equals a target <span>&#92;(\bar{&#92;ell}&#92;)</span> (given constant), the portfolio weights sum to one (<span>&#92;(e^T x = 1&#92;)</span>), and all positions are nonnegative (<span>&#92;(x &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span>). The minimum CVaR portfolio problem (1) can therefore be computed by solving:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{(x, &#92;alpha)} \; &#92;alpha + &#92;frac{1}{m(1-&#92;beta)} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;left((&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x - &#92;alpha&#92;right)^+ \quad &#92;text{s.t.} \quad &#92;bar{L}^T x = &#92;bar{&#92;ell},\; e^T x = 1,\; x &#92;geq 0. \tag{2} &#92;]</span>

## Alternative Formulation: CVaR Risk-Constrained Optimal Portfolio

In practice, a **risk budget** is often given: the portfolio manager wants the maximum expected return subject to an upper bound on CVaR risk. This leads to the **CVaR risk-constrained portfolio optimization problem**:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;x \; x^T &#92;mathbf{E}(&#92;mathbf{L}) \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad e^T x = 1,\; x &#92;geq 0,\; &#92;text{CVaR}&#95;&#92;beta(&#92;mathbf{L}^T x) &#92;leq &#92;Lambda, \tag{3} &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(&#92;Lambda&#92;)</span> is a given risk budget constant. Here minimizing expected loss is equivalent to maximizing expected profit (return).

## Solving CVaR Constrained Problems as Linear Programs

Both problem (2) and problem (3) can be solved as **linear programs (LPs)**. This is possible because the max function in the CVaR definition can be linearized using auxiliary variables. Introduce slack variables <span>&#92;(z&#95;i = ((&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x - &#92;alpha)^+&#92;)</span>. Then <span>&#92;(z&#95;i &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(z&#95;i &#92;geq (&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x - &#92;alpha&#92;)</span>. One can verify that the CVaR constraint

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;&#92;alpha &#92;left\{&#92;alpha + &#92;frac{1}{(1-&#92;beta)m} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m ((&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x - &#92;alpha)^+&#92;right\} &#92;leq &#92;Lambda &#92;]</span>

holds if and only if there exists <span>&#92;(z&#92;)</span> such that <span>&#92;(&#92;alpha, x, z&#92;)</span> satisfy

<span>&#92;[ &#92;alpha + &#92;frac{1}{(1-&#92;beta)m} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m z&#95;i &#92;leq &#92;Lambda, \quad z&#95;i - (&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x + &#92;alpha &#92;geq 0,\; z&#95;i &#92;geq 0,\; i=1,&#92;ldots,m. &#92;]</span>

Therefore the CVaR-constrained problem (3) is equivalent to the LP:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{x, z, &#92;alpha} \; &#92;bar{L}^T x \quad &#92;text{s.t.} \quad e^T x = 1,\; x &#92;geq 0,\; &#92;alpha + &#92;frac{1}{(1-&#92;beta)m} &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m z&#95;i &#92;leq &#92;Lambda,\; z&#95;i - (&#92;mathbf{L})&#95;i^T x + &#92;alpha &#92;geq 0,\; z&#95;i &#92;geq 0. \tag{5} &#92;]</span>

This LP has <span>&#92;(O(m+n)&#92;)</span> variables and <span>&#92;(O(m+n)&#92;)</span> constraints. LP software such as MOSEK, CVX, and CPLEX solves such problems efficiently. In Matlab, the built-in function

<span>&#92;[ z = &#92;texttt{linprog}(f, A, b, A&#95;{eq}, b&#95;{eq}, l, u) &#92;]</span>

solves the LP <span>&#92;(&#92;min&#95;z f^T z&#92;)</span> subject to <span>&#92;(Az &#92;leq b&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(A&#95;{eq} z = b&#95;{eq}&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(l &#92;leq z &#92;leq u&#92;)</span>, and can be used directly to solve (5) after appropriate reformulation.

---

# Lecture 22: Volatility Smile, Model Calibration, and Nonlinear Least Squares

## Outline

Lecture 22 covers four main topics: the volatility smile and model calibration, the formulation and solution of nonlinear least squares problems, the challenges specific to option model calibration, and the Newton and *Levenberg-Marquardt* methods together with Matlab's **lsqnonlin** and **fmincon**.

## The Volatility Smile and Model Calibration

### Implied Volatility Surface

Market option prices across different strikes <span>&#92;(K&#92;)</span> and maturities <span>&#92;(T&#92;)</span> can each be inverted through the Black-Scholes formula to produce an **implied volatility**. If the Black-Scholes model were exactly correct, implied volatility would be flat across all strikes and maturities. In reality, the implied volatility surface exhibits pronounced structure. For example, S&P 500 option market data from October 1995 shows implied volatility ranging from roughly 10% to 18%, decreasing markedly as the strike increases (the so-called **volatility skew** or smile). The implied volatility also varies with maturity. This non-flat surface indicates that the Black-Scholes model is **insufficient** for pricing options across the full smile, because the option price today depends on the volatility of future returns and thus contains statistical information about future return distributions.

### Calibrating Option Models

The implied volatility surface motivates the use of richer models. Given a model such as a **local volatility function (LVF)** model, a **stochastic volatility (SV)** model, or a **jump-diffusion** model, and using finite difference (FD) and Monte Carlo (MC) methods, one can price options, hedge options, and quantitatively evaluate risk measures such as VaR and CVaR. The central question is then: how does one obtain an accurate model for option pricing and hedging? The answer is to choose a model that is **consistent with the option market** — that is, to calibrate the model so that the corresponding model-implied option prices match the observed market prices.

### Marking a Model to Market

The process of calibrating a model is equivalent to **marking the model to market**. Assume that a set of <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span> today's liquid option prices <span>&#92;\{V_0^{\text{mkt}}(K_l, T_l),\; l = 1, 2, &#92;ldots, m\}&#92;)</span> on an underlying are observed from the option market. Let <span>&#92;(&#92;Omega&#92;)</span> denote the parameter set of possible models, and let <span>&#92;(V&#95;0(K&#95;l, T&#95;l; &#92;mathcal{H})&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{H} &#92;in &#92;Omega&#92;)</span> denote the initial option value computed from the model. A model is calibrated by choosing parameters <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{H}&#92;)</span> such that

<span>&#92;[ V&#95;0(K&#95;l, T&#95;l; &#92;mathcal{H}) - V&#95;0^{&#92;text{mkt}}(K&#95;l, T&#95;l) = 0, \quad l = 1, 2, &#92;ldots, m, \tag{1} &#92;]</span>

or, more typically (since an exact fit may not be achievable), by minimizing the sum of squared pricing errors:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{&#92;mathcal{H} &#92;in &#92;Omega} &#92;sum&#95;{l=1}^m &#92;left(V&#95;0(K&#95;l, T&#95;l; &#92;mathcal{H}) - V&#95;0^{&#92;text{mkt}}(K&#95;l, T&#95;l)&#92;right)^2. \tag{2} &#92;]</span>

The calibration problem is typically formulated as (2). Additional constraints (e.g., bound constraints on the parameters) can be imposed on <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{H}&#92;)</span>. An important question is whether a solution to (2) is always a solution to (1): this is only true if the minimum of (2) is zero, i.e., if a perfect fit is achievable.

Calibration ensures that the resulting option pricing model is **consistent with the current market option price information**. If the calibrated model is used to price illiquid options (options not in the calibration set), they are priced consistently with the current market information. If the calibrated model is used to hedge options, the hedging positions are determined by a model that is already consistent with current market information. This is why calibrating a model and marking it to market are equivalent concepts.

### Model Calibration Examples

For **Merton's jump-diffusion model**, the parameter set is <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{H} = \{&#92;sigma, &#92;lambda&#95;J, &#92;mu&#95;J, &#92;sigma&#95;J\}&#92;)</span>, a small finite-dimensional parameter vector. Similarly, stochastic volatility models (e.g., Heston's model) are described by a small number of parameters. For a **local volatility function (LVF)** model, different parametric forms have been proposed in the literature. A simple example is the quadratic local volatility function <span>&#92;(&#92;sigma(S, t; x) = &#92;max(x&#95;1 + x&#95;2(S - S&#95;0) + x&#95;3(S-S&#95;0)^2, 0)&#92;)</span> with <span>&#92;(x &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^3&#92;)</span>, or a more flexible six-parameter form

<span>&#92;[ &#92;sigma(S, t; x) = &#92;max(x&#95;1 + x&#95;2 S + x&#95;3 t + x&#95;4 S^2 + x&#95;5 t^2 + x&#95;6 St, 0). \tag{3} &#92;]</span>

A machine learning approach is also conceivable. Option model calibration in general means determining the model parameters so that the corresponding model option prices are consistent with observed market option prices.

A critical observation is that the Black-Scholes option formula depends nonlinearly on the volatility parameter, and more generally <span>&#92;(V&#95;0(K&#95;l, T&#95;l; &#92;mathcal{H})&#92;)</span> depends **nonlinearly** on the model parameters <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{H}&#92;)</span>. This makes the calibration a **nonlinear least squares** problem, and solving it is often not straightforward.

## Nonlinear Least Squares: Problem Formulation

The model which best fits the market option prices is a global solution to the following nonlinear least squares problem. Define the **residual vector** <span>&#92;(F(x) : &#92;mathbb{R}^n &#92;to &#92;mathbb{R}^m&#92;)</span> by

<span>&#92;[ F(x) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} &#92;begin{pmatrix} V&#95;0(K&#95;1, T&#95;1; &#92;sigma(S,t;x)) - V&#95;0^{&#92;text{mkt}}(K&#95;1, T&#95;1) \\\\ &#92;vdots \\\\ V&#95;0(K&#95;m, T&#95;m; &#92;sigma(S,t;x)) - V&#95;0^{&#92;text{mkt}}(K&#95;m, T&#95;m) &#92;end{pmatrix}. &#92;]</span>

The initial option value model <span>&#92;(V&#95;0(K&#95;j, T&#95;j; &#92;sigma(S,t;x))&#92;)</span> is a complicated nonlinear function of the parameter vector <span>&#92;(x &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^n&#92;)</span>. The calibration problem is the **nonlinear least squares** problem:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;{x &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^n} f(x) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;|F(x)&#92;|&#95;2^2 = &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^m &#92;left(V&#95;0(K&#95;j, T&#95;j; &#92;sigma(S,t;x)) - V&#95;0^{&#92;text{mkt}}(K&#95;j, T&#95;j)&#92;right)^2. &#92;]</span>

A nonlinear least squares problem is a special case of unconstrained minimization <span>&#92;(&#92;min&#95;{x &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}^n} f(x)&#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span> is continuously differentiable. Computing a solution requires evaluating the function and gradient, and possibly the Hessian matrix.

### Local and Global Minimizers

A **local minimizer** <span>&#92;(x&#95;&#42;&#92;)</span> satisfies <span>&#92;(f(x&#95;&#42;) &#92;leq f(x)&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> with <span>&#92;(&#92;|x - x&#95;&#42;&#92;| &#92;leq &#92;delta&#92;)</span> for some <span>&#92;(&#92;delta > 0&#92;)</span>. A **global minimizer** satisfies <span>&#92;(f(x&#95;&#42;) &#92;leq f(x)&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span>. The **warm start** strategy — beginning from a good initial point, ideally close to the global solution — is important in practice; the topic of convex versus nonconvex optimization helps explain why.

## Gradient and Hessian of the Least Squares Objective

Computing a solution to the nonlinear optimization problem requires calculating the function, gradient (and possibly the Hessian). For the least squares function <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;frac{1}{2}&#92;|F&#92;|&#95;2^2 = &#92;frac{1}{2} F(x)^T F(x)&#92;)</span>, the gradient is

<span>&#92;[ &#92;nabla f = &#92;begin{pmatrix} &#92;partial f/&#92;partial x&#95;1 \\\\ &#92;partial f/&#92;partial x&#95;2 \\\\ &#92;vdots \\\\ &#92;partial f/&#92;partial x&#95;n &#92;end{pmatrix} = J(x)^T F(x), &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(J(x)&#92;)</span> is the <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span>-by-<span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> **Jacobian matrix** of <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ J(x) = &#92;begin{pmatrix} &#92;partial F&#95;1/&#92;partial x&#95;1 & &#92;partial F&#95;1/&#92;partial x&#95;2 & &#92;cdots & &#92;partial F&#95;1/&#92;partial x&#95;n \\\\ &#92;partial F&#95;2/&#92;partial x&#95;1 & &#92;partial F&#95;2/&#92;partial x&#95;2 & &#92;cdots & &#92;partial F&#95;2/&#92;partial x&#95;n \\\\ &#92;vdots & &#92;vdots & &#92;ddots & &#92;vdots \\\\ &#92;partial F&#95;m/&#92;partial x&#95;1 & &#92;partial F&#95;m/&#92;partial x&#95;2 & &#92;cdots & &#92;partial F&#95;m/&#92;partial x&#95;n &#92;end{pmatrix}. &#92;]</span>

The Hessian of any function <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span> is the <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span>-by-<span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> symmetric matrix with <span>&#92;((i,j)&#92;)</span> entry <span>&#92;(&#92;partial^2 f / &#92;partial x&#95;i &#92;partial x&#95;j&#92;)</span>. For the least squares objective, the Hessian has a special structure:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;nabla^2 f(x) = J(x)^T J(x) + &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m F&#95;i(x) &#92;nabla^2 F&#95;i(x) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} J(x)^T J(x) + G(x), &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(G(x) = &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m F&#95;i(x) &#92;nabla^2 F&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> contains the second-order information from the individual residuals. Proof is left as an exercise.

## The Newton Method

A nonlinear optimization problem is solved by an iterative process that produces a sequence of approximations <span>&#92;(\{x&#95;k, k = 0, 1, &#92;ldots\}&#92;)</span> expected to converge to a solution. Assuming <span>&#92;(x&#95;c&#92;)</span> is the current approximation, the **Newton step** for solving <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla f = 0&#92;)</span> is

<span>&#92;[ x&#95;+ = x&#95;c - (&#92;nabla^2 f(x&#95;c))^{-1} &#92;nabla f(x&#95;c). &#92;]</span>

Substituting the expressions for gradient and Hessian of the least squares objective gives the full Newton step for the calibration problem:

<span>&#92;[ x&#95;+ = x&#95;c - (J(x&#95;c)^T J(x&#95;c) + G(x&#95;c))^{-1} J(x&#95;c)^T F(x&#95;c). &#92;]</span>

Applying Newton's method requires computing derivatives of <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span>, which requires computing derivatives of the option value functions with respect to the model parameters.

## Finite Difference Approximation of Derivatives

When analytic derivatives are unavailable, one resorts to **finite difference** approximation. For a single function <span>&#92;(g(z)&#92;)</span> of one variable <span>&#92;(z &#92;in &#92;mathbb{R}&#92;)</span>, the forward finite difference formula is

<span>&#92;[ g'(z) = &#92;frac{g(z + &#92;Delta z) - g(z)}{&#92;Delta z} + O(&#92;Delta z). &#92;]</span>

If <span>&#92;(g(z)&#92;)</span> is already evaluated at <span>&#92;(z = z&#95;c&#92;)</span>, the derivative can be approximated at the cost of one additional function evaluation <span>&#92;(g(z + &#92;Delta z)&#92;)</span>. The key question is how to choose <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta z&#92;)</span>. Choosing machine epsilon (**eps** in Matlab) directly as <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta z&#92;)</span> is not optimal because the truncation error <span>&#92;(O(&#92;Delta z)&#92;)</span> would be negligible but floating-point cancellation error in the numerator would be large. The standard recommendation, when no additional information is available, is to choose <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta z = &#92;sqrt{&#92;textbf{eps}}&#92;)</span>, which balances truncation error and rounding error. A **complex finite difference** approximation is another alternative that can achieve higher accuracy.

### Approximate Jacobian Using Finite Differences

Assume <span>&#92;(F(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> has been computed. Let <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta &#92;approx &#92;sqrt{&#92;epsilon}&#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;(&#92;epsilon&#92;)</span> is machine precision. The <span>&#92;(m&#92;)</span>-by-<span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> Jacobian matrix can be approximated column by column: for each <span>&#92;(i = 1, &#92;ldots, n&#92;)</span>, one evaluates <span>&#92;(F(x&#95;c + &#92;Delta &#92;cdot e&#95;i)&#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;(e&#95;i&#92;)</span> is the unit vector (zero everywhere except the <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span>th component), and then approximates

<span>&#92;[ &#92;frac{&#92;partial F&#95;j}{&#92;partial x&#95;i} &#92;approx &#92;frac{F&#95;j(x&#95;c + &#92;Delta &#92;cdot e&#95;i) - F&#95;j(x&#95;c)}{&#92;Delta} &#92;]</span>

for each <span>&#92;(j = 1, &#92;ldots, m&#92;)</span>. This requires <span>&#92;(n&#92;)</span> additional evaluations of <span>&#92;(F&#92;)</span> beyond the evaluation at <span>&#92;(x&#95;c&#92;)</span>.

---

# Lecture 23: Newton and Levenberg-Marquardt Methods

## Overview

Lecture 23 continues the analysis of numerical methods for nonlinear least squares and model calibration. The main topics are the Newton and *Gauss-Newton* methods in detail, the *Levenberg-Marquardt* method as a trust region approach, the option calibration problem and its challenges, and practical implementation using Matlab's **lsqnonlin** and **fmincon** functions. The lecture also extends to the nonlinear programming context: the Lagrangian, dual function, and KKT conditions as developed in Lecture 21a.

## Newton and Gauss-Newton Methods: Summary

Recall the setting: we want to minimize <span>&#92;(f(x) = &#92;frac{1}{2} F(x)^T F(x)&#92;)</span>. The **Newton method** computes the step using the full Hessian <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> and gradient <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla f(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ x&#95;+ = x&#95;c - (&#92;nabla^2 f(x&#95;c))^{-1} &#92;nabla f(x&#95;c). &#92;]</span>

For the least squares objective, <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x) = J(x)^T J(x) + G(x)&#92;)</span> where <span>&#92;(G(x) = &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m F&#95;i(x) &#92;nabla^2 F&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span>.

### The Gauss-Newton Step

Computing the individual Hessian matrices <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 F&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(i = 1, &#92;ldots, m&#92;)</span> is too expensive in practice. The **Gauss-Newton** method approximates <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> by <span>&#92;(J(x&#95;c)^T J(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span>, dropping the second-order term <span>&#92;(G(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> entirely. The motivation is to consider an **affine model** for <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span> around the current point <span>&#92;(x&#95;c&#92;)</span>:

<span>&#92;[ M&#95;c(x) = F(x&#95;c) + J(x&#95;c)(x - x&#95;c). &#92;]</span>

The system <span>&#92;(M&#95;c(x) = 0&#92;)</span> is an over-determined (when <span>&#92;(m &#92;geq n&#92;)</span>) linear least squares problem. It is natural to set <span>&#92;(x&#95;+&#92;)</span> as the least squares solution, i.e., to minimize

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;x &#92;frac{1}{2} &#92;|F(x&#95;c) + J(x&#95;c)(x - x&#95;c)&#92;|&#95;2^2, &#92;]</span>

whose solution is

<span>&#92;[ x&#95;+ = x&#95;c - (J(x&#95;c)^T J(x&#95;c))^{-1} J(x&#95;c)^T F(x&#95;c). &#92;]</span>

This is the *Gauss-Newton* step. In general, when <span>&#92;(m &#92;geq n&#92;)</span> the least squares residual at the solution of the linearized system is not zero.

The key difference between Newton and Gauss-Newton is that in the Gauss-Newton step, the second-order derivatives <span>&#92;(G(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> are not computed, and <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> is approximated by <span>&#92;(J(x&#95;c)^T J(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span>. This approximation is accurate when the residuals <span>&#92;(F&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span> are small at the solution (the problem is "nearly zero-residual"), because then the <span>&#92;(G&#92;)</span> term is small. Recall that <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 f(x) = J(x)^T J(x) + G(x)&#92;)</span>, so ignoring <span>&#92;(G&#92;)</span> is exact when the problem has small residuals.

### Convergence of Gauss-Newton

If at a minimizer <span>&#92;(x&#95;&#42;&#92;)</span> one has <span>&#92;(G(x&#95;&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span>, the *Gauss-Newton* method can be shown to converge **locally quadratically**. The condition <span>&#92;(G(x&#95;&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span> is satisfied in two important cases: either <span>&#92;(F(x&#95;&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span> (zero-residual problem, meaning the model perfectly fits the data), or <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla^2 F&#95;i(x&#95;&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span> for all <span>&#92;(i&#92;)</span> (each residual is linear near the solution).

**Advantages of the Gauss-Newton method:** Only function <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span> and Jacobian <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla F&#92;)</span> evaluations are required — no second derivatives. Each iteration solves a **linear least squares** problem. The method converges quickly (locally linearly, and quadratically in the zero-residual case) on problems that are not too nonlinear and/or have reasonably small residuals.

**Difficulties:** Problems that are sufficiently nonlinear or have reasonably large residuals exhibit slow linear convergence. Problems that are very nonlinear or have large residuals may not be locally convergent at all. The method is not well defined if <span>&#92;(J(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span> does not have full column rank (since <span>&#92;(J^T J&#92;)</span> would be singular). The method is not necessarily **globally** convergent — that is, it may fail to converge from some starting points.

## The Levenberg-Marquardt Method

Newton methods, including Gauss-Newton, converge only locally. To achieve more robust convergence from an arbitrary starting point, one turns to **trust region** methods. The idea is to restrict the step to a ball of radius <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta&#95;c &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span> around the current iterate. The **trust region subproblem** for the nonlinear least squares case is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;x &#92;\|F(x&#95;c) + J(x&#95;c)(x - x&#95;c)&#92;\|&#95;2^2 \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad &#92;\|x - x&#95;c&#92;\|&#95;2 &#92;leq &#92;Delta&#95;c. &#92;]</span>

It can be shown that the solution to this trust region subproblem has the form

<span>&#92;[ x&#95;+ = x&#95;c - (J(x&#95;c)^T J(x&#95;c) + &#92;mu&#95;c I)^{-1} J(x&#95;c)^T F(x&#95;c) &#92;]</span>

for some <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c > 0&#92;)</span>. This is the **Levenberg-Marquardt** step. The scalar <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c&#92;)</span> (often written <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda&#92;)</span> in the literature) is the **regularization parameter** or damping factor. When <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c = 0&#92;)</span>, the step reduces to the Gauss-Newton step. As <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c &#92;to &#92;infty&#92;)</span>, the step <span>&#92;(x&#95;+ - x&#95;c &#92;to 0&#92;)</span> in the direction of the steepest descent direction <span>&#92;(-J(x&#95;c)^T F(x&#95;c) = -&#92;nabla f(x&#95;c)&#92;)</span>, scaled by <span>&#92;(1/&#92;mu&#95;c&#92;)</span>. Thus the Levenberg-Marquardt method **interpolates between steepest descent** (large <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c&#92;)</span>) **and Gauss-Newton** (small <span>&#92;(&#92;mu&#95;c&#92;)</span>). The trust region size <span>&#92;(&#92;Delta&#95;k&#92;)</span> is updated adaptively: larger steps are allowed when <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span> behaves more linearly (the affine model is a good approximation), and smaller steps are taken when <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span> is more nonlinear.

## The Option Calibration Problem: Practical Challenges

The option calibration problem has several practical challenges that must be understood when applying these methods.

**Multiple local minimizers.** The calibration problem can have multiple local minimizers because the objective function is generally nonconvex. Choosing a good starting point (e.g., from historical calibrations or a coarse grid search over parameter space) can help locate a global minimizer.

**Limitations of Levenberg-Marquardt.** The *Levenberg-Marquardt* method uses only the Jacobian matrix; only partial Hessian information (the <span>&#92;(J^T J&#92;)</span> part) is used, and the second-order term <span>&#92;(G(x)&#92;)</span> is ignored. As a consequence, iterations can progress slowly in a very nonlinear region with large residuals. Again, choosing a good starting point can help substantially.

**Alternative methods.** Methods that use the exact or approximate full Hessian information are sometimes employed, though they are expensive to compute. **Automatic differentiation** can be used to compute exact derivatives at reasonable computational cost, enabling the use of Newton's method with the full Hessian.

## Matlab: lsqnonlin

The Matlab function **lsqnonlin** solves a nonlinear least squares problem. The basic calling syntax is:

```
x = lsqnonlin(fun, x0, lb, ub, options)
[x, resnorm, residual, exitflag] = lsqnonlin(...)
[x, resnorm, residual, exitflag, output] = lsqnonlin(...)
```

Here `fun` is a function handle that computes the residual vector <span>&#92;(F(x)&#92;)</span> (and optionally its Jacobian), `x0` is the initial guess, `lb` and `ub` are lower and upper bound vectors on the parameters, and `options` is a structure set by `optimset`. To use the *Levenberg-Marquardt* algorithm with user-supplied Jacobian:

```
options = optimset('Jacobian', 'on', 'Algorithm', 'levenberg-marquardt', ...
                   'Display', 'iter', 'MaxIter', 50)
```

The function `fun` should be implemented as:

```
function [F, J] = myfun(x)
    F = ...;          % Objective function values at x
    if nargout > 1    % Two output arguments requested
        J = ...;      % Jacobian of the function evaluated at x
    end
end
```

For more details, see `doc lsqnonlin` in Matlab.

## Matlab: fmincon

For minimizing a general nonlinear function subject to constraints, Matlab provides **fmincon**:

```
x = fmincon(fun, x0, A, b, Aeq, beq, lb, ub)
options = optimset('Algorithm', 'trust-region-reflective');
```

This function implements a trust-region reflective algorithm for nonlinear minimization subject to bounds and linear constraints, based on the approach of Coleman and Li ("An Interior, Trust Region Approach for Nonlinear Minimization Subject to Bounds," *SIAM Journal on Optimization*, Vol. 6, pp. 418-445, 1996).

## The General Nonlinear Programming Framework (Continued from Lec21a)

Lecture 23 also revisits the general nonlinear programming problem from Lecture 21a and deepens the discussion of optimality conditions in the context of the calibration problem. The general problem is:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;min&#95;x f(x) \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad g&#95;i(x) &#92;leq 0,\; i = 1,&#92;ldots,m; \quad q&#95;j(x) = 0,\; j = 1,&#92;ldots,l, \tag{3} &#92;]</span>

where <span>&#92;(f(x)&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x)&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x)&#92;)</span> are twice continuously differentiable. The Lagrangian is

<span>&#92;[ L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) &#92;stackrel{&#92;text{def}}{=} f(x) + &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;lambda&#95;i g&#95;i(x) + &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^l &#92;nu&#95;j q&#95;j(x). &#92;]</span>

The **Lagrange dual function** is <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = &#92;inf&#95;x L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu)&#92;)</span>. As shown in Lecture 21a, the function <span>&#92;(-L(x,&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span> is linear in <span>&#92;((&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span>, pointwise maximization over <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> preserves convexity, and so the Lagrange dual function <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span> is **concave**. If <span>&#92;(x&#92;)</span> is primal feasible and <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span> (dual feasible), then

<span>&#92;[ f(x) &#92;geq L(x,&#92;lambda,&#92;nu) &#92;geq &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda,&#92;nu), &#92;]</span>

establishing the lower bound property: <span>&#92;(f^&#42; &#92;geq &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span> for all dual feasible <span>&#92;((&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span>. The **dual problem** <span>&#92;(&#92;max&#95;{&#92;lambda &#92;geq 0, &#92;nu} &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda,&#92;nu)&#92;)</span> yields the best lower bound <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42;&#92;)</span> for the primal optimal <span>&#92;(f^&#42;&#92;)</span>. The dual problem is convex.

### Weak and Strong Duality

**Weak duality** states that <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42; &#92;leq f^&#42;&#92;)</span> always. **Strong duality**, <span>&#92;(&#92;mathcal{G}^&#42; = f^&#42;&#92;)</span>, holds if the duality gap is zero at a primal-dual feasible point <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>. For a convex problem, strong duality usually holds under mild constraint qualifications. For a nonconvex problem, strong duality may not hold.

### Primal and Dual for Standard LP

For the standard LP <span>&#92;(&#92;min&#95;x c^T x&#92;)</span> subject to <span>&#92;(Ax = b&#92;)</span>, <span>&#92;(x &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span>, the Lagrangian is <span>&#92;(L(x, &#92;lambda, &#92;nu) = c^T x - &#92;lambda^T x + &#92;nu^T(Ax - b)&#92;)</span>. The dual function

<span>&#92;[ &#92;mathcal{G}(&#92;lambda,&#92;nu) = &#92;inf&#95;x (c - &#92;lambda + A^T&#92;nu)^T x - b^T&#92;nu = &#92;begin{cases} -b^T&#92;nu & &#92;text{if } A^T&#92;nu - &#92;lambda + c = 0 \\\\ -&#92;infty & &#92;text{otherwise} &#92;end{cases} &#92;]</span>

leads to the dual LP:

<span>&#92;[ &#92;max&#95;&#92;nu -b^T&#92;nu \quad &#92;text{subject to} \quad A^T&#92;nu - &#92;lambda + c = 0,\; &#92;lambda &#92;geq 0. \tag{DLP} &#92;]</span>

Strong duality holds for LP under mild feasibility conditions (both primal and dual feasible).

### KKT Conditions (Revisited)

At a point <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>, the KKT conditions comprise:

**Primal feasibility:** <span>&#92;(q&#95;j(x^&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(j = 1,&#92;ldots,l&#92;)</span> and <span>&#92;(g&#95;i(x^&#42;) &#92;leq 0&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(i = 1,&#92;ldots,m&#92;)</span>.

**Dual feasibility:** <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda^&#42; &#92;geq 0&#92;)</span>.

**Stationarity:** <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla&#95;x L(x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;) = &#92;nabla f(x^&#42;) + &#92;sum&#95;{i=1}^m &#92;lambda&#95;i^&#42; &#92;nabla g&#95;i(x^&#42;) + &#92;sum&#95;{j=1}^l &#92;nu&#95;j^&#42; &#92;nabla q&#95;j(x^&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span>.

**Complementary slackness:** <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda&#95;i^&#42; g&#95;i(x^&#42;) = 0&#92;)</span> for <span>&#92;(i = 1,&#92;ldots,m&#92;)</span>.

For a **convex problem** with differentiable objective and constraints satisfying some technical condition (constraint qualification), <span>&#92;(x^&#42;&#92;)</span> is a minimizer **if and only if** there exist <span>&#92;(&#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;&#92;)</span> such that KKT is satisfied at <span>&#92;((x^&#42;, &#92;lambda^&#42;, &#92;nu^&#42;)&#92;)</span>. For a **nonconvex problem** with differentiable objective and constraints, KKT is a necessary condition but is not sufficient: a point satisfying KKT may be a local minimizer, a local maximizer, or a saddle point.

These theoretical foundations connect directly to the practical algorithms discussed in this lecture batch. The Levenberg-Marquardt method implicitly handles the stationarity condition <span>&#92;(&#92;nabla f(x) = 0&#92;)</span> through iterative minimization of the regularized linear least squares subproblem, while the trust region framework ensures sufficient decrease and convergence. For the CVaR portfolio problems in Lecture 21b, the LP reformulation converts the KKT conditions of the original nonconvex CVaR minimization into those of a linear program, for which strong duality holds and global solutions can be efficiently computed.
